All configurations --- AMIP, coupled round-09, \emph{and the CMIP7 production piControl} ---
run \texttt{LRDALB = .false.} with \texttt{NALBEDOSCHEME = 3}, which \textbf{discards the MODIS
albedo present in the input file} and constructs albedo each step
(\texttt{surfbc\_ctl\_mod.F90}:512) as
$\alpha=\mathtt{RVVEGALB}(t_{vl})c_{vl}+\mathtt{RVVEGALB}(t_{vh})c_{vh}+\alpha_{soil}(1-c_{vl}-c_{vh})$.

\dead{Hypothesis 1: the soil-albedo field dominates tundra.} The bare fraction is only
$0.01$--$0.03$, so the soil term is negligible. \dead{Hypothesis 2: the soil fields are
corrupt.} GRIB 117--120 do carry an inconsistent header (\texttt{N=128} with a 256-row
\texttt{pl} but only $40\,320$ values, the O96 count), which \textbf{breaks \texttt{cdo} and
\texttt{grib\_get\_data}} on those fields and should be reported upstream --- but the values
are O96-ordered and correct: zero over $99.2\,\%$ of ocean cells, $\mathrm{corr}(\alpha_{soil},
\mathrm{lsm})=+0.894$. Not our bias.

\subsection{The real signature is snow-melt timing}\label{sub:albsnow}
The blend at 70--75N yields $0.158$ while the model runs at $0.303$; the extra $\approx0.145$
is snow. Monthly, the model is \textbf{too dark in April} ($-0.06$ to $-0.07$),
\textbf{near-perfect in July/August} when snow-free ($+0.004$), and \textbf{too bright in June}
($+0.086$ to $+0.116$) \textbf{and September} ($+0.08$ to $+0.10$). So \texttt{RVVEGALB} is
essentially right and the \emph{snow season is too long at both ends}.

\textbf{Cause and effect.} Through February--April the model is too cold \emph{and} too dark
simultaneously; if bright snow caused the cold those signs would agree, so the winter cold is
\textbf{not} albedo-driven. The albedo bias turns positive only in June, after the surface
crosses freezing. But the June snow is a genuine defect in its own right: \textbf{June is
$+6.2\,^\circ$C with $213$\,W/m$^2$ of insolation and $0.020$\,m\,w.e.\ still lying}, and the
implied snow-cover fraction is $\approx17\,\%$ where CERES implies $\approx2\,\%$. That points
at the \textbf{snow-cover-fraction formulation}, not snow albedo (too \emph{low} in April) and
not melt energy (ample).

\subsection{Growing season: June is albedo, July is cloud}\label{sub:albgrow}
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
Siberia 55--75N, W/m$^2$ & Jun & Jul & Aug & JJA \\
\midrule
cloud             & $6.9$ & $\mathbf{9.9}$ & $4.4$ & $7.0$ \\
albedo / snow     & $\mathbf{13.4}$ & $1.7$ & $1.3$ & $5.5$ \\
total             & $20.2$ & $11.6$ & $5.7$ & $12.5$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Almost cleanly separated in time. The residual snow term is $5.5$\,W/m$^2$ on the JJA
mean, essentially all in June, worth $\approx0.2$--$0.7$\,K depending on which sensitivity
applies --- potentially another F4-sized gain, and \emph{orthogonal} to F4, which fixes SW-down
while this fixes the absorbed fraction.

\textbf{But albedo does not explain the land cold bias.} Insolation-weighted over land
60S--75N excluding Greenland, the mean albedo-induced SW loss is $-3.00$\,W/m$^2$ against a mean
T2m bias of $-1.35$\,K, with spatial correlation $r=+0.145$ and slope $+0.033$\,K per W/m$^2$.
Decisively, \textbf{cells where the model is too \emph{dark} are still $-1.16$\,K cold}. A
$\approx1.2$\,K land-wide cold bias therefore has no albedo signature at all, consistent with
the global tropospheric cold bias of \S\ref{sub:vprof}. Fixing albedo is worth doing; it is not
the root cause.

\section{Round 13: snow cover fraction, the June lever}\label{sec:h}
\subsection{Design and prediction}\label{sub:hdesign}
The albedo decomposition (\S\ref{sec:albedo}) splits the $12.5$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ Siberian JJA
surface-SW deficit into ${\sim}7.0$ cloud and ${\sim}5.5$ \textbf{surface albedo}, with the
albedo half concentrated almost entirely in \textbf{June} (\S\ref{sub:albgrow}; bias $+0.086$,
July and August near-perfect). That is a snow-\emph{melt-timing} signature, not a
snow-\emph{brightness} one. Cause and effect were established before touching anything: winter
cold is \emph{not} albedo-driven, since February--April is simultaneously cold and dark
(\S\ref{sub:albsnow}), which rules out the albedo\,$\to$\,cold direction for those months.

Snow cover fraction (\texttt{surfbc\_ctl\_mod.F90:317--322}) is
\[ \mathrm{ZCVS} \;=\; \min\!\left(1,\; d_{\mathrm{snow}}[\mathrm{cm}] \cdot \mathtt{RQSNCR}\right),
\qquad \mathtt{RQSNCR} = 1/10 \ \ \text{under \texttt{LESN09=T}}. \]
A grid box is therefore fully snow-covered at \textbf{10\,cm}, linearly below that, with no
dependence on vegetation height or orographic roughness. For boreal forest this is wrong in an
obvious direction: $10$\,cm of snow does not hide a spruce canopy, and the observed masking
depth over forest is several times larger. The H-series sets $\mathtt{RQSNCR}=1/30$.

\emph{Why the ice sheets are safe:} Antarctica and Greenland carry snow far deeper than
$30$\,cm, so $\min(1,\cdot)$ saturates at $1$ for any value in this range and they are
untouched. Only shallow, marginal, melting snow responds --- which is the target.

\textbf{H1} applies the change alone against control (clean attribution); \textbf{H2} applies
it on top of G1's F4 source change and D2b namelist (the deployable stack). $\mathrm{H2}-
\mathrm{G1}$ measured against $\mathrm{H1}-\mathrm{control}$ gives the interaction directly.

\textbf{Falsifiable prediction, recorded before the runs finished:}
\dead{H1 recovers a good part of June's $13.4$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ albedo term, worth
${\sim}+0.2$ to $+0.7$\,K on Siberian JJA T2m, and does little in July and August.}
\textcolor{red}{\textbf{Falsified}} (\S\ref{sub:hres}): JJA came out at $+0.020$\,K,
$t=0.17$. The failure mode was not the anticipated one --- it did not warm July as much as
June, it failed to warm \emph{any} summer month.

\subsection{Results: prediction falsified, lever rejected}\label{sub:hres}
\textbf{Predicted $+0.2$ to $+0.7$\,K on Siberian JJA T2m; measured $+0.020$\,K, $t=0.17$.}
And the lever inflicts the largest winter cold bias in the campaign, $-1.233$\,K DJF against a
$\pm0.588$ threshold, beating the $-0.720$ for which B5 was rejected.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
 & DJF & MAM & JJA & SON \\
\midrule
H1 \texttt{snowcr30} & $\mathbf{-1.233}^{*}$ & $-0.473^{*}$ & $\mathbf{+0.020}$ & $+0.428$ \\
H2 $=$ G1 $+$ snow & $-0.973^{*}$ & $-0.342$ & $+0.662^{*}$ & $+0.863^{*}$ \\
\emph{(G1, reference)} & $+0.070$ & $-0.296$ & $+0.521^{*}$ & $+0.280$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent\textbf{Why it failed: the mechanism was pointed at the wrong month.} The lever did
exactly what it was built to do to the albedo --- but not where the reasoning assumed. H1
all-sky surface albedo change ($\times 100$, ${}^{*}$ = clears that month's threshold):

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r r r r r r r}
\toprule
 & Jan & Feb & Mar & Apr & May & \textbf{Jun} & Jul & Aug & \textbf{Sep} & \textbf{Oct} & \textbf{Nov} & Dec \\
\midrule
H1 & $-0.51^{*}$ & $-0.21$ & $-0.42^{*}$ & $-0.84^{*}$ & $-1.43^{*}$ & $\mathbf{-0.95}^{*}$ &
$-0.10^{*}$ & $-0.32^{*}$ & $\mathbf{-3.08}^{*}$ & $\mathbf{-8.16}^{*}$ & $\mathbf{-4.66}^{*}$ & $-1.60^{*}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent The response concentrates in \textbf{September--November}, the \emph{snow-onset}
season, and is small in June. The design error is plain in hindsight:
$\mathrm{ZCVS}=\min(1, d_{\mathrm{cm}}\cdot\mathtt{RQSNCR})$ can only respond where snow is
\textbf{shallow}, and snow is shallow while it is \emph{accumulating in autumn}, not while it
is melting in June --- by June the pack either still saturates $\min(1,\cdot)$ under both
settings or is already gone. \textbf{Shallow snow is an autumn phenomenon, not a melt
phenomenon.} Consistently, June/July/August T2m move $-0.076/-0.067/+0.205$ (none
significant) while \textbf{October T2m is $+1.135$\,K}.

\noindent\textbf{The winter cooling is not radiative.} February and March are dark yet cool by
$-1.65$ and $-1.19$\,K. Reducing the snow-covered fraction moves area from the exposed and
sheltered \textbf{snow tiles (5, 7)} onto the vegetation tiles, and the snow tile is what
buffers the surface against radiative cooling in polar night. The lever trades a small autumn
warming for a large winter cooling through \textbf{tile fractions, not albedo}.

\noindent\textbf{Verdict: rejected}, and \texttt{RQSNCR} reverted. Three things survive:
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep2pt
\item The June surface-albedo bias is \textbf{not} dominated by the snow-cover-fraction
  formulation. Its cause --- snow-albedo decay, vegetation masking of snow albedo, or the
  snow-free surface albedo itself --- remains unidentified.
\item \texttt{RQSNCR} has large leverage on \emph{autumn} albedo ($-8$ points in October).
  If an autumn or snow-onset bias is ever diagnosed, this is the knob.
\item H2 posts the campaign's best SO SW RMSE ($4.787$), best subpolar N Atl ($4.713$) and
  best global T2m RMSE ($1.530$) --- but that is D2b's doing, and the winter damage
  disqualifies the combination. \textbf{G1 remains the configuration to carry forward.}
\end{enumerate}

\section{Round 14 (2026--08--03): the June albedo bias is a SPRING MELT bias}\label{sec:melt}
Round 13 established what the June albedo bias is \emph{not} (\S\ref{sub:hres}): not the
snow-cover formulation. This section identifies what it is, entirely from output already on
disk plus ERA5 --- no new integration was needed to get here.

\subsection{Against ERA5: too much snow, and snow that is too DARK}\label{sub:e5alb}
\texttt{scripts/analysis/albedo\_decompose.py}, model \texttt{amip\_presentday} vs ERA5
1990--2014, both land-masked to the Siberian box, June:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
June & model & ERA5 & $\Delta$ \\
\midrule
surface albedo \texttt{fal} & $0.2147$ & $0.1730$ & $\mathbf{+0.042}$ \\
snow cover fraction & $0.380$ & $0.261$ & $\mathbf{+0.119}$ \\
snow albedo \texttt{asn} & $0.658$ & $0.759$ & $\mathbf{-0.102}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Both \texttt{f\_snow} columns are the \emph{same} HTESSEL formula
$\min(1, d_{\mathrm{cm}}/10)$ applied to each dataset's snow mass, so the gap is
\textbf{snow AMOUNT, not the cover formula} --- which is precisely why round 13, which
changed only the formula, could not move it. The model carries roughly twice ERA5's June
snow mass.

\emph{Corroborated independently by CERES} (\S\ref{sub:ceresalb}), which puts the June
excess slightly higher at $+0.046$; and the cover excess is larger still against
satellite, $+0.176$ rather than $+0.119$ (\S\ref{sub:satsnow}).

\noindent\textbf{And the model's snow is too dark, not too bright} ($-0.10$ in June,
$-0.11$ in May). Making snow albedo more realistic would make June \emph{worse}. Any lever
that brightens snow is pushing the wrong way; the excess reflection comes from snow
\emph{extent}, not snow \emph{brightness}.

\emph{Two things this analysis does not claim.} The naive two-term split
$\alpha_{\mathrm{box}} = f\,\alpha_{\mathrm{snow}} + (1-f)\,\alpha_{\mathrm{free}}$ does
\textbf{not} close: backing out $\alpha_{\mathrm{free}}$ gives negative values over the box,
because HTESSEL does not apply \texttt{asn} to the whole snow fraction --- tile 7 is snow
\emph{sheltered} under high vegetation and carries a canopy-shaded albedo far below
\texttt{asn}. That the residual is large and negative in exactly the melt months is direct
evidence the canopy-masking term already does most of the work there, so no snow-free albedo
is reported. Separately, ERA5's albedo scheme is a relative of HTESSEL, so \texttt{asn}
agreement is not independent evidence; only ERA5's snow \emph{extent} is observationally
constrained, via assimilated IMS snow cover.

\subsection{Accumulation is right; the melt is a month late and 30\,\% too slow}\label{sub:melt}
Snow water equivalent over the box, model vs ERA5 [mm], with the month-to-month increment:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\toprule
 & model & ERA5 & $\Delta$ & $\delta$(model) & $\delta$(ERA5) \\
\midrule
Jan & $89.8$ & $82.3$ & $+7.5$ & & \\
Feb & $106.5$ & $97.6$ & $+9.0$ & $+16.7$ & $+15.3$ \\
Mar & $122.1$ & $114.5$ & $+7.7$ & $+15.6$ & $+16.9$ \\
\textbf{Apr} & $131.6$ & $112.8$ & $+18.7$ & $\mathbf{+9.4}$ & $\mathbf{-1.6}$ \\
\textbf{May} & $98.1$ & $65.3$ & $\mathbf{+32.8}$ & $\mathbf{-33.5}$ & $\mathbf{-47.5}$ \\
Jun & $14.1$ & $9.6$ & $+4.5$ & $-84.0$ & $-55.7$ \\
Nov & $49.2$ & $41.5$ & $+7.7$ & $+25.9$ & $+25.4$ \\
Dec & $70.7$ & $63.8$ & $+6.9$ & $+21.5$ & $+22.3$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{Accumulation is essentially correct.} Through the accumulation season the
model is only 7--9\,\% high and the monthly increments track ERA5 almost exactly
($+16.7$ vs $+15.3$, $+15.6$ vs $+16.9$, $+25.9$ vs $+25.4$, $+21.5$ vs $+22.3$). The
divergence is entirely in spring:

\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{April}: the model is still \emph{gaining} ($+9.4$\,mm) while ERA5 has already
  peaked and begun to melt ($-1.6$). \textbf{The snowpack peaks a month late.}
\item \textbf{May}: the model melts $33.5$\,mm against ERA5's $47.5$ --- \textbf{30\,\% too
  slow} --- leaving it with 50\,\% more snow by the end of May.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Both figures come from ERA5. The satellite record confirms the timing directly
and makes it worse: melt-out is 13 days late, and June cover is nearly double observed
(\S\ref{sub:satsnow}).

\subsection{May is albedo, not cloud --- and it is a runaway}\label{sub:mayloop}
Model \texttt{amip\_presentday} vs CERES on the same box:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
 & SW$_{\mathrm{net}}$ mod & SW$_{\mathrm{net}}$ CERES & $\Delta$ & SW$_\downarrow$ mod & SW$_\downarrow$ CERES & $\Delta$ \\
\midrule
Apr & $87.8$ & $86.6$ & $+1.2$ & $176.9$ & $187.7$ & $-10.8$ \\
\textbf{May} & $122.5$ & $136.4$ & $\mathbf{-13.9}$ & $220.7$ & $224.2$ & $\mathbf{-3.5}$ \\
Jun & $167.6$ & $189.7$ & $-22.2$ & $215.7$ & $228.2$ & $-12.5$ \\
Jul & $165.9$ & $180.4$ & $-14.5$ & $193.8$ & $208.7$ & $-14.9$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{In May the downwelling flux is nearly right} ($-3.5$) while the net is
$-13.9$: the May deficit is \emph{almost pure surface albedo}, i.e. the excess snow
reflecting. June is roughly half cloud ($-12.5$ of downwelling) and half albedo. July is
essentially all cloud ($-14.9$ down, $-14.5$ net), consistent with \S\ref{sub:albgrow}.

\noindent This closes a \textbf{self-reinforcing loop}: late melt $\to$ more snow in May
$\to$ higher albedo $\to$ $-13.9$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ less absorbed $\to$ less melt energy $\to$
snow survives into June. It also explains why the model melts slower \emph{despite} having
darker snow than ERA5: the brightness term is working against the bias, and the extent term
is winning.

\subsection{The gap F4 leaves: tundra is the largest cover type in the box}\label{sub:tundra}
Area-weighted vegetation cover over land in the evaluation box, from the model's own
\texttt{tvh}/\texttt{tvl}/\texttt{cvh}/\texttt{cvl} output:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l r l}
\toprule
 & type & cover & \\
\midrule
low & 9 \ Tundra & $\mathbf{25.6\,\%}$ & \textbf{F4 does not touch this} \\
high & 4 \ Deciduous Needleleaf & $19.2\,\%$ & F4 \\
high & 18 Mixed Forest & $5.2\,\%$ & \\
low & 13 Bogs/Marshes & $3.9\,\%$ & \\
high & 5 \ Deciduous Broadleaf & $3.6\,\%$ & \\
high & 19 Interrupted Forest & $3.5\,\%$ & \\
high & 3 \ Evergreen Needleleaf & $3.4\,\%$ & F4 \\
high & 16 Evergreen Shrubs & $3.0\,\%$ & \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{Tundra is the single largest cover type in the box, and the campaign's best
lever misses it entirely.} F4 reaches about $24\,\%$ of the box (types 3, 4); tundra is
another $26\,\%$.

\noindent\textbf{Its stomatal resistance is a table-internal outlier.} \texttt{RVRSMIN(9)}
$= 80$\,s\,m$^{-1}$ is the \emph{lowest value of any vegetated type} in HTESSEL:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\texttt{crops 100 \ short grass 100 \ tall grass 100 \ semidesert 150 \ decid.\ broadleaf 175}\\
\texttt{interrupted forest 175 \ evergreen shrubs 225 \ decid.\ shrubs 225 \ evergr.\ broadleaf 240}\\
\texttt{bogs/marshes 240 \ desert 250 \ needleleaf 250 \ mixed forest 250 \ \textbf{TUNDRA 80}}
\end{center}

\noindent The model therefore has arctic tundra transpiring \emph{more freely than tropical
grassland}. Tundra's closest physiognomic analogues in the same table --- evergreen and
deciduous shrubs --- are both $225$, and bogs/marshes, which co-occur with tundra in this very
box, are $240$. This is a \textbf{table-consistency} argument, checkable against the table
itself and independent of our bias; it is not a value chosen because it helps. Contrast the
standing criticism of F4, whose $250\to1000$ remains an unanchored $4\times$ excursion.

\subsection{Round 14 runs, and their predictions}\label{sub:r14runs}
All three are \textbf{namelist-only} (\S\ref{sub:nmltune}) --- one shared binary, no rebuild,
no serialisation against the model tree. That is what the \texttt{NAMSURFTUNE} work bought.

\begin{itemize}\itemsep3pt
\item \textbf{G2} --- \texttt{RVRSMIN(3,4)} $=500$. Half-strength F4.
\item \textbf{G3} --- \texttt{RVRSMIN(3,4)} $=2000$. Double-strength F4.

  G2/G3 bracket G1 and map the response curve, answering the standing criticism that $1000$
  was never chosen from a measurement. \emph{Prediction:} G2 keeps $>60\,\%$ of G1's gain
  ($\ge +0.31$\,K) \dead{and G3 adds little ($< +0.15$\,K beyond G1)}. First half held
  (64\,\%); second half \textcolor{red}{\textbf{failed}} --- G3 added $+0.355$
  (\S\ref{sub:r14res}). \emph{The response is a ramp, which was flagged in advance as an
  argument} \textbf{against} \emph{the approach, not for a larger number.}
\item \textbf{G4} --- G1 plus \texttt{RVRSMIN(9)} $80\to225$ (tundra), keeping $1000$ for
  types 3 and 4. \emph{Prediction:} $+0.2$ to $+0.5$\,K beyond G1's $+0.521$ (held: $+0.431$),
  \dead{accompanied by a measurable drop in May/June snow water equivalent}
  \textcolor{red}{\textbf{--- falsified}}, \S\ref{sub:r14res}. As stated in advance,
  \textbf{T2m rose with no change in May--June snow mass, so the melt mechanism is
  unsupported} and the gain is the plain sensible-heat route.
  Guardrails: tundra is confined to high latitudes so tropical net TOA should stay near
  $42.6$; check DJF against its own $\pm0.588$\,K threshold and the Nordic Seas SW RMSE that
  G1 already degraded.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Round 14 results: G4 adopted, mechanism falsified, \texttt{RVRSMIN} capped}\label{sub:r14res}
\textbf{G4 is the best configuration of the campaign and closes $48\,\%$ of the boreal bias},
against G1's $26\,\%$ --- and its stated mechanism is falsified. Both matter.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\toprule
metric & control & G1 & G2 (500) & G3 (2000) & \textbf{G4} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Siberia JJA T2m} [$^\circ$C] & $9.73$ & $+0.521$ & $+0.336$ & $+0.876$ & $\mathbf{+0.952}$ ($t{=}7.68$) \\
Siberia sfc net SW & $153.78$ & $+5.54$ & $+3.21$ & $+7.27$ & $\mathbf{+8.10}$ \\
\textbf{SO SW RMSE} & $6.877$ & $4.809$ & $4.816$ & $4.903$ & $\mathbf{4.800}$ \\
subpolar N Atl SW RMSE & $5.007$ & $4.872$ & $4.866$ & $4.728$ & $\mathbf{4.738}$ \\
Nordic Seas SW RMSE & $9.058$ & $+0.300$ & $+0.098$ & $+0.304$ & $\mathbf{+0.089}$ \\
tropics net TOA & $42.61$ & $+0.096$ & $+0.041$ & $+0.082$ & $+0.124$ \\
global T2m RMSE [K] & $1.579$ & $1.553$ & $1.558$ & $1.546$ & $\mathbf{1.543}$ \\
DJF & --- & $+0.070$ & $-0.325$ & $\mathbf{-0.798}^{*}$ & $-0.385$ \\
MAM / SON & --- & $-0.296/{+}0.280$ & $-0.462^{*}/{-}0.030$ & $-0.217/{-}0.012$ & $-0.360/{+}0.185$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent G4 is seasonally clean on every season's own threshold, and it
\textbf{largely repairs G1's Nordic Seas damage} ($+0.300 \to +0.089$) --- which nothing
predicted and nothing yet explains. An unexplained improvement is as much a loose end as an
unexplained degradation.

\subsubsection*{The melt mechanism is falsified}
The prediction on record was a measurable drop in May--June snow water equivalent. Siberian
land box SWE [mm], with run$\times$year ANOVA thresholds:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
 & Mar ($\pm3.53$) & Apr ($\pm3.85$) & May ($\pm5.25$) & Jun ($\pm2.72$) \\
\midrule
control & $121.98$ & $131.07$ & $97.12$ & $15.39$ \\
G1 & $+0.88$ & $+0.37$ & $\mathbf{+5.58}^{*}$ & $\mathbf{+5.68}^{*}$ \\
\textbf{G4} & $-1.44$ & $-2.39$ & $+2.75$ & $+1.60$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{G4's snow mass did not move.} T2m rose $+0.952$\,K with May and June SWE
unchanged within noise, so the gain is the \textbf{plain sensible-heat route}, exactly as for
F4 --- \emph{not} the melt-timing route that motivated the run. The tundra lever stands on its
independent table-consistency justification (\S\ref{sub:tundra}) and it works; the mechanism
claimed for it does not.

\noindent \textbf{And F4 makes the snow bias worse.} G1 \emph{significantly increases}
May--June SWE ($+5.58$, $+5.68$, both clearing threshold). The campaign's best boreal lever
improves temperature while \textbf{aggravating} the very snow bias it was meant to address.
Leading suspect: cutting evapotranspiration also cuts \textbf{sublimation}, a genuine
snowpack sink. Untested.

\subsubsection*{\texttt{RVRSMIN} does not saturate --- cap it at 1000}
$500/1000/2000$ give $+0.336/+0.521/+0.876$: increments per doubling of $+0.185$ then
$\mathbf{+0.355}$, i.e.\ \emph{accelerating}. \textbf{There is no knee.} $1000$ is defensible
only as \emph{the largest value before the winter damage starts} --- G3 at $2000$ costs
DJF $-0.798$, clearing $\pm0.588$ and joining B5 and H2 in the ``warms JJA, cools DJF'' list.
F4's magnitude is a fitted ramp, not a corrected bias, and \S\ref{sub:r14runs} flagged this
outcome in advance as an argument against pushing it further.

\noindent \textbf{Two mechanisms for the June albedo bias are now dead}: the snow
\emph{cover formulation} (round 13) and the snow \emph{melt rate} (round 14). Untried
candidates: the snow-albedo decay timescale; the canopy-masking tile-7 formulation, which the
round-13 residual showed already does most of the work in the melt months; and the
\texttt{RVVEGALB} vegetation albedo table, which the campaign has never touched.

\subsection{Tuning without rebuilding: \texttt{NAMSURFTUNE}}\label{sub:nmltune}
The ECMWF \texttt{surf} library deliberately ships no namelist, so every HTESSEL lever in this
campaign was a source edit plus a rebuild. That serialises experiments against the single
model tree, and it puts AWI tuning inside upstream ECMWF files where it collides with
EC-Earth's tuning of the same tables at every merge.

\texttt{surf/module/surfece.F90} is already an AWI module \emph{inside} \texttt{surf} with its
own namelist, so the overrides live there: \texttt{ECE\_TUNE\_RVRSMIN}, \texttt{\_RVLAI},
\texttt{\_RVCOV}, \texttt{\_RVZ0H}, \texttt{\_RVLAMSK} (all $0{:}20$, by vegetation type) and
scalar \texttt{ECE\_TUNE\_RQSNCR} --- covering the whole F-series, the H-series and the
skin-conductivity levers. They are applied by \texttt{SURFECE\_APPLY\_TUNING} from
\texttt{susurf.F90} \emph{after} \texttt{SUSURF\_CTL} has filled the tables; nothing in the
setup path derives from these entries, so a late override is equivalent to editing the table
in place. Every element defaults to a sentinel meaning ``keep the as-released value'', so a
run setting none of them is bit-identical to untouched code, and every applied override is
logged to \texttt{NULOUT}.

They occupy their own \texttt{\&NAMSURFTUNE} group, \emph{not} \texttt{NAMECECFG}:
\texttt{NAMECECFG} is read twice from the same \texttt{fort.4}, by \texttt{ECE\_CONFIG} in
\texttt{arpifs/ecearth.F90} and by \texttt{SURFECE\_CONFIG} in \texttt{surf}, and a Fortran
namelist read aborts with \emph{invalid reference to variable} on any name the reading module
does not declare --- so adding the tune entries there killed the arpifs read at
\texttt{su0yoma.F90:152}. Found by a one-day test run, which is the argument for always doing
one. Exactly \textbf{one line} changes in an ECMWF file. G1 is now

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\texttt{NAMCLDP: RCL\_INPSEA=0.2, RCL\_INPPMIN=70000.0} \quad
\texttt{NAMSURFTUNE: ECE\_TUNE\_RVRSMIN(3)=ECE\_TUNE\_RVRSMIN(4)=1000.0}
\end{center}

\section{Observational grounding: what ERA5 can and cannot settle}\label{sec:obs}
Rounds 13 and 14 leaned on ERA5 for the snow diagnosis. That is a methodological
weakness worth stating plainly: \textbf{ERA5's land surface is the same HTESSEL family
as ours}, so comparing our snow and flux fields to ERA5's is partly comparing a scheme
against a sibling of itself. This section replaces ERA5 with genuine observations
wherever one exists, and marks the places where none does.

\subsection{Surface albedo: CERES corroborates ERA5, and the model bias is real}\label{sub:ceresalb}
CERES supplies \texttt{sfc\_sw\_up} and \texttt{sfc\_sw\_down} separately, so their ratio
is a surface albedo derived from a \emph{radiative retrieval} rather than a land model.
Siberian land box, 1990--2014, against model \texttt{amip\_presentday} and ERA5
\texttt{fal}:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
 & CERES & ERA5 & model & ERA5$-$CERES & \textbf{model$-$CERES} & SW$_\downarrow$ \\
\midrule
Mar & $0.5540$ & $0.5325$ & $0.5055$ & $-0.021$ & $\mathbf{-0.049}$ & $109$ \\
Apr & $0.5386$ & $0.5045$ & $0.4990$ & $-0.034$ & $\mathbf{-0.040}$ & $188$ \\
\textbf{May} & $0.3918$ & $0.3722$ & $0.4285$ & $-0.020$ & $\mathbf{+0.037}$ & $224$ \\
\textbf{Jun} & $0.1685$ & $0.1730$ & $0.2147$ & $+0.005$ & $\mathbf{+0.046}$ & $228$ \\
Jul & $0.1357$ & $0.1362$ & $0.1487$ & $+0.001$ & $+0.013$ & $209$ \\
Oct--Feb & --- & --- & --- & $+0.10\ldots+0.13$ & --- & $6$--$42$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{ERA5 and CERES agree to $\pm0.02$ in every month with enough sun to
retrieve} (Mar--Sep), which is what licenses ERA5's albedo for this purpose. The June
excess is confirmed \emph{independently} at $+0.046$ --- slightly larger than the
$+0.042$ measured against ERA5 (\S\ref{sub:e5alb}). The two diverge badly Oct--Feb, but
at $6$--$42$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ downwelling no albedo retrieval is trustworthy at these
latitudes, so that is expected rather than disqualifying.

\subsection{Snow cover and melt date: satellite, and ERA5 was flattering us}\label{sub:satsnow}
The Rutgers NH 24\,km Weekly Snow Cover Extent CDR (NSIDC G10035, 1980--2024; NOAA
visible-band snow charts to 1999, NOAA/NIC IMS thereafter) gives snow \emph{extent} and
\emph{melt timing} from observation. \texttt{scripts/analysis/snowcover\_vs\_satellite.py}.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
snow cover fraction & \textbf{Rutgers (sat.)} & ERA5 & model & \textbf{G4} \\
\midrule
Apr & $0.950$ & $0.991$ & $0.964$ & $0.961$ \\
\textbf{May} & $\mathbf{0.647}$ & $0.796$ & $0.890$ & $0.893$ \\
\textbf{Jun} & $\mathbf{0.203}$ & $0.261$ & $0.380$ & $\mathbf{0.447}$ \\
Sep & $0.075$ & $0.178$ & $0.257$ & $0.249$ \\
Oct & $0.612$ & $0.755$ & $0.825$ & $0.834$ \\
\midrule
\textbf{melt-out (50\,\% crossing)} & \textbf{11 May} & $\sim$17 May & \textbf{24 May} & \textbf{28 May} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Three consequences.

\textbf{(i) ERA5 was flattering the model.} ERA5 itself runs $+0.058$ high in June and
$+0.149$ in May against satellite, so the model excess quoted in \S\ref{sub:e5alb} as
$+0.119$ is really $\mathbf{+0.176}$ --- June snow cover is close to \emph{double} the
observed value.

\textbf{(ii) The model melts about 15 days late} (control; \texttt{amip\_presentday} is 13),
now measured against an observation rather than inferred from a sibling model.
\emph{Note the two runs differ} --- June cover $0.413$ / melt-out 26~May for the control,
$0.380$ / 24~May for \texttt{amip\_presentday} --- and earlier drafts of this report quoted
them interchangeably. This is round 14's ``peaks a month late''
(\S\ref{sub:melt}) confirmed independently and given a number.

\textbf{(iii) \textcolor{red}{G4 makes the snow field worse.}} June cover
$0.380 \to 0.447$; melt-out 24 May $\to$ 28 May. The campaign's best \emph{temperature}
configuration pushes the spring snow cycle \emph{further} from satellite, consistent
with G1/F4 significantly increasing May--June SWE (\S\ref{sub:r14res}). \textbf{This
matters for the coupled goal:} a later melt is a later growing-season onset for
LPJ-GUESS, which is a plausible route to losing boreal forest even while AMIP T2m
improves. It should be weighed before G4 is committed to the PI spin-up.

\emph{Comparability caveat:} Rutgers SCE is a \textbf{binary} classification per 24\,km
cell, whereas HTESSEL's \texttt{ZCVS} is a \emph{sub-grid} fraction within a ${\sim}100$\,km
cell. The two agree on ``how much of the region is snow'' but differ in how a partially
covered cell is counted, which matters most mid-melt. The \textbf{crossing date} is the
robust comparison; absolute mid-melt fractions are not over-read here.

\subsection{Where no observation exists: the turbulent fluxes}\label{sub:noobs}
\textcolor{red}{\textbf{A claim is withdrawn here.}} The surface energy budget
(\texttt{scripts/analysis/snow\_energy.py}) appeared to show the model venting
$3$--$5$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ more upward sensible heat than ERA5 in February--April, which would
have made turbulent exchange over snow the round-15 target. \dead{The model loses its
March--April melt energy to the atmosphere as sensible heat.} \textbf{That comparison has
no observational authority}: ERA5's \texttt{sshf}/\texttt{slhf} are pure HTESSEL output,
unconstrained by any assimilated observation, so it compares our scheme to a sibling of
itself. It survives only as a hypothesis. The same caveat applies to the cold-content
test on \texttt{tsn} (model within a few tenths of a K of ERA5), which is likewise
model-versus-model --- though as a \emph{null} result it is the less dangerous of the two.

\textbf{No satellite product measures surface turbulent fluxes.} Only eddy-covariance
towers can, and the Siberian candidates (Spasskaya Pad, ZOTTO) are point sites, not a
gridded field. Any round-15 lever aimed at turbulent exchange over snow would therefore
be tested against its \emph{consequences} --- melt date and snow cover, both now
observationally anchored --- rather than against the flux itself.

\subsection{Summary: what each reference can support}\label{sub:refsummary}
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\toprule
quantity & reference & status \\
\midrule
surface albedo & CERES up/down & \textbf{observational}; confirms model $+0.046$ June \\
snow cover extent, melt date & Rutgers/IMS & \textbf{observational}; model 13 days late \\
TOA and surface SW fluxes & CERES & \textbf{observational} \\
snow water equivalent & ERA5 & partly constrained (SYNOP, IMS); forest bias \\
snowfall, snowmelt & ERA5 & model output, weakly constrained \\
\texttt{sshf}, \texttt{slhf}, \texttt{str}, \texttt{tsn} & ERA5 & \textbf{unconstrained --- HTESSEL output} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent ERA5 assimilates IMS snow cover, so ERA5 agreeing with Rutgers on extent is
\emph{expected} and is not independent corroboration --- but it is what justifies having
treated extent as ERA5's trustworthy field throughout.

\section{Route B resolved: HTESSEL has no sub-grid snow depletion}\label{sec:scf}
Rounds 13 and 14 both failed on Route B, and the observational work
(\S\ref{sec:obs}) sharpened the target without naming the cause. It is now named,
and it also explains \emph{why both rounds failed in the way they did}.

\subsection{The arithmetic that settles it}\label{sub:scfwhy}
Snow cover fraction is set from the \textbf{cell-mean} snow depth alone
(\texttt{surfbc\_ctl\_mod.F90:317--322}):
\[ \mathrm{ZCVS} = \min\!\left(1,\ \bar{d}_{\mathrm{cm}} \cdot \mathtt{RQSNCR}\right),
   \qquad \mathtt{RQSNCR} = 1/10 . \]
So cover can only fall below 1 once the \emph{cell mean} drops under 10\,cm. The
model's actual depth distribution over the box:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
 & mean $d$ & $p_{10}$ & $p_{50}$ & $p_{90}$ & frac $<10$\,cm & ZCVS \\
\midrule
Mar & $51.1$ & $31.4$ & $48.7$ & $75.6$ & $0.037$ & $0.964$ \\
Apr & $48.9$ & $24.8$ & $47.2$ & $75.8$ & $0.044$ & $0.964$ \\
\textbf{May} & $28.8$ & $4.5$ & $\mathbf{27.1}$ & $56.5$ & $0.181$ & $\mathbf{0.890}$ \\
Jun & $4.8$ & $0.0$ & $2.7$ & $13.5$ & $0.842$ & $0.380$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The median May cell still holds 27\,cm}, so the formula returns
essentially full cover --- while the satellite record shows $0.647$. Real
$100$\,km regions at $27$\,cm mean depth are patchy: ridges blow bare, hollows hold
metre drifts, canopies intercept. The model has no representation of that variance.

\subsection{Why both previous attempts had to fail}\label{sub:scffail}
This is the part that makes the diagnosis convincing rather than merely plausible.

\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{Round 13 was doomed by arithmetic.} Changing \texttt{RQSNCR}
  $1/10\to1/30$ moves the threshold from $10$ to $30$\,cm --- which does nothing at
  all where depths are $27$--$51$\,cm. It could only bite where depths sit
  \emph{near} the threshold, i.e.\ $13$--$27$\,cm, which is October and November.
  That is precisely where the response appeared ($-8.2$ albedo points in October,
  $-0.95$ in June, \S\ref{sub:hres}). The lever was not weak; it was arithmetically
  incapable of acting in spring.
\item \textbf{The commented-out \texttt{tanh} and \texttt{sqrt} variants} at
  \texttt{surfbc\_ctl\_mod.F90:325} share the defect --- still functions of the
  cell-mean depth. $\tanh(27/10) = 0.99$.
\item \textbf{Snow brightness is the wrong direction} (model snow is darker than
  both ERA5 and CERES) and \textbf{energy supply is not short} in Mar--Apr
  (\S\ref{sub:ceresalb}).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The fix, validated offline before implementation}\label{sub:scffix}
A depth-only function cannot work; the depletion must respond to something that
tracks the \emph{state} of the pack. Niu \& Yang (2007), as used in Noah-MP and CLM:
\[ \mathrm{SCF} = \tanh\!\left(\frac{d}{2.5\,z_{0g}\,(\rho_{\mathrm{bulk}}/\rho_{\mathrm{new}})^{m}}\right) \]
The density ratio supplies exactly the missing behaviour: aged, melting snow is
denser, so it patches out at a given depth. It needs \textbf{no new prognostic} ---
depth and density are already carried.

Tested on existing output \emph{before} writing any code, Siberian box,
\texttt{amip\_presentday} vs Rutgers 1990--2014:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
 & satellite & as-released & $z_0=0.01$ & $z_0=0.02$ \\
\midrule
Jan--Mar & $0.999$ & $0.964$ & $0.964$ & $0.952$ \\
Apr & $0.950$ & $0.964$ & $0.948$ & $0.893$ \\
\textbf{May} & $\mathbf{0.647}$ & $0.890$ & $0.772$ & $\mathbf{0.588}$ \\
\textbf{Jun} & $\mathbf{0.203}$ & $0.380$ & $0.321$ & $\mathbf{0.180}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent $z_0 = 0.02$ matches the melt season closely at a small cost in midwinter
cover; $z_0 = 0.01$ is conservative, leaving winter untouched and closing about half
the May--June gap.

\emph{Provenance.} The problem and the two standard remedies are well established in the
land-surface literature: Liston (2004) sets out why a cell-mean depth cannot represent
depletion; Niu \& Yang (2007) give the density-dependent $\tanh$ form used here, calibrated
against North American basin observations and adopted in Noah-MP and CLM; Swenson \&
Lawrence (2012) give the main alternative, separate accumulation and depletion curves driven
by a snow-depth standard deviation, which is more faithful but needs an extra prognostic;
Roesch et al.\ (2001) take the third route of adding sub-grid \emph{orographic} variance in a
GCM. Douville et al.\ (1995) is the classic depth-threshold form that HTESSEL's
\texttt{RQSNCR} formulation resembles, and Dutra et al.\ (2010) documents the \texttt{LESN09}
scheme this campaign has been modifying. That CMIP-class models share the bias found here is
documented by Thackeray et al.\ (2015), and its consequence for the snow-albedo feedback
spread by Qu \& Hall (2014) and Loranty et al.\ (2014). Betts \& Ball (1997) remains the
reference for boreal canopy masking, which is why the tile-7 residual behaves as it does
(\S\ref{sub:e5alb}).

\textbf{Implemented as \texttt{ECE\_SNOW\_SCF} in \texttt{\&NAMSURFTUNE}}
(\S\ref{sub:nmltune}), with \texttt{ECE\_SNOW\_SCF\_Z0}, \texttt{\_M}, \texttt{\_RHONEW}.
\textbf{Default is $0$ = the as-released formula}, so the change is inert unless
switched on, consistent with every other entry in that namelist. Verified by a 1-day
run: the switch logs to \texttt{NULOUT}, no namelist errors, the model steps normally.

\subsection{What this still does not tell us}\label{sub:scfcaveat}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The temperature leverage remains unproven} (\S\ref{sub:weaklink}). G4
  gained a full kelvin with snow mass unchanged and cover \emph{worse}. Fixing the
  cover may buy much less T2m than its $-5.6$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ suggests. Where it should
  matter is \emph{coupled}, through LPJ-GUESS growing-season onset.
\item \textbf{The March--April mass deficit is a separate, weaker claim.} It rests on
  ERA5 SWE (forest-biased) and may be a second effect or partly an artifact
  (\S\ref{sub:melt}).
\item \textbf{Comparability.} Rutgers is a \emph{binary} $24$\,km classification, so
  its $0.647$ is ``65\,\% of cells classed snow'', not a true areal fraction. The
  melt-out \emph{date} is the robust comparison; mid-melt absolutes are softer, and
  the $z_0$ choice should not be over-fitted to them.
\end{itemize}

\section{Round 15 (2026--08--04): the snow fix works, buys no summer, and cools winter}\label{sec:r15}
Three runs, all namelist-only on one binary: \textbf{I1} $=$ G4 $+$ depletion,
\textbf{I2} $=$ depletion alone (isolating the snow route from the vegetation route), and
\textbf{I3} $=$ G4 $+$ the SDOR-scaled variant. Sections \S\ref{sub:r15works}--\S\ref{sub:skinsoil}
were written on I1 and I2 alone; \textbf{I3 is evaluated in \S\ref{sub:i3}}, and it changes
the round's verdict --- the snow route \emph{does} buy summer temperature, but only in the
scale-aware formulation, and it presents a bill in the Arctic energy budget.

\subsection{The scheme does exactly what it was built to do}\label{sub:r15works}
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\toprule
Siberian snow cover & satellite & control & G4 & \textbf{I1} & \textbf{I2} \\
\midrule
Apr & $0.950$ & $0.962$ & $0.961$ & $0.931$ & $0.931$ \\
May & $0.647$ & $0.891$ & $0.893$ & $\mathbf{0.703}$ & $\mathbf{0.721}$ \\
Jun & $0.203$ & $0.413$ & $0.447$ & $\mathbf{0.160}$ & $\mathbf{0.175}$ \\
\midrule
\textbf{melt-out} & \textbf{11 May} & 26 May & 28 May & \textbf{12 May} & \textbf{13 May} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\noindent Melt-out went from \textbf{15 days late to 1 day late}, against the satellite record.

\subsection{And it delivers no summer temperature}\label{sub:r15notemp}
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
 & surface SW gain & JJA T2m & K per W\,m$^{-2}$ \\
\midrule
G4 (vegetation route) & $+8.10$ & $\mathbf{+0.952}$ & $0.12$ \\
\textbf{I2 (snow route alone)} & $+2.91$ & $\mathbf{+0.094}$ (noise) & $\mathbf{0.03}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{This settles \S\ref{sub:weaklink}: the link is weak.} Route~B was a genuine,
satellite-measured radiative bias with almost no temperature leverage --- per watt it is
\textbf{four times less efficient} than the vegetation route. The reading is clean: energy
added over a \emph{melting} snowpack goes into melt, not sensible heat, which is precisely
what it did. Fixing it remains right --- the snow field is now correct, which is what matters
for LPJ-GUESS growing-season onset --- but Route~B is not where the remaining boreal kelvin
lives.

\subsection{The winter response is a compensating error being removed}\label{sub:r15compensate}
Scored against ERA5 rather than against the control (period-clean, \texttt{amip\_presentday}):

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
bias vs ERA5 [K] & DJF & MAM & JJA & SON \\
\midrule
control & $-1.95$ & $-1.97$ & $-2.58$ & $-2.69$ \\
G4 & $-2.33$ & $-2.33$ & $\mathbf{-1.63}$ & $-2.51$ \\
I1 & $\mathbf{-4.71}$ & $-2.28$ & $\mathbf{-1.33}$ & $-3.46$ \\
I2 & $-4.63$ & $-2.36$ & $-2.49$ & $-3.77$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The control is too cold in every season}, so there was never a warm bias to
protect --- scoring against observations rather than against the control is what makes that
visible, and eleven rounds had not done it.

\textcolor{red}{\textbf{What this does NOT establish.}}
\dead{The excess snow cover had been propping DJF up by ${\sim}2.7$\,K, masking a $-4.7$\,K
bias that was there all along.} That reading was asserted here and is \textbf{not supported}:
winter cover is \emph{unchanged} between the two schemes ($0.963$ vs $0.964$, both saturating
at 43\,cm, \S\ref{sub:r15lw}), so excess \emph{winter} cover cannot have been doing the
propping. What is measured is only that \textbf{I1 cools DJF by $-2.76$\,K by an unidentified
route}. Whether that unmasks a pre-existing bias or creates a new one is \textbf{untested}.

The point is sharpened by a precedent this report got the other way round: H1 produced the
same signature ($-1.233$\,K DJF by cutting snow-tile fraction, \S\ref{sub:hres}) and was
\emph{rejected} for it. Identical evidence cannot be disqualifying in one run and revelatory
in another. Until the route is identified, the honest position is that the snow fix and the
winter response are two findings, not one story --- which is also why round 16 tests the
winter on its own terms rather than assuming it is recoverable.

\subsection{Ruled out by measurement: it is not a radiation-supply problem}\label{sub:r15lw}
Downward longwave over the box, model vs CERES [W\,m$^{-2}$]: Oct $\mathbf{-12.9}$,
Nov $\mathbf{-11.9}$, Dec $-3.1$, Jan $+0.1$, Feb $+5.6$ --- \textbf{DJF mean $+0.9$,
essentially exact}. That kills the Pithan et al.\ (2014) supercooled-liquid route for
midwinter. The Oct/Nov deficit is real and is a \emph{separate} autumn problem. The
$+30$\,pp polar-night cloud excess against CERES is retrieval failure over snow at night,
not evidence.

\emph{Still unresolved:} winter cover is \textbf{unchanged} ($0.963$ vs $0.964$ --- both
formulas saturate at 43\,cm) and winter SWE is slightly \emph{higher}, so the DJF response is
not a direct cover or mass effect. \texttt{str} and \texttt{sshf} shifts are the surface being
colder, i.e.\ responses. \dead{\texttt{stl1} and \texttt{skt} are unusable ($\sim0.07$\,K
--- the same dead-field trap as pressure-level \texttt{r}).} \textcolor{red}{\textbf{That was
wrong}}: both fields are sound (210--319\,K). The $0.07$ was $273/3600$ --- an analysis-side
bug that divided an \emph{instantaneous} temperature by the accumulation period. Fixed in
\texttt{boxcache.py}; unlike pressure-level \texttt{r}, which really is identically zero,
these fields were never broken. Using them (\S\ref{sub:skinsoil}) shows I1 and I2 cool
\texttt{stl4} by ${\sim}2.1$\,K, so the unexplained winter signal reaches the \emph{deep
soil} --- which matters because LPJ-GUESS is fed \texttt{ST1L}--\texttt{ST4L}. H1 cooled DJF by
$-1.233$ through a different route to the same tile fractions, so this is the second time
cutting snow-tile fraction has cooled winter hard.

\subsection{Does the warming reach the skin and the soil?}\label{sub:skinsoil}
A lever that raises 2\,m air temperature by mixing heat \emph{away} from the surface would
improve the metric we score while degrading the surface itself. That is not a cosmetic
concern: \textbf{LPJ-GUESS is fed \texttt{ST1L}--\texttt{ST4L\_LPJ} (soil temperature, layers
1--4) and \texttt{SM1L}--\texttt{SM4L\_LPJ}} alongside \texttt{T2M\_LPJG}
(\texttt{lpj\_guess/framework/OasisCoupler.cpp:17-95}), so a warmer-air/colder-ground
configuration would hand the vegetation model an incoherent state. Skin temperature itself is
not coupled.

Siberian land box, JJA, deltas against control [K]:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\toprule
 & T2m & \texttt{skt} & \texttt{stl1} & \texttt{stl4} & $\Delta$(\texttt{skt}$-$T2m) \\
\midrule
\emph{control (absolute)} & $9.73$ & $10.18$ & $9.31$ & $-1.31$ & $0.45$ \\
\textbf{G4} & $+0.952$ & $\mathbf{+0.990}$ & $\mathbf{+0.883}$ & $+0.157$ & $+0.037$ \\
I1 & $+1.250$ & $+1.318$ & $+1.160$ & $\mathbf{-2.092}$ & $+0.068$ \\
I2 & $+0.094$ & $+0.100$ & $+0.046$ & $\mathbf{-2.190}$ & $+0.006$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{G4's warming is genuine, not a redistribution}: skin and layer-1 soil warm
\emph{as much as} the air and the skin-to-2\,m gradient barely moves ($+0.037$\,K). What
LPJ-GUESS would receive from G4 is coherent.

\textcolor{red}{\textbf{But I1 and I2 cool the deep soil by ${\sim}2.1$\,K}}, I2 doing so with
essentially no surface change at all. The unexplained winter response of the snow scheme
therefore reaches \texttt{stl4}, which \emph{is} a coupled field. That is a second, independent
reason to keep the snow-depletion scheme out of a coupled run until round 16 explains the
winter behaviour.

\emph{This subsection exists because the diagnostic was wrongly believed impossible}: the soil
and skin fields had been written off as dead (\S\ref{sub:r15lw}) on the basis of an
analysis-side scaling bug.

\subsection{I3 (2026--08--04): the strongest summer lever, and the Arctic bill}\label{sub:i3}
I3 $=$ G4 $+$ mode~2, the SDOR-scaled depletion, calibrated to match I1 \emph{over the
Siberian box} so that any remaining difference would be spatial structure
(\S\ref{sub:scffix}). It does not behave like I1, and the differences are the point.

\textbf{What it wins.}

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\toprule
 & control & G4 & I1 & \textbf{I3} & CERES \\
\midrule
Siberian JJA T2m [$^\circ$C] & $9.728$ & $+0.952$ & $+1.250$ & $\mathbf{+1.322}$ ($t{=}10.61$) & --- \\
Siberian surface net SW [W\,m$^{-2}$] & $153.78$ & $+8.10$ & $+12.77$ & $\mathbf{166.58}$ & $\mathbf{166.26}$ \\
global T2m RMSE vs ERA5 [K] & $1.579$ & $-0.036$ & $\mathbf{+0.048}$ & $\mathbf{-0.023}$ & --- \\
global net TOA [W\,m$^{-2}$] & $0.643$ & $-0.128$ & $-0.021$ & $+0.056$ & --- \\
SO SW RMSE & $6.877$ & $-2.076$ & $-1.906$ & $-2.065$ & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent $+1.322$\,K is the \textbf{largest JJA gain of 36 evaluated tuning runs}, and the
Siberian surface SW deficit that \S\ref{sec:albedo} opened this whole thread with
\textbf{closes to within $0.3$\,W\,m$^{-2}$} of CERES. Global TOA does not move, and where I1
\emph{degrades} global T2m RMSE ($+0.048$), I3 improves it. Per season it dominates I1
outright --- more JJA, $0.32$\,K less DJF damage, and I1's significant autumn penalty
($-0.766^*$) collapses to a null:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
$\Delta$ vs control [K] & DJF & MAM & JJA & SON \\
\midrule
threshold (own season) & $\pm0.601$ & $\pm0.394$ & $\pm0.244$ & $\pm0.441$ \\
G4 & $-0.385$ & $-0.360$ & $+0.952^*$ & $+0.185$ \\
I1 & $-2.759^*$ & $-0.306$ & $+1.250^*$ & $-0.766^*$ \\
\textbf{I3} & $\mathbf{-2.439^*}$ & $-0.214$ & $\mathbf{+1.322^*}$ & $\mathbf{-0.105}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textcolor{red}{\textbf{What only the full guardrail set revealed.}} The Siberian-box scripts
alone would have promoted I3. They were run first, and the verdict ``best lever of the
campaign'' was drafted on them --- prematurely, and caught only because the guardrails were
demanded before writing. Four guardrails move together, all one way:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
guardrail & control & I1 & \textbf{I3} & CERES \\
\midrule
net TOA 60--90N [W\,m$^{-2}$] & $-97.74$ & $+0.87$ & $\mathbf{-96.02\ (+1.72)}$ & $\mathbf{-97.98}$ \\
net TOA NH [W\,m$^{-2}$] & $0.280$ & $+0.245$ & $\mathbf{0.952\ (+0.672)}$ & $\mathbf{0.08}$ \\
NH$-$SH albedo & $-0.003$ & $-0.002$ & $\mathbf{-0.007\ (-0.004)}$ & --- \\
Nordic Seas SW RMSE & $9.058$ & $+0.196$ & $\mathbf{9.557\ (+0.499)}$ & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent This is the mechanism \emph{working as designed}: removing high-latitude snow cover
admits more shortwave at high latitude. The Arctic did not have that energy spare. The control
sat almost exactly on the observation at 60--90N ($-97.74$ vs $-97.98$); I3 leaves it $1.96$
away. The NH net TOA change ($+0.672$) and the NH$-$SH albedo change ($-0.004$) are each the
\textbf{largest of all 37 runs}, and Nordic Seas is the \textbf{only significant SW RMSE
degradation in the campaign} --- \texttt{rmse\_significance.py} flags exactly two runs there and
I3 is one ($+0.07$ against a $\pm0.051$ threshold).

\emph{Caveat against these four, stated so it is not over-read}: these are PI runs scored
against present-day CERES, and this report's own rule (\S\ref{sub:evalsh}) is that the PI
target is net TOA ${\approx}0$, \textbf{not} the CERES column. That weakens the
``away from CERES'' reading of the two regional TOA rows. It does \emph{not} weaken the
NH$-$SH albedo or Nordic Seas results, which are model-versus-model and model-versus-RMSE.

\textbf{Why this matters more for the coupled run than for AMIP.} All four are Arctic
energy-\emph{gain} guardrails, and AMIP structurally hides their consequence: prescribed SST
and prescribed sea ice absorb $1.7$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ without responding. In the coupled PI
spin-up --- the configuration I3 is a candidate for --- they would not. This is the same
structural blindness as \S\ref{sub:amplifier}, in the opposite direction.

\subsection{The winter penalty is not radiative}\label{sub:djfnotrad}
\S\ref{sub:r15lw} left the DJF route unidentified. It can now be excluded as radiative.
Siberian land, DJF, all four configurations:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
Siberian DJF & snow albedo & SWE [m] & absorbed SW [W\,m$^{-2}$] \\
\midrule
control & $0.6789$ & $0.6933$ & $8.16$ \\
G4 & $0.6760$ & $0.6901$ & $8.18$ \\
I1 & $0.6800$ & $0.6926$ & $8.16$ \\
I2 & $0.6796$ & $0.6958$ & $8.16$ \\
\textbf{I3} & $0.6797$ & $0.6944$ & $8.22$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Albedo, snowpack and absorbed shortwave are \textbf{unchanged to three decimals},
and there are only ${\sim}8$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ of insolation in the box in midwinter regardless.
\textbf{A $-2.4$\,K penalty cannot be constructed from that budget.} This generalises the
finding that killed the compensating-error story (\S\ref{sub:r15compensate}, winter cover
$0.963$ vs $0.964$) from cover to the whole radiative chain, and it holds for I2, which
carries the penalty with no vegetation change at all. The route must be
\textbf{thermal --- the snow/exposed-tile coupling} --- which is precisely what round 16
(\S\ref{sec:lamsk}) tests, so the diagnosis and the running experiment agree.

\subsection{Direct observations at last: RIHMI-WDC station soil temperature (2026--08--05)}\label{sub:rihmi}
Every soil claim in this report had rested on ERA5, whose land surface \emph{is} HTESSEL ---
the same snow scheme, the same soil thermal conductivity --- so it is a sibling, not a
witness. \textbf{RIHMI-WDC (Sherstyukov v3)} ends that: daily soil temperature at Russian
meteorological stations, 1963--2024, twelve depths from 2 to 320\,cm, measured
\textbf{under natural cover} so the snowpack is intact. 43 of the 110 stations lie in the
campaign box. Model and ERA5 are sampled \emph{at the station points}, not box-averaged, which
is the one representativeness control available without land-cover weighting.

\textbf{What the archive cannot do:} depths 2/5/10/15\,cm carry \emph{no} winter data --- the
Russian network withdraws the shallow Savinov thermometers for the cold season. So
\texttt{stl1} (0--7\,cm), where the damage is largest, \textbf{has no observational
counterpart}; \texttt{stl2} is the shallowest verifiable layer and bounds it. Only
\texttt{tsoil\_qc == 0} is used.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l r r r r r}
\toprule
 & obs depth & OBS & model & bias & OBS offset & model offset \\
\midrule
\texttt{stl2} & 20\,cm & $-6.07$ & $-5.03$ & $\mathbf{+1.04}$ & $\mathbf{+17.78}$ & $+20.11$ \\
\texttt{stl3} & 40/80\,cm & $-3.58$ & $-3.19$ & $\mathbf{+0.39}$ & $\mathbf{+20.28}$ & $+21.95$ \\
\texttt{stl4} & 120--240\,cm & $+0.34$ & $-0.23$ & $\mathbf{-0.57}$ & $\mathbf{+24.18}$ & $+24.85$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Model is \texttt{amip\_presentday} (1990--2014), period-clean. Offset $=$ soil minus
2\,m air, each dataset using its \emph{own} air, which is what makes it insensitive to the
$2.8$\,K air-temperature change the I-series also carries.

\textbf{Two results, \textcolor{red}{both DJF-only}.} In \emph{winter} the control soil is
correct to ${\sim}1$\,K at every verifiable depth --- there was no pre-existing \emph{winter}
soil bias to remove --- and the model is mildly \emph{over}-insulated, by $+0.7$ to $+2.3$\,K
of offset.

\dead{The control soil is correct to ${\sim}1$\,K at every verifiable depth.} \textbf{That was
measured in DJF only and does not hold in summer} (\S\ref{sub:summersoil}): the same control
is $-3.4$ to $-5.4$\,K too cold in JJA, a bias \emph{larger} than the $2.58$\,K Siberian air
bias this campaign has been chasing. The winter statement stands; the unqualified one does not.

\dead{The winter soil-air offset under a deep Siberian snowpack is +10 to +20\,K.} That was
quoted from memory of the permafrost literature and is too low: the station record gives
$+17.8$ to $+24.2$\,K. The correction strengthens rather than weakens the conclusion below.

Scored on offset (the tuning runs are 1872--1915, so absolutes are not period-comparable):

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
 & \texttt{stl1} & \texttt{stl2} & \texttt{stl3} & \texttt{stl4} \\
\midrule
control & $19.87$ & $20.39$ & $22.24$ & $25.13$ \\
G4 & $20.27$ & $20.81$ & $22.72$ & $25.82$ \\
\textbf{I1} & $\mathbf{1.53}$ & $\mathbf{2.61}$ & $\mathbf{6.36}$ & $\mathbf{15.25}$ \\
\textbf{I3} & $1.41$ & $2.54$ & $6.46$ & $15.63$ \\
\midrule
\textbf{OBSERVED} & --- & $\mathbf{+17.78}$ & $\mathbf{+20.28}$ & $\mathbf{+24.18}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent I1 is \textbf{$-15.2$\,K wrong at 20\,cm}, $-13.9$ at 40--80\,cm, $-8.9$ at
1.2--2.4\,m. In raw terms its soil sits at $-25.8\,^\circ$C where stations measure
$-6.1\,^\circ$C. The snowpack has stopped insulating at every depth.

\subsection{The summer soil bias, and why it dwarfs the air bias}\label{sub:summersoil}
The DJF validation above was extended to JJA at the same 43 stations, and it changes the
picture completely.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{3}{c}{DJF} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{JJA}} \\
\texttt{amip\_presentday} & OBS & model & bias & OBS & model & \textbf{bias} \\
\midrule
boreal \texttt{stl2} 20\,cm & $-6.07$ & $-5.03$ & $+1.04$ & $13.68$ & $10.33$ & $\mathbf{-3.35}$ \\
boreal \texttt{stl3} 40/80\,cm & $-3.60$ & $-3.19$ & $+0.42$ & $10.17$ & $5.84$ & $\mathbf{-4.33}$ \\
larch \texttt{stl2} 20\,cm & $-11.05$ & $-7.76$ & $+3.29$ & $12.52$ & $8.33$ & $\mathbf{-4.19}$ \\
larch \texttt{stl3} 40/80\,cm & $-7.80$ & $-5.54$ & $+2.26$ & $7.64$ & $2.29$ & $\mathbf{-5.35}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The soil is $3$--$5$\,K too cold in summer while being slightly too warm in
winter}, and the summer soil bias is \emph{larger than the $2.58$\,K Siberian air bias} the
campaign has spent 45 runs on. It was never looked at because no soil diagnostic was scored
until \S\ref{sub:rihmi}.

\textbf{It is far worse coupled.} LPJ-GUESS's \texttt{soiltemp25cm} is the OIFS soil
temperature received through OASIS, so it measures the same quantity:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r}
\toprule
larch zone, ${\approx}25$\,cm & DJF & \textbf{JJA} \\
\midrule
\textbf{RIHMI obs} (20/40\,cm bracket) & $-11.4$ / $-8.3$ & $\mathbf{+12.5\ /\ +10.0}$ \\
06 Baseline & $-15.96$ & $+1.80$ \\
080a CRUNCEP & $-16.35$ & $+1.38$ \\
09A newSeaIce & $-18.09$ & $+0.72$ \\
\textbf{10A} (G4) & $-21.04$ & $\mathbf{-0.37}$ \\
\textbf{10B} (G4 $+$ snow depletion) & $-20.85$ & $\mathbf{+2.31}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Against an observed ${\approx}+11.8\,^\circ$C at 25\,cm, every coupled run sits at
$+0.7$ to $+2.3\,^\circ$C: a \textbf{${\sim}10$\,K summer soil cold bias}. \textbf{Larch cannot
build cover in soil near freezing all summer}, which makes this a more plausible driver of the
forest collapse than either the evergreen gate or grass competition (\S\ref{sec:tworoutes}) ---
those describe \emph{which} PFT wins, this describes why none of them grow.

\emph{Representativeness caveat, and it is a real one.} RIHMI plots are open, bare or
short-grass sites in full sun; a forested cell's summer soil is legitimately cooler under
canopy shade. Part of AMIP's $-4$\,K is therefore representativeness and cannot be separated
without land-cover weighting. What is \emph{not} representativeness is the ${\sim}6$\,K gap
between AMIP and coupled at the same stations with the same comparison --- that is real
degradation contributed by the coupled vegetation feedback.

\subsection{The snow scheme, reconsidered on coupled evidence (2026--08--05)}\label{sub:scfoff}
\dead{\texttt{ECE\_SNOW\_SCF} is switched off and stays off; no production run should set it.}
\textbf{Retracted the same day.} That verdict was reached on AMIP alone, and \textbf{AMIP was
the wrong testbed for it}: it has no vegetation response, which is precisely the thing the
scheme was helping. On the coupled 10A/10B pair --- identical binaries, identical settings
(\texttt{ECE\_SNOW\_SCF=1}, \texttt{Z0=0.016}), differing \emph{only} in the scheme:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
larch zone & BNS LAI & C3G LAI & grass\,:\,larch & TREEFPC \\
\midrule
09C reference & $0.096$ & $0.400$ & $4.2\times$ & $0.048$ \\
\textbf{10A} (G4) & $\mathbf{0.019}$ & $0.388$ & $\mathbf{20.4\times}$ & $\mathbf{0.019}$ \\
\textbf{10B} (G4 $+$ scheme) & $\mathbf{0.134}$ & $0.830$ & $6.2\times$ & $\mathbf{0.065}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{10B carries $7\times$ the larch and $3.4\times$ the tree cover of 10A}, and
G4 alone (10A) is \emph{worse than the 09C reference} --- it actively degrades the forest. NH
total LAI bias against the Lund CRUNCEP reference halves, $-0.62$ ($-28.1\,\%$) $\to$ $-0.28$
($-12.9\,\%$).

\textbf{And the winter soil damage does not appear coupled.} 10A and 10B are $-21.04$ and
$-20.85$ in DJF --- identical --- while JJA goes $-0.37 \to +2.31$. The ${\sim}5$\,K winter
cooling relative to the 06/070/080a baselines came in with G4 $+$ CERES-init, \emph{not} with
the scheme. And 10A's summer soil is the outlier against every earlier baseline
($+1.4$ to $+3.0$); \textbf{the scheme restores the baseline range rather than departing from
it}, recovering $2.7$\,K of the ${\sim}12$\,K deficit.

\dead{Standing position: the scheme is not rejected. The AMIP soil error is real, but it is
not disqualifying for coupling, where the scheme is the difference between a badly degraded
and a substantially recovered boreal forest.} \textcolor{red}{\textbf{RETRACTED the same day
--- see \S\ref{sub:scfcoupled}.}} The claim that the winter damage does not appear coupled was
an \emph{artefact of where I sampled}: I measured only the larch zone, where 10A is already
saturated near $-21\,^\circ$C, so both runs looked alike. Across the full station network 10B
is $-16.2$\,K in DJF and $-18.3$\,K in January. The AMIP testbed had predicted exactly this.

\textbf{Why.} Verified against \emph{direct observations} --- RIHMI-WDC station soil
temperature, 43 stations in the box, under natural cover (\S\ref{sub:rihmi}) --- the scheme
destroys the winter snow insulation. Observed thermal offset (soil minus 2\,m air) is
$+17.8$\,K at 20\,cm, $+20.3$ at 40--80\,cm, $+24.2$ at 1.2--2.4\,m. The control reproduces
those to within $0.7$--$2.3$\,K. I1 gives $+2.6$, $+6.4$, $+15.3$: an error of $-15$\,K at
20\,cm. The control soil itself is correct to ${\sim}1$\,K at every depth the Russian network
reports in winter, so this is a \textbf{new error, not the removal of an old one}. Since
\texttt{ST1L}--\texttt{ST4L} feed LPJ-GUESS, that alone disqualifies it for coupling.

\textbf{What we still do not know, and it matters.} The mechanism is \emph{unidentified}. The
monthly decomposition rules out the obvious candidates: Siberian snow cover changes by
$-0.02$ in October and SWE not at all, so it is neither a cover nor a mass effect. The soil
\emph{leads} the air (October: soil $-4.19$\,K, air $-0.02$), so it is a surface-coupling
change rather than an atmospheric one. Winter snow density falls $20$--$27$\,kg\,m$^{-3}$,
which with unchanged SWE means a \emph{deeper, less dense} pack --- that insulates
\emph{better}, so density is a consequence of a cold pack, not its cause.
\dead{The tanh formula bites hardest in October when snow is shallow, cutting cover and
exposing the soil.} Measured: cover is essentially unchanged. That explanation was written
into \texttt{soil\_temp\_vs\_obs.py} before it was checked and is wrong.

\textbf{Reactivation therefore needs three things, in order:}
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{Diagnose \texttt{ZCVS}.} It is not in the output stream, which is why the
  mechanism is still open --- every field we do write exonerates itself. Nothing should be
  rebuilt before the snow-tile fraction can actually be seen.
\item \textbf{Accumulation/ablation hysteresis.} Our implementation applies one depletion
  curve year-round. Both Niu \& Yang (2007) and Swenson \& Lawrence (2012) use
  \emph{different} curves for accumulation and ablation, and that omission is the most likely
  structural defect. It would also match the measured timing: the benefit is May--June, the
  damage begins in October, so the two are cleanly separable in season.
\item \textbf{Re-verify on the SOIL OFFSET, not on snow cover.} The scheme was accepted in
  round 15 because it fixed melt-out to within a day of satellite. That was true and it was
  not sufficient. \S\ref{sub:rihmi} is the test any successor must pass.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{And weigh the prize before spending the effort.} I2 --- the scheme alone, no
vegetation levers --- bought \textbf{$+0.094$\,K JJA, inside the noise floor}. The value of
the snow fix is not temperature; it is that the snow field becomes correct, which matters for
LPJ-GUESS growing-season onset. That is a real but narrow benefit, and it does not justify a
source change plus a run family while a $20$\,K soil error remains unexplained.

\medskip
\section{Round 16 (2026--08--04): the snow-tile skin conductivity}\label{sec:lamsk}
\texttt{vlamsk\_mod.F90} hardcoded the snow-tile skin conductivities, so they were untunable.
$\lambda_{sk}$ sets how tightly the skin couples to the medium below it; a small value lets it
radiatively decouple and crash in polar night. Physically
$\lambda_{sk} \approx k_{\mathrm{snow}}/d_{\mathrm{skin}}$ with $k_{\mathrm{snow}}$
$0.1$--$0.4$\,W\,m$^{-1}$\,K$^{-1}$ and $d_{\mathrm{skin}}$ $0.01$--$0.05$\,m, so
\textbf{roughly 2--40}. As released:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r l}
\toprule
constant & value & tile \\
\midrule
\textbf{\texttt{ZSNOW}} & $\mathbf{7}$ & 5, \textbf{exposed snow} --- the weakly coupled one, and the tile covering tundra ($25.6\,\%$ of the box) \\
\texttt{ZSNOW\_GLACIER} & $8$ & 5, where SWE $>9000$\,mm \\
\texttt{ZSNOWHVEG} & $20$ & 7, snow under high vegetation \\
\texttt{ZLARGESN} & $50$ & 5, when melting \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent The as-released $7$ sits at the decoupled end of the physical range, on precisely
the tile whose \emph{fraction} round 15 corrected. Now exposed as
\texttt{ECE\_LAMSK\_SN/\_SNGL/\_SNHV/\_SNMLT}, all defaulting to the shipped values.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\toprule
run & setting & prediction \\
\midrule
\textbf{J1} & I1 $+$ \texttt{ECE\_LAMSK\_SN: 15} & DJF $+1$ to $+3$\,K, JJA barely moves \\
\textbf{J2} & I1 $+$ \texttt{ECE\_LAMSK\_SN: 25} & same, stronger \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{Falsifier on record: if DJF does not respond, skin conductivity is not the
route}, and the winter bias lies in boundary-layer mixing instead (Holtslag et al.\ 2013;
Sandu et al.\ 2013). Also watched: melt-out and May/June cover must \emph{stay} fixed --- drift
back toward the control would mean the two changes interact and the pair is not separable.

\subsection{Round 16 result (2026--08--05): falsified, and the falsifier was on record}\label{sub:r16res}
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
$\Delta$ vs control [K] & DJF & JJA & SON & global T2m RMSE \\
\midrule
I1 (the base) & $-2.759^*$ & $+1.250^*$ & $-0.766^*$ & $1.627$ \\
\textbf{J1} ($\lambda_{sk}\,15$) & $\mathbf{-2.834^*}$ & $+1.313^*$ & $-1.132^*$ & $1.581$ \\
\textbf{J2} ($\lambda_{sk}\,25$) & $\mathbf{-3.226^*}$ & $+1.246^*$ & $-0.879^*$ & $1.597$ \\
\emph{control} & --- & --- & --- & $1.579$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent The prediction was \textbf{DJF $+1$ to $+3$\,K}. Measured: DJF got \textbf{worse},
and \emph{monotonically with the knob} --- $-2.834$ at $\lambda_{sk}{=}15$, $-3.226$ at $25$,
against I1's $-2.759$. Both runs carry a global T2m RMSE \emph{worse than the control}.

\textbf{The pre-registered falsifier fires: skin conductivity is not the winter route.} By
\S\ref{sub:djfnotrad} the route is non-radiative, and $\lambda_{sk}$ was the leading
non-radiative candidate; with it excluded, \textbf{boundary-layer mixing} (Holtslag et al.\
2013; Sandu et al.\ 2013) is what remains. The round is doubly moot in practice, since its
base I1 is now deactivated (\S\ref{sub:scfoff}) --- but the falsification stands on its own
and the diagnosis it leaves behind is the useful part.

\emph{Both J runs were built on I1, before \S\ref{sub:i3} showed I3 to be the better base in
every season.} That was the correct decision with the information available and the round is
still diagnostic --- it tests whether $\lambda_{sk}$ is the winter route at all, and
\S\ref{sub:djfnotrad} shows I1 and I3 share the same non-radiative penalty, so an answer on
I1 transfers. But if $\lambda_{sk}$ does prove to be the route, \textbf{the production pair
must be re-run on I3}, and the Arctic guardrails of \S\ref{sub:i3} re-checked on the
combination rather than assumed to superpose --- superposition has been wrong in sign twice
in this campaign (\S\ref{sub:evalsh}).

\section{Land albedo (2026--08--04): half of it is the snow tile}\label{sec:landalb}
With the boreal budget nearly spent (\S\ref{sub:tilebias}) the target moved to the
${\sim}0.7$\,K \emph{universal} land bias, and \S\ref{sub:vprof} localised its energy to a
clear-sky shortwave deficit --- cloud is the wrong sign. Decomposing that deficit
assumption-free (each dataset divided by its \emph{own} downward flux) gives an all-sky land
surface albedo $+0.0154$ too high against CERES.

\subsection{Snow is a tile, not a vegetation type}\label{sub:snowtile}
The first attribution split that $+0.0154$ by \emph{dominant vegetation type}, built from
\texttt{tvh}/\texttt{cvh}/\texttt{tvl}/\texttt{cvl}. That map has no snow in it: in HTESSEL
snow is a separate tile (5 on low vegetation, 7 under high) that \textbf{overlies} every
vegetation type. So each type was part-time snow, and the ranking the split produced was
largely a map of \emph{snow duration} --- evergreen needleleaf is snow-covered $53.9\,\%$ of
the year, tundra $63.2\,\%$, ``bare soil'' $49.2\,\%$ (a bucket holding the Sahara and
high-Arctic bare ground together). An annual-mean $T_{2m}>5^\circ$C filter does not fix this:
a cell averaging $6^\circ$C still has months of snow.

Masking snow \textbf{per cell per month} (SWE $<1$\,mm, sunlit months only) splits the error
almost exactly in half:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
$\Delta$ albedo vs CERES & \multicolumn{2}{c}{all sunlit months} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{snow-free only} \\
\midrule
ALL LAND & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$\mathbf{+0.0154}$} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$\mathbf{+0.0080}$} \\
\multicolumn{5}{l}{\emph{$\Rightarrow$ snow contributes $+0.0074$, the snow-free surface $+0.0080$}} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent and the per-type ranking \emph{inverts}:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
type & snow-covered & all months & \textbf{snow-free} \\
\midrule
Evgr Needleleaf & $53.9\,\%$ & $+0.0250$ & $\mathbf{+0.0032}$ \\
Bogs/Marshes & $37.1\,\%$ & $+0.0143$ & $+0.0039$ \\
Evgr Shrubs & $41.2\,\%$ & $+0.0150$ & $+0.0058$ \\
Evgr Broadleaf (tropical) & $6.9\,\%$ & $+0.0017$ & $\mathbf{-0.0003}$ \\
\midrule
\textbf{Crops} & $25.9\,\%$ & $+0.0215$ & $\mathbf{+0.0204}$ \\
\textbf{Semidesert} & $22.5\,\%$ & $+0.0342$ & $\mathbf{+0.0169}$ \\
\textbf{bare soil} & $49.2\,\%$ & $+0.0240$ & $\mathbf{+0.0145}$ \\
\textbf{Irrig Crops} & $24.6\,\%$ & $+0.0253$ & $\mathbf{+0.0138}$ \\
\textbf{Tundra} & $63.2\,\%$ & $+0.0064$ & $\mathbf{+0.0105}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{Every forest type falls inside $+0.006$ once snow is removed}, tropical
broadleaf to $-0.0003$. \textbf{This vindicates \S\ref{sub:albreg} and \S\ref{sub:albsnow}}:
\texttt{RVVEGALB}'s high-vegetation entries are sound and the snow season really is the story.
What is \emph{new} is the residual $+0.0080$, which sits on the sparse and cultivated surfaces
where the soil background shows through --- and on tundra, which gets \textbf{worse} under
masking ($+0.0064\to+0.0105$) because snow was hiding a genuine snow-free summer error.
Nothing in 41 runs has touched that half.

\subsection{The snow half already has a lever}\label{sub:snowhalflever}
Land albedo over snow-covered sunlit months, model-internal:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
run & snow-month albedo & vs G4 & all-land \\
\midrule
G4 & $0.5185$ & --- & $0.2586$ \\
I1 $=$ G4 $+$ mode~1 & $0.5011$ & $-0.0174$ & $-0.0024$ \\
I2 $=$ mode~1 alone & $0.4996$ & $-0.0189$ & $-0.0018$ \\
\textbf{I3 $=$ G4 $+$ mode~2} & $\mathbf{0.4689}$ & $\mathbf{-0.0496}$ & $\mathbf{-0.0052}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{I3 removes ${\sim}70\,\%$ of the snow half}, I1 ${\sim}32\,\%$ --- and I3 was
calibrated to match I1 \emph{in the Siberian box}, so globally it is twice as strong as
intended. That is new information about mode~2 and it is the same over-reach that
\S\ref{sub:i3} bills at 60--90N: the SDOR scaling does most of its work outside the box it was
tuned in.

\subsection{Why the two halves cannot simply be stacked}\label{sub:stacking}
The obvious next move --- keep I3 and add a vegetation-albedo correction on top --- is
\textbf{not additive in the direction that matters}. A darker land surface at high latitude
adds absorbed shortwave to precisely the region I3 has already pushed $1.72$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ past
CERES. The two changes compete for one energy budget. Consequences for the plan:

\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The mid-latitude and subtropical entries are the safe ones.} Crops
  ($+0.0204$), semidesert ($+0.0169$), bare soil ($+0.0145$) and irrigated crops
  ($+0.0138$) lie outside the Arctic, so correcting them attacks the ${\sim}0.7$\,K
  \emph{universal} bias without adding to the 60--90N surplus.
\item \textbf{Tundra ($+0.0105$) is the dangerous one.} It is the single entry that
  \emph{would} stack with I3, being boreal and in-season --- which makes it both the most
  attractive for the summer target and the one most likely to break the Arctic guardrails.
\item \textbf{The snow-free half is not reachable by \texttt{RVVEGALB} alone} where bare
  fraction dominates; there the background soil albedo climatology carries it, and that is an
  ancillary field, not a namelist parameter (\S\ref{sub:albbuild}).
\end{itemize}

\emph{Assets}: \texttt{land\_albedo\_snow\_split.py}, chained into \texttt{evaluate.sh --obs}.

\section{Round 17 (2026--08--05): K1 adopted, and land albedo closed out}\label{sec:r17}
Two families on the G4 base, run concurrently and deliberately orthogonal: \textbf{L} puts a
moss/organic layer on the boreal types, \textbf{K} corrects the snow-free land albedo
diagnosed in \S\ref{sec:landalb}.

\subsection{L: the moss proxy is falsified, monotonically the wrong way}\label{sub:r17l}
$\lambda_{sk}$ $10\to2.9$ (L1) and $10\to1.7$ (L2) on types 4 (larch) and 9 (tundra) only ---
the two most boreal-confined types, 62\,\% and 70\,\% of their area poleward of 50N. Gaillard
(2025) attributes most of a ${>}2$\,K high-latitude summer gain to a surface organic layer,
which HTESSEL lacks entirely.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r l}
\toprule
 & JJA & vs G4 & verdict (threshold $\pm0.244$) \\
\midrule
G4 & $+0.952^*$ & --- & the base \\
L1 ($\lambda_{sk}\,2.9$) & $+0.784^*$ & $-0.168$ & inside $\to$ null \\
\textbf{L2} ($\lambda_{sk}\,1.7$) & $+0.638^*$ & $\mathbf{-0.314}$ & \textbf{outside $\to$ significant degradation} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \emph{More} moss is \emph{worse}, the opposite of the prediction, and the monotonic
ordering makes a noise explanation implausible. \textbf{Caveat that limits the claim}:
$\lambda_{sk}$ is only a \emph{proxy} for an organic layer --- it changes the skin-to-substrate
coupling but not the heat capacity, not the moisture retention, and not the summer
evaporative regime. This falsifies \emph{the proxy}, not necessarily the Gaillard mechanism.
A real organic layer would need a soil-column change, which is a much larger piece of work.

\subsection{K: adopted}\label{sub:r17k}
K1 $=$ G4 $+$ \texttt{RVVEGALB} on crops ($\times0.887$), irrigated crops ($\times0.910$) and
semidesert ($\times0.906$) $+$ \texttt{ECE\_TUNE\_SOILALB} $0.95$. K2 is the soil background
\emph{alone}, isolating the ${\sim}25\,\%$ of land that \texttt{RVVEGALB} cannot reach.
\textbf{Tundra was deliberately excluded} so K stayed orthogonal to L and did not add
absorbed shortwave to the Arctic (\S\ref{sub:stacking}).

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
 & Siberian JJA & DJF & net TOA 60--90N & \textbf{global T2m RMSE} \\
\midrule
control & --- & --- & $-97.737$ & $1.579$ \\
G4 & $+0.952^*$ & $-0.385$ & $-97.603$ & $1.543$ \\
I3 & $+1.322^*$ & $-2.439^*$ & $\mathbf{-96.017}$ & $1.556$ \\
\textbf{K1} & $\mathbf{+1.036^*}$ & $-0.496$ & $-97.463$ & $\mathbf{1.524}$ \\
K2 & $+0.913^*$ & $-0.144$ & $-97.525$ & $\mathbf{1.518}$ \\
\emph{CERES} & --- & --- & $-97.98$ & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{Both are clean} --- every season inside its own threshold --- and they are
the \textbf{two best global T2m RMSE of the campaign}. The tundra exclusion did its job: 60--90N
stays near the control, against I3's $+1.72$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ excursion. In the Siberian box
neither K is significant against G4 ($+0.084$, $-0.039$, both inside $\pm0.244$), \emph{which
was predicted in advance} --- K's mass is crops, semidesert and the subtropical desert belt.

\subsection{What K bought, measured on the metric it was designed for}\label{sub:r17budget}
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
global LAND, vs G4 & land T2m & land SW$_{net}$ & land albedo \\
\midrule
\textbf{K1} & $\mathbf{+0.089}$\,K & $+0.77$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ & $-0.0059$ \\
K2 & $+0.076$\,K & $+0.48$ & $-0.0038$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The lever did what it was built to do.} The snow-free residual was
$+0.0080$; K1 removed $-0.0059$ of it, \textbf{74\,\%}. The measured sensitivity is
$0.089/0.77 = \mathbf{0.116}$\,K per W\,m$^{-2}$ against the $0.12$ assumed --- essentially
exact. The geography is right too: land equatorward of 60N warms $+0.109$\,K while land
poleward of 60N \emph{cools} $0.055$\,K, exactly what excluding tundra was meant to achieve.

\dead{Predicted ${\sim}1.1$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ and $0.1$--$0.2$\,K of land warming.} Measured
$0.77$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ and $0.089$\,K --- the SW gain was $70\,\%$ of the prediction and the
temperature at or below its low end. The sensitivity assumption was right; the forcing
estimate was optimistic.

\textcolor{red}{\textbf{THE NEGATIVE RESULT IS WORTH MORE THAN THE $0.089$\,K.}} Three quarters
of a \emph{directly measured} land albedo bias has now been removed, and it recovered
$0.089$\,K of the ${\sim}0.7$\,K universal bias. Extrapolating, a \emph{perfect} albedo fix
buys ${\sim}0.12$\,K --- about \textbf{one sixth} of the target. \textbf{Land surface albedo is
therefore closed out as the explanation for the universal cold bias}, which is a real
constraint on where the remaining ${\sim}0.6$\,K lives.

\dead{K3 should push the soil-albedo scale further, since K2 alone gives the best global RMSE.}
\textbf{Retracted before it was run.} The remaining $26\,\%$ of the residual is worth roughly
$0.03$\,K, and the $0.95$ scale was calibrated to the \emph{measured} $-0.0163$ bias --- pushing
past it means tuning \emph{against} the observation, which is the same overfitting-to-present-day
trap that ruled out editing the soil ancillary (\S\ref{sub:stacking}). \textbf{K1 is adopted and
albedo tuning stops here.}

\section{The boreal forest fails by TWO different routes (2026--08--05)}\label{sec:tworoutes}
The report has carried a single story for the missing forest: the winter evergreen cold gate
(\S\ref{sub:levervsera5}). Evaluating the \texttt{Tuning\_test\_07*}/\texttt{08*} LPJ-GUESS
families (\texttt{lai.out}, \texttt{fpc.out}, \texttt{est\_limits.out}) shows that story is
\textbf{correct but only half the picture}, and that it does not apply at all to the region
that matters most --- NE Siberia, which is ${\sim}80\,\%$ larch.

\subsection{BNE is gate-excluded; BNS is permitted and outcompeted}\label{sub:tworoutes}
Larch zone, 60--72\textdegree N $\times$ 100--160\textdegree E, 2520 cells, last decade:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r}
\toprule
 & 080a (CRUNCEP base) & 09A (newSeaIce) \\
\midrule
\texttt{mTmin20} & $-38.94\,^\circ$C & $-41.89\,^\circ$C \\
\textbf{BNE} gate \texttt{tcmin\_est}$=-30$ & \textbf{0.7\,\% clear} & \textbf{0.0\,\% clear} \\
\textbf{BNS} cold gate & \textbf{none, 100\,\%} & \textbf{none, 100\,\%} \\
\texttt{agdd5} vs BNS \texttt{gdd5min\_est}$=350$ & \textbf{83.6\,\% clear} & $79.0\,\%$ \\
\midrule
BNE LAI & $\mathbf{0.000}$ & $\mathbf{0.000}$ \\
BNS LAI & $0.129$ & $0.103$ \\
C3G LAI & $0.751$ & $0.510$ \\
\textbf{grass\,:\,larch} & $\mathbf{5.8\times}$ & $5.0\times$ \\
cells with C3G $>$ BNS & $65\,\%$ & $60\,\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The two PFTs fail for completely different reasons.}

\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{BNE/BINE: a climate gate.} $0.7\,\%$ of larch-zone cells clear
  \texttt{tcmin\_est}$=-30$, and their LAI is \emph{exactly} $0.000$. The winter-cold story of
  \S\ref{sub:levervsera5} is right, and this is where it applies --- the evergreen zone of
  west Eurasia and North America, \emph{not} NE Siberia.
\item \textbf{BNS (larch): no gate at all, and it loses.} It has no cold limit and clears its
  \texttt{gdd5min\_est}$=350$ on $84\,\%$ of cells, so it is \textbf{climatically permitted}
