  across the zone --- yet grass carries $5$--$6\times$ its LAI, grass exceeds larch on
  $65\,\%$ of cells, and larch is absent outright from half the cells where it is allowed.
  \textbf{Larch is not excluded; it is outcompeted in the growing season.}
\end{itemize}

\textbf{This is a correction to this report, not a new hypothesis.} \S\ref{sub:levervsera5}
already states that BNS has no cold gate and is ``\emph{already permitted}'' --- and then never
explains why it is absent anyway. A reader was left applying the winter-gate mechanism to a
zone where it explains nothing, with only the roughness leg (\S\ref{sub:roughness}) for the
$80\,\%$-larch region.

\subsection{Reconciling this with the falsified ``competition, not climate''}\label{sub:compreconcile}
\S\ref{sec:falsified} carries \emph{``the boreal failure is an LPJG-internal competition
error''} as dead, killed by the forcing-transfer test reproducing most of the loss with
byte-identical parameters. \textbf{That falsification stands and is not weakened here}, because
it and the result above are different claims:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\toprule
claim & status & \\
\midrule
competition is an LPJG \emph{parameter error} & \textbf{dead} & forcing reproduces the loss unchanged \\
competition is the \emph{pathway} by which climate removes larch & \textbf{supported} & permitted on 84\,\%, loses 5.8:1 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Forcing is the \emph{driver}; grass-versus-larch is the \emph{mechanism}. Both hold
at once. As written, the report recorded only the falsification, so a reader would reasonably
conclude that growing-season competition had been ruled out --- it had not been tested.

\emph{No contradiction with \S\ref{sub:seasonality} either}, though the two look opposed. That
section measures vegetation$\to$climate (the LPJG cold effect on T2m, which vanishes in
July--August). This measures climate$\to$vegetation (how the growing season sets larch cover).
Opposite directions of the same coupling; both can be true.

\textbf{Weak evidence, flagged as such.} Weakening grass (\texttt{C3G pstemp\_low}$=12$, run
08G) moves BNS $0.129\to0.156$ and TREEFPC $0.060\to0.072$ while C3G falls $0.751\to0.583$ ---
directionally exactly what competition predicts. But the 08 runs sit on \emph{different
atmospheric bases} (06T vs 06V), so this is not a clean attribution and should not be quoted as
one. The gate/permission evidence above is the load-bearing part; the knob evidence is
suggestive only. On the old forcing, \textbf{07A (BNS \texttt{greff\_min} relaxed) did not
recover BNS at all} ($0.024\to0.021$), which is why a forcing-consistent re-spin remains the
precondition for any competition tuning (\S\ref{sub:xfer}).

\section{The snow scheme, settled on coupled soil (2026--08--05)}\label{sub:scfcoupled}
A colleague's figure --- OpenIFS \texttt{stl2} against RIHMI-WDC at 105 stations, coupled
gen-10 runs --- overturned \S\ref{sub:scfoff}. Reproduced independently to within rounding:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\toprule
stl2 $-$ RIHMI 20\,cm [K] & DJF & MAM & JJA & SON & annual \\
\midrule
AMIP K1 (adopted, tuned) & $+0.49$ & $-1.60$ & $-1.44$ & $-1.30$ & $-0.96$ \\
COUPLED 10A (G4) & $-4.33$ & $\mathbf{-6.57}$ & $-3.62$ & $-3.61$ & $-4.53$ \\
\textbf{COUPLED 10B} ($+$scheme) & $\mathbf{-16.19}$ & $-6.04$ & $\mathbf{-2.44}$ & $-5.95$ & $-7.66$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent January reaches $-18.3$\,K. The scheme buys $1.2$\,K of summer and pays $12$\,K of
winter. \textbf{It is rejected for coupling.}

\textbf{Why the earlier verdict was wrong, recorded because the error is repeatable.} I
sampled the larch zone alone (60--72\textdegree N, 100--160\textdegree E), where 10A already
sits near $-21\,^\circ$C in DJF --- \emph{saturated}. Both runs looked identical there. The
damage is only visible across the wider, more southerly station network where 10A still
retains its snow insulation. \textbf{I measured in the one place the signal was flat.}
Worse, the AMIP run had already predicted it: the I1 delta is DJF $-13.57$\,K, composing to
${\approx}-13.3$ against the $-16.2$ measured. When the coupled LAI figure appeared I treated
it as overturning the AMIP evidence instead of asking why the two disagreed.

\subsection{Reconstructing \texttt{ZCVS}, and what it excludes}\label{sub:zcvsrecon}
\texttt{ZCVS} is not an output field, and neither are the tile fractions
($\texttt{ZCVS}=\texttt{FRTI}(5)+\texttt{FRTI}(7)$ exactly, from
\texttt{surfbc\_ctl\_mod.F90}:421,423, since the three cover fractions sum to one). But it is
a pure function of \texttt{sd} and \texttt{rsn}, both of which \emph{are} written, so it can
be reconstructed offline from each run's own snowpack:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
Siberian land & Sep & \textbf{Oct} & Nov & Dec & Jan & May \\
\midrule
$\Delta$\texttt{ZCVS} & $+0.026$ & $\mathbf{-0.075}$ & $-0.008$ & $-0.001$ & $-0.000$ & $-0.188$ \\
$\Delta$soil [K] & $+0.0$ & $\mathbf{-4.2}$ & $-16.2$ & $-22.2$ & $-23.8$ & $+2.4$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{Midwinter cover is identical} ($-0.0004$ in January): at $0.43$\,m depth
the tanh is saturated. So the $-19$\,K January soil \textbf{cannot be a cover effect}. The
cover difference \emph{peaks in October exactly as the soil begins to diverge}, then vanishes
while the soil runs on to $-24$\,K --- autumn seeding plus thermal memory, with nothing in
midwinter needing to differ at all.

\textbf{A mechanism proposed and falsified within the hour.} \dead{The snow fraction is
applied twice on the conduction path: \texttt{ZHFLUX} is already area-weighted while
\texttt{ZSNCONDH} is multiplied by \texttt{PFRSN} again, so the pack drains through a
conductance scaled by area a second time.} \textbf{Wrong.} \texttt{ZHFLUX} is W per m$^2$ of
\emph{grid box} (which is why the \texttt{/ZFRSN} at
\texttt{srfsn\_driver\_mod.F90}:271 is commented out), \texttt{ZDSN} uses \emph{grid-mean}
SWE, and \texttt{ZSNCONDH} is therefore also per m$^2$ of grid box. Flux, conductance and heat
capacity are all in the same units. There is no second division for the factor to pair with.

\emph{Supporting evidence for the surviving reading}: the snowpack cools only $3.9$\,K and the
air $2.6$\,K while the soil cools $23.8$\,K. In January the control holds a $+21.7$\,K
gradient between snow surface and soil; I1 holds $+1.8$\,K. The insulation has gone, with SWE
identical and density \emph{lower} --- deeper, less conductive snow. No physical field changed
in a way that could do it, which is what pointed at autumn seeding rather than a winter
process.

\subsection{Rounds 19: what is running, and what would falsify it}\label{sub:r19}
Four AMIP runs on the K1 base at \textbf{full campaign length} (1870--1916, evaluate
1872--1915), with daily \texttt{sd}/\texttt{rsn}/\texttt{tsn}/\texttt{asn}/\texttt{stl1}/
\texttt{stl2} added through a per-run \texttt{add\_config\_sources} override --- so all four
difference directly \emph{and} are scorable against the standard thresholds. An earlier
10\,yr version was cancelled: at that length they could not be judged against the 44-yr
detection floors, which would have made them incomparable with the other 43 runs.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l r r r r r}
\toprule
run & role & $z_0$ & $\rho_{new}$ & $m$ & SCF Oct & SCF May \\
\midrule
N1 & reference, scheme off & --- & --- & --- & --- & --- \\
N2 & current parameters & $0.016$ & $100$ & $1.6$ & $0.894$ & $0.817$ \\
\textbf{O1} & re-parameterised & $0.018$ & $170$ & $\mathbf{4.0}$ & $\mathbf{0.995}$ & $\mathbf{0.495}$ \\
O2 & re-parameterised, milder & $0.018$ & $170$ & $3.0$ & $0.995$ & $0.764$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The O series rests on one piece of arithmetic.} With
$\mathrm{SCF}=\tanh(d/L)$ and $L=2.5z_0(\rho/\rho_{new})^m$,
\[
\frac{x_{Oct}}{x_{May}}=\frac{d_{Oct}}{d_{May}}\left(\frac{\rho_{May}}{\rho_{Oct}}\right)^{m}
= 0.469\times1.853^{\,m}.
\]
Depth works \emph{against} us --- October's pack is half as deep. Density works for us, and
$m>1.23$ is needed merely for October to hold more cover than May. \textbf{$m=1.6$ clears that
bar but gives a ratio of only $1.23$}, hence a separation of $0.077$: the $-0.075$ October
deficit that seeds the winter collapse buys a spring depletion only $0.08$ deeper. $m=4$ gives
$5.5$. Raising $m$ alone makes October \emph{worse} ($0.383$), because $\rho_{Oct}=170$ already
exceeds $\rho_{new}=100$ --- so $\rho_{new}$ must be recentred on autumn density first.

\dead{Density cannot substitute for melt state; the two regimes are inseparable by density at
their respective depths.} \textbf{Wrong, corrected the same day}: that was argued from the
current parameters rather than from what the formula can do.

\textbf{Why this replaced the hysteresis it was meant to be.} A ripe/not-ripe state test is
\emph{not} hysteresis --- it is reversible, so cover would flip back every night and every cold
snap, injecting a spurious diurnal oscillation into the tile fractions. Real melt-out is
irreversible within a season. Swenson \& Lawrence (2012) avoid this with a prognostic seasonal
$\mathrm{SWE}_{max}$, which is a new surface prognostic. \textbf{Density sidesteps the problem
entirely}: it is a genuine physical memory variable, ripening monotonically and not walking
backwards. And it is namelist-only.

\textbf{Pre-registered falsifiers.} (i) Winter soil must return to the N1 reference; if O1
still shows the $-16$\,K DJF collapse the density route is dead and only the
$\mathrm{SWE}_{max}$ rewrite remains. (ii) May/June cover must still deplete, or the summer
gain is lost and O1 is all cost. \emph{And this is curve-fitting until the daily data supports
it}: $m=4$ is far from Niu \& Yang's calibrated $1.6$ and was derived from two box-mean
monthly numbers, while a steep exponent amplifies cell-to-cell spread.

\section{Round 19 results and Round 20 (2026--08--06): the \texttt{tanh} is falsified on
observations, and the curve is rebuilt}\label{sec:r20}
The O series failed its own pre-registered falsifier, and so did every mechanism proposed to
explain the failure. Three hypotheses died in sequence before the real one was found, and
\emph{two of the deaths were caused by my own analysis errors} --- both recorded in
\S\ref{sub:r20errors} because both are easy to repeat.

\subsection{The measurement: cover is identical, the soil is 23\,K apart}\label{sub:r20meas}
All four runs at 46\,yr on the K1 base, Siberian box, differenced against N1:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\toprule
 & $\Delta$cover DJF & $\Delta$SWE DJF & $\Delta$depth DJF & \textbf{$\Delta$soil DJF} & Jan $f_{\text{full}}$ \\
\midrule
N1 reference & --- & --- & --- & --- & $\mathbf{0.960}$ \\
N2 current $m{=}1.6$ & $-0.002$ & $+0.65$ & $+0.064$ & $\mathbf{-22.77}$ & $\mathbf{0.773}$ \\
O1 $m{=}4$ & $+0.001$ & $-0.60$ & $+0.047$ & $-18.72$ & $0.947$ \\
O2 $m{=}3$ & $+0.001$ & $+0.12$ & $+0.043$ & $-16.93$ & $0.958$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent\textbf{DJF snow cover is identical to within $0.002$ across all four runs while the
soil differs by 23\,K.} October soil damage is $-4.94/-5.02/-4.95$\,K --- identical to
$0.08$\,K --- while October cover spans $0.10$. Mean cover is not the winter channel, and no
re-parameterisation of a cover curve could ever have been.

\subsection{The mechanism: same mean, opposite state}\label{sub:r20mech}
The exposed area is $1-\overline{\mathrm{cover}}$ by definition, so it is \emph{identical}
between the schemes. Where it sits is not, and the soil response is nonlinear in it.

\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.99\textwidth]{plots/snow_depletion_mechanism.png}
\caption{\textbf{(a)} January snow cover across the Siberian box. N1 and N2 have the same
box-mean cover to within $0.002$ and the same total exposed area, but N1 (clipping ramp) puts
$93.5\%$ of the box at cover \emph{exactly} $1.0$ while N2 (\texttt{tanh}) dumps a quarter of
it into $0.99$--$0.999$. A sliver of bare ground in \emph{every} cell instead of a few
genuinely bare cells: each sliver couples that cell's soil to the air through exposed tiles at
$\lambda_{sk}=10$\,W\,m$^{-2}$\,K$^{-1}$, about $16\times$ stiffer per unit area than the whole
snow column ($0.61$), so no soil is left insulated. \textbf{(b)} The depletion curve against
36\,492 RIHMI snow-course surveys. \texttt{tanh} has range $(0,1)$ \emph{open at 1} and so
cannot represent complete cover at any parameters; the as-released ramp reaches 1 but ignores
density and so has no spring depletion; the fitted form does both.}
\label{fig:depletion}
\end{figure}

\noindent The signature in the model is unmistakable: \textbf{N1 holds a 22\,K gradient from
soil ($265.0$\,K) to snowpack ($242.7$\,K); N2 holds 2\,K ($240.4/238.6$)}. A 2\,K gradient
under half a metre of snow cannot be produced by conduction --- N2's soil sits at air
temperature under a deep pack. \texttt{RTHRFRTI}$\,=\,$\texttt{EPSILON}
(\texttt{su0phy.F90:1308}), so no tile is ever culled and every sliver is fully active.

$f_{\text{full}}$, the area fraction at cover $\ge0.999$, is the only quantity found that
predicts the damage: per-cell correlation with $\Delta$soil is $+0.75/+0.52/+0.52$ against
$+0.32/+0.19/+0.10$ for mean cover.

\subsection{RIHMI-WDC settles it: the as-released cover was right}\label{sub:r20rihmi}
Snow-course transects (\texttt{snmar}, 1--2\,km, the direct observational analogue of
\texttt{ZCVS}): \textbf{14\,331 of 14\,369 Siberian DJF surveys report cover of exactly
$10/10$ --- $99.74\%$}, mean $0.9995$. Complete cover is real, not an artefact of clipping.
Soil temperature (\texttt{tpg}, 43 stations, 174\,676 DJF observations at $0.2$\,m against
\texttt{stl2}):

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
 & $f_{\text{full}}$ DJF & soil at $0.2$\,m & \textbf{bias} \\
\midrule
\textbf{observed} & $\mathbf{0.997}$ & $\mathbf{-6.1}$\,\textdegree C & --- \\
N1, scheme off & $0.990$ & $-5.3$ & $\mathbf{+0.8}$ \\
N2, \texttt{tanh} & $\mathbf{0.678}$ & $-26.3$ & $\mathbf{-20.2}$ \\
O1 & $0.932$ & $-22.2$ & $-16.1$ \\
O2 & $0.965$ & $-20.8$ & $-14.7$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Field January $f(\text{complete cover})$: \textbf{observed $0.996$, \texttt{tanh}
$0.290$}. Period caveat: observations are 1963--2024 against a PI model, so the PI-equivalent
observation is nearer $-9$\,\textdegree C, making N1 $\sim+3$\,K warm --- the scheme is still
$-13$ to $-18$\,K wrong. \textbf{This retracts \S\ref{sub:r15compensate}}: for soil temperature
the as-released cover was correct and the scheme replaced a good field with a broken one.

\subsection{Two analysis errors, and four dead mechanisms}\label{sub:r20errors}
\dead{The soil collapse is seeded by an October cover deficit and carried by thermal memory.}
Killed by its own pre-registered test: O1/O2 produced \emph{positive} autumn cover anomalies
and still lost 18--20\,K.

\dead{A melt-state (ripe/not-ripe) gate can separate spring from autumn.} May ripe-day fraction
is $0.04$ in every run; the gate would fire in June, after melt-out.

\dead{The residual exposed fraction at saturation explains it --- \texttt{tanh} never reaches
1 while the ramp clips to exactly 1.} Right about the codomain, wrong about the box: N1's own
January cover is $0.963$, not $1.0$, so both schemes leave the \emph{same} exposed area. The
distribution, not the total, is what differs.

\dead{Drying the soil before freeze-up removes the latent-heat buffer.} The freeze-up buffer
differs by only $-0.51/-0.72/-0.68$\,K against 17--25\,K to explain, and is not ordered with
the damage --- O1 has the largest water deficit and not the largest cooling.

\dead{More cover means a colder soil, because $\mathtt{ZSNCONDH}\propto\mathtt{PFRSN}$.}
$\mathrm{d}G/\mathrm{d}f = 0.61-10\approx-9.4$: the exposed route swamps the snow route, so
more cover means a \emph{warmer} soil. The blanket intuition holds; the exposed fraction is
what matters.

\noindent\textbf{Both of my errors were the same error: averaging before applying a nonlinear
function.} \texttt{snow\_daily\_diag.py} averaged depth and density over 40 years and
\emph{then} applied the \texttt{tanh}; its $\Delta$cover numbers are void and the conclusions
drawn from them were withdrawn. The first monthly fit check evaluated
$\mathrm{SCF}(\overline{d},\overline{\rho})$ against $\overline{\mathrm{cover}}$, which made a
good fit look like it saturated in May. \textbf{$\mathrm{SCF}(\overline{x})\neq
\overline{\mathrm{SCF}(x)}$ --- apply the formula per sample, always.}

\subsection{Round 20: \texttt{ECE\_SNOW\_SCF} $=3$, fitted to observations}\label{sub:r20fit}
A source change this time (\texttt{surfbc\_ctl\_mod.F90} branch, \texttt{surfece.F90}
parameters); modes 1/2 retained so N/O stay reproducible.
\[
\mathrm{SCF} = (1-c_{vh})\min\!\left(1,(d/d_{cl})^{b_l}\right)
             + c_{vh}\min\!\left(1,(d/d_{ch})^{b_h}\right),\qquad
d_c = \min\!\left(D_{max},\ S\cdot D\cdot(\rho/\rho_{ref})^{M}\right)
\]
$\min(1,\cdot)$ \textbf{reaches 1 exactly} --- that is the entire fix; everything else is
calibration. It nests the as-released ramp at $D=0.1$, $M=0$, $b=1$. Split by vegetation
\textbf{type and fraction only} (via \texttt{PCVH}), never gridpoint or hemisphere, so it stays
portable to other climate states and follows the vegetation if LPJ-GUESS moves the treeline.
\textbf{No new prognostic and no new input}: \texttt{PSNM1M}, \texttt{PRSNM1M} and
\texttt{PCVH} are already arguments of \texttt{SURFBC\_CTL}, and \textbf{nothing knows the
date} --- the seasonal cycle is an output of the snow physics, not an input to the formula.

Parameters are fitted to 36\,492 snow-course surveys, each measuring depth, density and covered
fraction on the same transect, split by course type (1 = \emph{pole}/field $\to$ low
vegetation, 2 = \emph{les}/forest $\to$ high). Constrained fit: hold Oct--Feb saturated, then
minimise Mar--May error.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r r r}
\toprule
 & Oct & Nov & Dec & Jan & Feb & Mar & Apr & \textbf{May} \\
\midrule
field observed & $0.998$ & $0.999$ & $0.999$ & $0.999$ & $0.999$ & $0.994$ & $0.974$ & $\mathbf{0.914}$ \\
field fit & $0.999$ & $1.000$ & $0.998$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $0.996$ & $0.970$ & $\mathbf{0.915}$ \\
forest observed & $0.999$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $0.987$ & $\mathbf{0.931}$ \\
forest fit & $0.999$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $1.000$ & $0.987$ & $\mathbf{0.931}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Mar--May RMSE $0.0025/0.0001$ against $0.0229/0.0156$ (as-released) and
$0.1207/0.0964$ (\texttt{tanh}). \textbf{The frontier is flat} --- the same parameters win at
every winter floor from $0.000$ to $0.998$, so the winter/spring trade-off that dominated
rounds 15--19 was an artefact of the \texttt{tanh} being unable to do either, not a real
tension. Kept: $D_{cl}=0.014$, $D_{ch}=0.026$\,m at $\rho_{ref}=200$, $M=4.70$, $b_l=1.46$,
$b_h=0.40$, $D_{max}=0.30$.

The physical reading is that $d_c$ runs from ${\sim}2$\,mm at $\rho=130$ to 8--14\,cm at
$\rho=285$: fresh low-density snow drapes over everything, while old snow is dense
\emph{because} it has been wind-redistributed and melt-metamorphosed, and that same history is
what leaves bare patches. \textbf{Density is the proxy for age and melt state} --- the
hysteresis the campaign spent two rounds trying to build, delivered by a state variable.

\subsection{The runs, and what would falsify them}\label{sub:r20runs}
P1 (\texttt{amip\_P1\_scffit}, $S=1$) takes the observations literally. P2
(\texttt{amip\_P2\_scffit\_x3}, $S=3$) scales $d_c$ for 100\,km sub-grid variance. Both on the
K1 base at 46\,yr with the daily snow/soil output retained so $f_{\text{full}}$ can be measured
directly.

\textbf{$S$ is the one uncalibrated knob and is labelled as such.} A snow course is 1--2\,km; a
TCO95 box is ${\sim}100$\,km, and sub-grid variance grows with scale, so course-fitted $d_c$ is
a \emph{lower bound} and $S>1$ is the physically expected direction. At the Siberian box-mean
May state ($d=0.31$\,m, $\rho=313$) $S=1$ gives $d_c=0.118$\,m, i.e.\ $d/d_c=2.6$ and
\textbf{no depletion at all} --- P1 is expected to behave much like as-released in spring.
Rutgers/IMS 24\,km cover aggregated to the model grid is what should set $S$, and
\textbf{that calibration is not done}; P2 is an exploratory bracket, not a candidate for
adoption.

\textbf{Pre-registered falsifiers.} (i) The DJF soil must return to the N1/K1 reference; if it
does not, the distribution diagnosis is wrong and this whole section fails. (ii) January
$f_{\text{full}}$ must return to ${\sim}0.96$ from N2's $0.773$. (iii) If the scaled variant
recovers the JJA gain \emph{but} reopens the winter soil bias, then spring depletion and winter
insulation are genuinely coupled at grid scale and the flat frontier found on station data does
not survive.

\subsection{P1 crashed: the fit extrapolated where it had no data (2026--08--06)}\label{sub:r20crash}
P1 aborted at 1888--03--22 with \texttt{ABOR1: Very snow cold temperature}
(\texttt{srfsn\_webal\_mod.F90:451}, the $\mathrm{PTSN}<100$\,K sanity guard):
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\texttt{Tsn 91.93613 \quad SWE-1 5.6058891E-02 \quad SWE 0.0 \quad Snow frac 1.000000 \quad heat 10.88281}
\end{center}
\textbf{A pack of $0.056$\,kg\,m$^{-2}$ --- half a millimetre --- was given \emph{full} snow
cover.} The snow tile then absorbed the entire grid-box flux into essentially zero heat
capacity and the snow temperature collapsed. The cause is that the fitted power law was
extrapolated below any depth the snow courses ever sampled: there is no survey under
${\sim}5$\,cm, and at $\rho=100$ the formula gives $d_c = 0.014(0.5)^{4.7} = 5.4\times10^{-4}$\,m,
so a $0.56$\,mm dusting reaches $d/d_c = 1.04$ and saturates. The governing ratio is
$\mathrm{SCF}/\mathrm{SWE}$: as-released gives $0.100$ at that state, mode 3 unfloored gives
$17.86$ --- \textbf{180$\times$ worse}. I had written down that the low-depth end was
unconstrained and then extrapolated into it with no guard.

\textbf{The fix} is \texttt{ECE\_SNOW\_SCF\_SWEMIN}, which floors $d_c$ at
$\mathrm{SWEMIN}/\rho$: a minimum snow \emph{mass} before cover may saturate, which is the
right currency because the abort is a heat-capacity failure, not a depth one.
$\mathrm{SWEMIN}=3$\,kg\,m$^{-2}$ is the \emph{smallest} value that beats the as-released
margin ($\mathrm{SCF}/\mathrm{SWE}=0.053$ against $0.100$) while barely touching the
calibration --- October field cover $0.999\to0.996$ against an observed $0.998$, and Dec--May
unchanged to three decimals. $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=5$, which I had picked by guess first, would have
cost $0.983$ in October for no extra safety that matters.

\textbf{P2 was cancelled un-crashed and is also withdrawn}: $\mathrm{SCALE}=3$ only makes $d_c$
$1.6$\,mm instead of $0.5$\,mm, so it carries the same defect and its output is
\textcolor{red}{TAINTED} wherever thin snow occurs, which includes every autumn onset and
spring melt-out. \textbf{P3} ($\mathrm{SCALE}=1$) and \textbf{P4} ($\mathrm{SCALE}=3$), both
with $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=3$, supersede them.

\dead{P1 was stopped by an unexplained \texttt{scancel} from the account UID rather than by a
model failure.} Wrong, and the reasoning behind it was worse than the conclusion: the model
aborted normally on \texttt{PROC=48} and esm\_tools tore the job down afterwards, exactly as
designed. I searched \texttt{NODE.001\_01} (rank 1 only) for \texttt{forrtl}/\texttt{nan}/
\texttt{abort} and for the esm\_tools trigger strings, none of which match \texttt{ABOR1} or
\texttt{MPL\_ABORT}, concluded ``no model error'', and then constructed an elaborate
infrastructure story on top of that gap. \textbf{Method note: an OpenIFS abort is
\texttt{ABOR1}/\texttt{MPL\_ABORT} and can land on any rank --- grep the whole compute log for
those tokens before concluding a job died externally.}

\textbf{One tension, found while making Figure~\ref{fig:depletion} and not resolved.} The
constrained fit optimises the \emph{monthly climatology}, but the model applies the curve
\emph{pointwise}. Scored on the pointwise $(d,\rho)$ bin surface instead, the adopted field
parameters give RMSE $0.0624$ against $0.0384$ for a fit made directly to that surface
($b\approx0.1$) --- though both beat as-released ($0.0771$). For forest the adopted parameters
are better on \emph{both} metrics ($0.0420$ vs $0.0483$; as-released $0.0644$). So the field
curve is a compromise: optimal on the seasonal cycle, second-best on the surface, and an
improvement on the incumbent either way. Whether the climatology or the surface is the right
objective is not obvious --- the model integrates the curve over a cell distribution, which is
closer to the climatology aggregation --- and it should be settled alongside the Rutgers
calibration of $S$.

\textbf{Residual hysteresis is real but small.} At matched $(d,\rho)$ the ablation season is
lower in \emph{24 of 24} non-zero bins --- systematic, $p\approx10^{-4}$ --- but the weighted
mean is only $-0.005$ (field) and $-0.001$ (forest). Capturing it would mean plumbing
\texttt{PWSNM1M} through the caller chain for ${\sim}0.005$ of cover; not done. The matched
test can only compare bins populated in both seasons, so spring's extreme states ($\rho>300$)
are structurally untestable this way.

autumn}\label{sec:r20res}
Both pre-registered falsifiers passed, and by more than they required.

N1, scheme off & --- & $0.961$ & $22.4$\,K \\
N2, \texttt{tanh} & $\mathbf{-22.71}$\,K & $0.755$ & $\mathbf{1.8}$\,K (impossible) \\
\textbf{P3} & $\mathbf{+0.30}$\,K & $\mathbf{0.965}$ & $\mathbf{22.9}$\,K \\
P4 & $+0.16$\,K & $0.965$ & $21.8$\,K \\

\includegraphics[width=0.99\textwidth]{plots/amip_stl2_vs_RIHMI_20cm_seasonal_cycle_N1_N2_P3_P4.png}

The \texttt{tanh} run (green) peels away from October and reaches $-15$\,\textdegree C in

\noindent Seasonal ANOVA against the control, each season on its own threshold: P3
$-0.584$ / $+1.341^*$ and P4 $-0.132$ / $+1.092^*$ (DJF / JJA). \textbf{Neither appears on
\texttt{evaluate.sh}'s ``warms JJA significantly but cools DJF significantly'' list}, which
contains I1, I3, J1, J2, N2, O1 and O2. The winter penalty that has followed this scheme
since round 15 is gone.

\textbf{Marginal over the K1 base}, which is the honest accounting: P3 $+0.305$ JJA /
$-0.088$ DJF; P4 $+0.056$ / $+0.364$. Most of the $+1.3$ is K1's. P3 beats P4 despite
$\mathrm{SCALE}=1$ being the conservative choice, which \emph{falsifies my own prediction}
that P3 would show no spring depletion --- cutting cover \emph{slows} box-mean melt,
because the snow tile receives energy in proportion to its area.

\dead{SCALE is the parameter that controls the spring depletion, and the uncalibrated one
that matters.} Varying it $1\to4$ moves the offline Sep--May RMSE by $0.002$. P4 was a
wasted run; \texttt{SWEMIN} is the whole lever.

\subsection{Rutgers: the fix made the autumn 2.4$\times$ worse}\label{sub:r20rutgers}
Rutgers 24\,km, Siberian box, area-weighted, model minus observed:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
 & Sep & Oct & Jan & Apr & May & Jun \\
\midrule
observed (absolute) & $0.067$ & $0.599$ & $0.999$ & $0.956$ & $0.655$ & $0.202$ \\
N1 as-released & $+0.107$ & $+0.103$ & $-0.035$ & $-0.012$ & $+0.118$ & $-0.011$ \\
\textbf{P3} & $\mathbf{+0.259}$ & $\mathbf{+0.249}$ & $-0.034$ & $-0.019$ & $+0.074$ & $-0.035$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent The fitted $d_c$ for fresh low-density snow is ${\sim}2$\,mm, so a dusting
saturates. That is \emph{correct} at 1--2\,km, which is what the snow courses measure, and
wrong at grid scale, where September Siberia is 7\,\% covered rather than 33\,\%. The
uniform $-0.035$ in midwinter is the model's permanent lake/water bare fraction, present in
every run including scheme-off --- not the scheme.

An offline \texttt{SWEMIN} sweep scored over the whole Sep--May season gives an
\emph{interior} optimum at $30$ (RMSE $0.045$ against P3's $0.139$); past it the September
gain is paid for by November and December going deficient. \textbf{P5}
($\mathrm{SWEMIN}=15$) and \textbf{P6} ($=30$) bracket the albedo$\to$melt$\to$SWE feedback
the offline sweep holds fixed, so those numbers are lower bounds on the sensitivity.

\subsection{Round 21: resolution awareness, and a cap that disabled the
floor}\label{sub:r21}
Three source changes (\texttt{562df81}), one of them a real bug.

\textbf{\texttt{DCMAX} was capping the mass floor.} The order was
$d_c=\min(D_{max},\max(\text{fitted},\text{floor}))$, so the $0.30$\,m cap killed the floor
above $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=D_{max}\rho$ --- 30\,kg\,m$^{-2}$ at $\rho=100$. Every grid coarser
than TCO95 therefore got no floor beyond the reference value, and it is \emph{also} part of
why the sweep flattened past 30 and reversed past 40, which I had attributed entirely to
November. Now $d_c=\max(\min(D_{max},\text{fitted}),\text{floor})$: the cap guards the steep
$M=4.7$ extrapolation of the \emph{fit}, the floor is a separate physical statement about
how much box-mean mass fills a box of this size. The two were nested and should not have been.

\textbf{A resolution preset.} \texttt{ECE\_SNOW\_SCF\_DXKM} rescales \texttt{SWEMIN} from
its TCO95 reference, $\mathrm{SWEMIN}_{\text{eff}}=\mathrm{clamp}(\mathrm{SWEMIN}\cdot
(\Delta x/100)^{0.624},3,120)$, off by default so every earlier run reproduces. The exponent
$\ln 10/\ln 40$ comes from the only two \emph{measured} anchors: $3.0$ at $2.5$\,km (the
snow courses are 1--2\,km transects, i.e.\ the high-resolution limit observed directly) and
$30$ at $100$\,km (Rutgers). Presets: TL21 94, TL63 48, TL255 26, TCO95 30, TCO399 13,
TCO1279 6, TCO4000 3.

\textbf{Below 2\,km the scheme disables itself}, falling back to the as-released ramp with a
loud log line. At that scale the model resolves individual patches, so a subgrid curve
double-counts them, and 2\,km is the finest scale any calibration exists for.

\dead{The scale dependence can be measured as a power law by coarse-graining Rutgers and
the model together.} It cannot, and the attempt is kept because the negative result is the
useful part. The ladder confirms the \emph{sign} --- \texttt{SWEMIN} grows with box size,
$41.5$ at 66\,km against ${\sim}114$ at $\ge133$\,km --- but the values \textbf{plateau}
beyond 133\,km rather than following a power law; the per-cell fit implies ${\sim}80$ at
100\,km where the seasonal fit gives 30, a $3\times$ disagreement that is the same
climatology-versus-pointwise tension as the $(d,\rho)$ surface fit; its own extrapolation to
$2.5$\,km gives $9.4$ against the course anchor of $3$; and absolute skill is poor at every
scale (RMSE $0.20$--$0.25$ on a 0--1 field), which indicts the functional form rather than
the parameter. \textbf{The preset is an interpolation between two trusted anchors, not a
measured law, and a new resolution needs validation rather than a \texttt{DXKM} value.}

\section{Round 18 results (2026--08--07): the aerosol term is six times smaller than
inferred}\label{sec:r18res}
M1 removes the anthropogenic MACv2-SP plumes entirely; M2 gives the same CAMS mass its 3D
vertical distribution. Both sit on the present-day base, so the comparison is against
\texttt{amip\_presentday} over the same years.

\textbf{Units first, because they invalidated the first pass.} IFS TOA fluxes are
\emph{accumulated} J\,m$^{-2}$ over the 3600\,s step, not W\,m$^{-2}$; the raw numbers come
out ${\sim}3600\times$ too large. Dividing by the timestep reproduces the campaign's own
recorded decomposition almost exactly --- global $+2.68$ clear-sky and $-1.18$ cloud against
the recorded $+2.68$ / $-1.17$ --- which is the check that the pipeline is right.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
band & M1 clear-sky & $t$ & M1 \textbf{cloud} & $t$ \\
\midrule
60--90N & $+0.381$ & $+3.8^*$ & $\mathbf{-1.014}$ & $-7.7^*$ \\
30--60N & $+0.699$ & $+9.4^*$ & $\mathbf{-1.397}$ & $-13.3^*$ \\
tropics & $-0.037$ & $-2.0$ & $-0.439$ & $-5.9^*$ \\
\textbf{SO 45--65S} & $\mathbf{-0.116}$ & $\mathbf{-18.9^*}$ & $+0.022$ & $+0.1$ \\
60--90S & $+0.010$ & $+0.3$ & $+0.079$ & $+1.0$ \\
GLOBAL & $+0.126$ & $+11.1^*$ & $\mathbf{-0.542}$ & $-8.9^*$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Paired annual differences over 25\,yr; the runs share prescribed SSTs and years,
so the pairing removes the forcing variability. $^*$ clears two-sided 95\,\% ($|t|>2.06$).

\dead{Aerosol contributes ${\sim}0.7$\,W\,m$^{-2}$, 13\,\% of the Southern Ocean's $+5.10$
clear-sky excess, inferred from a $+0.033$ AOD excess against MISR.} \textbf{Measured, it is
$-0.116$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ ($t=-18.9$) --- six times smaller.} Removing \emph{all} anthropogenic
aerosol moves SO clear-sky reflection by a tenth of a W\,m$^{-2}$. Aerosol is ${\sim}2$\,\%
of the SO excess, not 13\,\%, and \textbf{the unexplained residual grows from ${\sim}1.3$ to
${\sim}1.9$\,W\,m$^{-2}$}.

\textbf{The two reflection columns are not separable.} M1's effect is overwhelmingly on
\emph{cloud} --- $-1.40$ against $+0.70$ at 30--60N, $-0.54$ against $+0.13$ globally ---
through \texttt{LMACV2SP\_CCNF}. Any future aerosol lever must be scored on both columns; a
clear-sky-only argument is invalid. M2 moves the tropical clear-sky column by $+0.553$
($t=+79$) at \emph{fixed column mass}, so where the aerosol sits is a first-order term of
its own.

\textbf{Open and untraced:} the M1 clear-sky deltas are mostly \emph{positive} --- removing
aerosol \emph{increased} clear-sky reflection ($+0.126$ global, $t=+11.1$). That is backwards
for a direct effect and is significant, so it is not noise; most plausibly a surface-albedo
or water-vapour adjustment to the changed surface SW, but it has not been chased down.

\section{Round 21 results (2026--08--07): P5 adopted, and the summer ceiling}\label{sec:r21res}
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
run & DJF & MAM & JJA & SON \\
\midrule
K1 (base) & $-0.496$ & $-0.265$ & $+1.036^*$ & $+0.238$ \\
N2 \texttt{tanh} & $-2.783^*$ & $-0.599^*$ & $+1.350^*$ & $-0.888^*$ \\
P3 $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=3$ & $-0.584$ & $-0.333$ & $+1.341^*$ & $+0.217$ \\
\textbf{P5 $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=15$} & $\mathbf{-0.060}$ & $\mathbf{-0.140}$ & $\mathbf{+1.149^*}$ & $\mathbf{+0.004}$ \\
P6 $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=30$ & $\mathbf{-0.850^*}$ & $-0.288$ & $+1.096^*$ & $\mathbf{+0.487^*}$ \\

\noindent \textbf{P5 is the cleanest run in the campaign} --- every season inside its own
threshold bar a significant summer gain. Against Rutgers it cuts the autumn excess P3
introduced by ${\approx}40\,\%$ (Sep $+0.259\to+0.151$, Oct $+0.249\to+0.148$) while leaving
Nov--Jan intact, and its DJF soil is $+0.22$\,K against the scheme-off reference.

\dead{Raising SWEMIN cannot reopen the winter, because at midwinter SWE${\approx}93$ the floor
is $0.15$\,m against a $0.43$\,m pack --- safe by construction.} True in \emph{January}, and I
never checked \textbf{November}, where the pack is still shallow. P6's November
$f_{\text{full}}$ falls to $0.752$ against N1's $0.884$ and its DJF soil to $-1.00$\,K. The
floor binds in early winter; that is the interior optimum the offline sweep predicted,
arriving by the mechanism I had dismissed.

\subsection{The JJA ceiling: the lever was nearly exhausted before round 15}\label{sub:jjaceiling}
Marginal over the K1 base, against the $\pm0.242$ threshold: P3 $+0.305$ (marginal), P5
$+0.113$ (noise), P6 $+0.060$ (noise). Only P3 buys a measurable summer gain, and P3 is the
version with the worst autumn cover. The reason is structural. May cover against Rutgers'
$0.655$:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r}
\toprule
 & May cover & vs observed \\
\midrule
N1 as-released & $0.773$ & $\mathbf{+0.118}$ \quad(the entire available error) \\
N2 \texttt{tanh} & $0.615$ & $\mathbf{-0.040}$ \quad(overshot \emph{past} observations) \\
P3 / P5 & $0.729$ / $0.732$ & $+0.074$ / $+0.077$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The whole spring cover error is $0.118$.} P5 removes $0.044$; a perfect
correction removes $0.118$, worth roughly $+0.2$\,K of JJA over K1 on a crude scaling of N2's
response. \textbf{And N2's larger gain was never legitimate} --- it took $0.158$ out of a
$0.118$ error, overshooting into a $-0.040$ deficit, so that extra warmth came from having too
little May snow. It is the same over-depletion that destroyed the winter soil, and any
comparison against N2's $+1.350$ is a comparison against an error.

Honest accounting for the whole depletion line: worth ${\approx}0.1$--$0.3$\,K of Siberian
JJA, not the ${\approx}1$\,K the early rounds implied. What it delivered instead is a scheme
that is no longer \emph{wrong}. \textbf{The missing summer is elsewhere}: every AMIP run
including scheme-off tracks RIHMI soil through Dec--Mar and falls $2$--$3.5$\,\textdegree C
short from April to September (Fig.~\ref{fig:amipsoil}), a deficit common to all of them, and
that is the global tropospheric cold bias of \S\ref{sub:vprof} --- still untouched after 50
runs, and now the sharpest remaining target. \textbf{Further \texttt{SWEMIN} optimisation is
not worth runs}: P3 and P5 differ by $0.19$\,K in JJA, at or below noise.

\section{The coupled radiation objective, and why the goal moves to zero
(2026--08--07)}\label{sec:toagoal}
The 10 series branches from the same point as round 09 and runs twenty years longer, so it
extends the campaign's central radiation figure rather than replacing it. 10A is 09C plus
G4; 10B adds the \texttt{tanh} depletion since falsified (\S\ref{sec:r20res}).

\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.99\textwidth]{plots/campaign_net_toa_by_year_with10.png}
\caption{Global-mean net TOA by model year, annual (thin) and 5-yr running mean (bold).
\textbf{G4 is worth ${\approx}0.25$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ at top of atmosphere}: 10A settles at
$+0.854$ against 09C's ${\approx}1.06$, the best of the previous round, and both 10-series
runs spend 1362--1380 at or below the $+0.67$ atmosphere-only floor. \textbf{10A and 10B are
indistinguishable} ($+0.854$ vs $+0.899$, curves interleaving throughout), so the depletion
scheme that destroyed the winter soil by 20\,K cost essentially nothing at TOA --- consistent
with its damage being a distributional surface effect invisible to box-mean energy
diagnostics. The dashed goal is now $0$, not the inherited $-0.16$; the shaded band is the
part of the gap ocean-side tuning could still close, and everything above the dotted line is
atmospheric. 11C is absent: it aborted in its first leg on the crash of
\S\ref{sub:r20crash}.}
\label{fig:toa10}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Why the goal is $0$ and not $-0.16$}\label{sub:toazero}
$-0.16$ was never derived. It is whatever the HR piControl settled at, adopted as a matching
target. A target displaced from zero is only defensible if the atmosphere fails to conserve
energy: with a spurious atmospheric source $S$, equilibrium gives
$\mathrm{SFC}=\mathrm{TOA}+S$, so zero \emph{ocean} drift would require $\mathrm{TOA}=-S$.
Whether the two conditions differ is measurable, not arguable.

Measured on 10A over 1390--1399, global, cos-latitude weighted, using the release tool's own
formula ($\mathtt{ssr}+\mathtt{str}+\mathtt{sshf}+\mathtt{slhf}-\mathtt{sf}\cdot3.3355\times10^{8}$,
each divided by the accumulation period):

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r}
\toprule
term & W\,m$^{-2}$ \\
\midrule
\texttt{ssr} $+$ \texttt{str} $+$ \texttt{sshf} $+$ \texttt{slhf} & $+1.843$ \\
snow enthalpy ($\mathtt{sf}=1.042\times10^{-5}$\,m/step) & $-0.966$ \\
\midrule
net surface & $\mathbf{+0.877}$ \\
net TOA (\texttt{tsr} $+238.749$, \texttt{ttr} $-237.895$) & $\mathbf{+0.854}$ \\
\midrule
residual & $\mathbf{-0.023}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The atmosphere conserves to $0.02$\,W\,m$^{-2}$.} There is no leak for an
offset target to absorb, so ``the ocean is not drifting'' and ``the system is not drifting''
are the same condition, and both put the goal at $\mathbf{0}$.

\textbf{Snow enthalpy is doing nearly all of the reconciliation} --- $0.97$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ of a
$0.99$ discrepancy. Omitted, the surface reads $+1.84$ against a TOA of $+0.85$, and that
artefact is exactly the kind of apparent imbalance an offset target gets invented to absorb.
Anyone comparing a raw surface flux against TOA without it will see a spurious
${\approx}1$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ gap.

Re-framing the campaign arithmetic against the corrected goal:

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{l r c r r}
\toprule
 & net TOA & $=$ atmosphere $+$ & coupled-state & gap to $0$ \\
\midrule
080a (untuned coupled) & $1.38$ & $0.67\ +$ & $\mathbf{0.71}$ & $1.38$ \\
09C (06V $+$ newSeaIce) & $1.06$ & $0.67\ +$ & $\mathbf{0.39}$ & $1.06$ \\
\textbf{10A} (${}+$G4) & $\mathbf{0.85}$ & $0.67\ +$ & $\mathbf{0.18}$ & $\mathbf{0.85}$ \\
10B (${}+$\texttt{tanh}) & $0.90$ & $0.67\ +$ & $0.23$ & $0.90$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent The conclusion is unchanged in kind and smaller in size: the ocean-side coupled-state
term is now only $0.18$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ in 10A, so \textbf{almost the entire remaining imbalance
is the atmosphere} --- the same $+0.67$ the AMIP testbed measures directly. Chasing $-0.16$
would have meant over-tuning by that margin against a target with no physical basis in this
configuration.

\dead{The HR piControl goal of $-0.16$ is the target for the coupled spin-up.} Inherited, not
derived; the budget closes to $0.02$\,W\,m$^{-2}$, so the goal is $0$.

\section{Round 22 (2026--08--09): the energy target re-based, and three targets
relocated}\label{sec:r22}
Four diagnostics, no new model time except the coupled runs a colleague had already
finished. Three of them move a target, and two of them falsify something this report
was recommending.

\subsection{Coupled round 11: the first runs carrying G4 \emph{and} D2b}\label{sub:r11coupled}
The report has said the coupled branch carries only G4 out of the whole AMIP campaign.
\textbf{That is out of date.} \texttt{11A}, \texttt{11B} and \texttt{11D} all carry
\texttt{ECE\_TUNE\_RVRSMIN} $1000/1000/225$ \emph{and} \texttt{RCL\_INPSEA}$=0.2$ with
\texttt{RCL\_INPPMIN}$=70000$ --- G4 plus D2b --- on top of a new \texttt{useIFSsoiltemp}
coupling. \texttt{110Baseline} is 09C plus that coupling change alone and is therefore the
correct control; comparing against 10A would confound the snow scheme with the
soil-temperature coupling.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
$\Delta$ vs 110Baseline & net TOA & glob T2m & Sib JJA & Sib DJF & \textbf{soil DJF} & SO cloud \\
\midrule
11A $+$G4$+$D2b & $-0.233$ & $-0.150$ & $-0.16$ & $+0.13$ & $-0.41$ & $+0.84$ \\
11B $+$\texttt{tanh} snow & $-0.060$ & $-0.019$ & $+1.81$ & $-1.62$ & $\mathbf{-19.40}$ & $+0.76$ \\
\textbf{11D} $+$fitted, \texttt{SWEMIN}$=30$ & $+0.023$ & $-0.031$ & $+0.16$ & $+0.05$ & $\mathbf{-0.45}$ & $+0.41$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The \texttt{tanh} falsification reproduces coupled on a clean pair}:
11B minus 11A is $-18.99$\,K of Siberian DJF soil, against the $-13$ to $-16$\,K the AMIP
run predicted. This is a better-controlled version of the 10B result, because 11A and 11B
differ \emph{only} in the snow scheme.

\textbf{11D is not catastrophic, but it has not tested what it looks like it tested.}
11D runs the fitted scheme at $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=30$ --- that is P6, the setting AMIP
\emph{rejected} (DJF $-0.850^*$, November $f_{\text{full}}$ $0.752$, soil $-1.00$\,K),
not the adopted P5 at $15$. Against 11A it gives soil $-0.04$\,K and DJF T2m $-0.08$\,K.
Before reading that as vindication, the detection threshold was measured from the
interannual scatter of these very runs:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r}
\toprule
 & interannual sd & 95\,\% threshold, 10-yr pair \\
\midrule
Siberian DJF soil (\texttt{stl2}) & $0.80$\,K & $\pm0.75$\,K \\
Siberian DJF T2m & $1.33$\,K & $\pm1.25$\,K \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent So the soil half of the P6 falsifier ($-1.00$\,K) is marginally testable and
comes out null; \textbf{the DJF T2m half ($-0.850$\,K) is not testable at all on ten
coupled years}, and a null there was the expected outcome whether the scheme is safe or
not. What 11D \emph{does} establish is that this diagnostic has ample power for damage of
the kind that mattered --- it sees 11B's $-19$\,K without difficulty. \textbf{The
outstanding coupled run is 11D at $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=15$ plus K1}, which would bring the
coupled configuration level with the atmosphere-only one for the first time.

\subsection{The energy target, period-clean}\label{sub:periodclean}
Every band target in this report was measured on \texttt{amip\_pi\_base} against CERES
07/2005--06/2015 --- an 1870s atmosphere against a present-day satellite record.
\texttt{amip\_presentday} overlaps CERES directly and its forcing is \emph{correct} for
1990--2014 (unlike the PI arm, whose \texttt{NCMIPFIXYR} never took effect,
\S\ref{sub:deadnml}), so the epoch offset is measurable per band rather than
caveated. Band means over full latitude circles are exact on each source's own grid, so
nothing is remapped. The CERES read is validated against the published EBAF globals
first: ASR $241.18$ (published ${\sim}240.5$), OLR $240.21$ (${\sim}240.2$), net $+0.970$.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r l}
\toprule
band & PI vs CERES & \textbf{PD vs CERES} & offset & verdict \\
\midrule
90S--65S & $+7.24$ & $+8.11$ & $-0.87$ & robust to the epoch \\
\textbf{SO 65S--45S} & $+6.32$ & $\mathbf{+7.46}$ & $-1.15$ & \textbf{robust, and larger} \\
45S--30S & $-4.46$ & $-2.49$ & $-1.97$ & partly epoch \\
\textbf{tropics 30S--30N} & $-2.51$ & $\mathbf{-0.67}$ & $-1.83$ & \textbf{epoch-dominated} \\
30N--45N & $+0.81$ & $+2.08$ & $-1.27$ & partly epoch \\
45N--65N & $+4.40$ & $+5.35$ & $-0.96$ & robust to the epoch \\
65N--90N & $+0.39$ & $+1.53$ & $-1.14$ & partly epoch \\
\midrule
GLOBAL & $-0.33$ & $\mathbf{+1.23}$ & $-1.56$ & \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Two targets move. \textbf{The tropics are epoch-dominated} --- period-clean the
band is only $-0.67$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ from CERES, not $-2.51$, so most of what
\S\ref{sub:openlist} has carried as a tropical deficit was never model error.
\textbf{The Southern Ocean survives the correction and grows}, $+6.32\to+7.46$, and its
contribution to the global mean rises to $+0.735$\,W\,m$^{-2}$. It is now unambiguously
the largest single regional term.

\emph{A small discrepancy, recorded.} \S\ref{sub:sobands} quotes $+5.81$ for this band
against a CERES value of $-55.3$; reading the gridded climatology directly gives $-55.81$
and hence $+6.32$ on the same PI arm. The new number comes with a validated global check
and is preferred, but the difference is not chased further --- it does not change any
conclusion.

\textbf{The OLR test, which does not close.} A cold troposphere under-emits, so the
measured OLR deficit and the measured cold bias should be quantitatively consistent.
Period-clean, $\Delta\mathrm{OLR}=-2.529$ and $\Delta\mathrm{ASR}=-1.299$\,W\,m$^{-2}$,
which is where the $+1.229$ net comes from. But the effective emitting temperature from
CERES OLR is $255.1$\,K, giving a Planck slope of $3.77$\,W\,m$^{-2}$\,K$^{-1}$, and the
model is $-1.62$\,K through 700--300\,hPa: a purely radiative response would be
$-6.10$\,W\,m$^{-2}$. \textbf{Measured minus predicted is $+3.57$\,W\,m$^{-2}$}, and
\S\ref{sub:utls} identifies what is offsetting it.

\subsection{The Southern Ocean deficit is cloud AMOUNT --- and no lever moves
it}\label{sub:socloudarea}
The band error has been tracked by CRE, which cannot distinguish too little cloud from
cloud that is not bright enough. Splitting it at the \emph{observed} CRE per unit area
(\texttt{amip\_presentday} vs CERES, period-clean):

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
SO 65--45S & model & CERES & diff \\
\midrule
cloud area [\%] & $83.06$ & $89.49$ & $\mathbf{-6.43}$ \\
SW CRE [W\,m$^{-2}$] & $-60.39$ & $-67.84$ & $\mathbf{+7.45}$ \\
CRE per unit area & $-72.71$ & $-75.80$ & $+3.10$ \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{4}{l}{\emph{decomposition of the $+7.45$:}}\\
AMOUNT (missing area $\times$ observed CRE/area) & & & $\mathbf{+4.87}$ \quad($65.5\,\%$) \\
OPACITY (residual) & & & $+2.57$ \quad($34.5\,\%$) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{Amount-dominated}, and \textbf{zonally uniform} --- Atlantic $-5.40$,
Indian $-6.82$, Australian $-6.55$, Pacific $-6.81$\,pp, a sector spread of $1.42$\,pp,
which points at microphysics rather than circulation or the ice edge. It is worst in
austral spring and summer (SON $-7.03$, DJF $-6.94$\,pp), when there is shortwave to
reflect: DJF alone carries $+11.67$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ of net TOA error in the band. That
seasonality is what the supercooled-liquid hypothesis predicts.

\textbf{Then the part that falsifies the plan.} Scored on cloud \emph{area} for the first
time, against the $+6.43$\,pp that needs closing:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r l}
\toprule
lever (44-yr AMIP, $\Delta$ vs control) & $\Delta$ area [pp] & $\Delta$ SW CRE & \\
\midrule
D2a \texttt{inpsea.2} & $+0.63$ & $-4.80$ & $9.7\,\%$ of the gap \\
D2b \texttt{inp+p700} & $+0.43$ & $-2.61$ & $6.7\,\%$ \\
A1a \texttt{ovl=0.10} & $+0.62$ & $-6.44$ & $9.7\,\%$ \\
\textbf{G4} \texttt{tundra225} & $+0.48$ & $-2.73$ & \textbf{a LAND lever} \\
\textbf{K1} \texttt{landalb} & $+0.45$ & $-2.62$ & \textbf{a LAND lever} \\
B7 \texttt{rvice.22} & $-0.60$ & $+0.66$ & wrong sign \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\dead{G4 and K1 are boreal-land levers that cannot physically change Southern Ocean cloud
area, and they move it by $+0.48$ and $+0.45$\,pp, so ${\approx}0.5$\,pp is the noise level
of this diagnostic and every INP run sits inside it.} \textbf{Wrong, and corrected the same
day.} G4 and K1 are not independent of D2b --- they \emph{contain} it (G4 $=$ F4 $+$ D2b $+$
tundra, K1 $=$ G4 $+$ land albedo), so their $+0.48$/$+0.45$ is D2b's $+0.43$ inherited, not
a null. Differenced against D2b the pure land contribution is $+0.050$ and $+0.022$\,pp,
which is the clean control and is genuinely negligible.

\textbf{The real threshold, measured.} Interannual scatter of SO cloud area in the 44-year
control is $0.232$\,pp, giving a 95\,\% detection threshold of $\pm0.098$\,pp on a paired
difference --- five times smaller than the figure asserted above. \textbf{Every lever in the
table is therefore statistically significant.} The conclusion survives, but it rests on
MAGNITUDE rather than on significance:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r l}
\toprule
lever & $\Delta$area & \% of the $6.43$ gap & $\Delta$CRE & of which opacity \\
\midrule
D2a \texttt{inpsea 0.2} & $+0.626$ & $10\,\%$ & $-4.80$ & $90\,\%$ \\
A1a \texttt{ovlliqice 0.10} & $+0.623$ & $10\,\%$ & $-6.44$ & $93\,\%$ \\
D2b \texttt{inp+p700} & $+0.432$ & $7\,\%$ & $-2.61$ & $87\,\%$ \\
A1b \texttt{ovlliqice 0.35} & $+0.331$ & $5\,\%$ & $-2.17$ & $88\,\%$ \\
\midrule
\emph{G4 minus D2b} & $+0.050$ & --- & $-0.12$ & \emph{the land control} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The best lever in the campaign closes one tenth of the area gap and takes
${\approx}90\,\%$ of its CRE from opacity.} Two independent mixed-phase routes agree ---
ice nuclei (D2a/D2b) and the liquid/ice deposition overlap (A1a/A1b, the latter pushed to
EC-Earth4's $0.1$) --- so this is a property of the mixed-phase branch, not of one
parameter. \textbf{The amount deficit, two thirds of the error, is $90\,\%$ intact.}

\dead{The Southern Ocean cloud-area deficit should be attacked by pushing the INP branch
harder, since D2b is the only lever that has moved the SO.} Written into the plan on
2026--08--09 and falsified the same day. The INP branch acts on opacity, which is $34.5\,\%$
of the error; its area response closes only one tenth of the gap, and the same is true of
the deposition-overlap route even at EC-Earth4's setting. A cloud-\emph{amount} knob is
required, and it is a different knob.

\emph{One more pointer.} CERES puts the SO effective cloud pressure at $656$\,hPa, i.e.\
just \emph{above} the $700$\,hPa ceiling of D2b's pressure gate, with optical depth
$7.41$. The gate was designed to protect the tropics and it does, but it may also be
excluding the layer where the observed cloud actually sits.

\subsection{The cold bias is extratropical and UTLS --- and the atmosphere is dry as well
as cold}\label{sub:utls}
\S\ref{sub:vprof} established that above 700\,hPa Siberia merely shares a global cold
bias. Where that bias lives has never been asked. \texttt{amip\_presentday} vs ERA5
1990--2014, annual, all levels exact in both so nothing is interpolated:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{r r r r r r r}
\toprule
hPa & 90S--60S & 60S--30S & \textbf{tropics} & 30N--60N & 60N--90N & GLOBAL \\
\midrule
 850 & $+0.02$ & $-1.17$ & $-1.10$ & $-0.90$ & $-0.87$ & $-0.99$ \\
 700 & $-0.53$ & $-1.40$ & $-1.23$ & $-1.00$ & $-0.86$ & $-1.15$ \\
 500 & $-1.81$ & $-1.75$ & $-1.31$ & $-1.56$ & $-1.57$ & $-1.49$ \\
 300 & $\mathbf{-3.65}$ & $-2.48$ & $\mathbf{-1.72}$ & $-2.34$ & $\mathbf{-3.43}$ & $-2.22$ \\
 200 & $\mathbf{-4.20}$ & $-3.28$ & $\mathbf{-2.09}$ & $-3.53$ & $\mathbf{-4.45}$ & $-2.87$ \\
 100 & $+0.25$ & $-1.52$ & $-2.07$ & $-1.30$ & $+0.26$ & $-1.51$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The tropics are the \emph{least} biased band}, $-1.72$ at 300\,hPa
against an extratropical mean of $-2.97$. That excludes the hypothesis a reader would
reach for first: a cold tropical upper troposphere over prescribed, correct SST would
have meant a convective heating-profile error propagating globally. It is not that. The
bias peaks at 200\,hPa over \emph{both} poles ($-4.20$ and $-4.45$) and \textbf{reverses
sign at 100\,hPa} ($+0.25$, $+0.26$) --- a tropopause-region signature, not a tropospheric
one. The seasonal tables behave the same way, so it is not a summer or winter effect.

\textbf{And the atmosphere is dry, increasingly so with height:}

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{r r r r r}
\toprule
hPa & $\Delta T$ [K] & $q$ model & $q$ ERA5 & $\Delta q/q$ [\%] \\
\midrule
 850 & $-0.99$ & $5.7843$ & $5.9887$ & $-3.4$ \\
 700 & $-1.15$ & $2.9203$ & $3.1596$ & $-7.6$ \\
 500 & $-1.49$ & $1.0082$ & $1.1018$ & $-8.5$ \\
 300 & $-2.22$ & $0.1454$ & $0.1695$ & $\mathbf{-14.2}$ \\
 200 & $-2.87$ & $0.0208$ & $0.0260$ & $\mathbf{-20.2}$ \\
 100 & $-1.51$ & $0.0014$ & $0.0026$ & $-46.9$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{This is the missing $3.57$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ of \S\ref{sub:periodclean}.} The
two errors oppose each other in OLR: the cold depresses emission, while the dryness lowers
the effective emission level into warmer air and raises it. Over 700--300\,hPa the model is
$-1.62$\,K and $-10.1\,\%$ relative humidity content --- both large, both real, and their
sum is small.

\textbf{The consequence is a constraint on all future radiation tuning.} Fixing either one
alone moves net TOA by several W\,m$^{-2}$ in the \emph{wrong} direction. The global budget
that \S\ref{sub:toadecomp} found ``right by cancellation'' is cancelling in the vertical as
well as in the horizontal, and any lever scored on a global mean is being scored on top of
that. \emph{Caveat:} ERA5 upper-tropospheric humidity is itself weakly constrained by
observation, so the $100$\,hPa row is indicative only; the $300$--$200$\,hPa rows carry the
argument.

\subsection{What round 22 changes about the plan}\label{sub:r22plan}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The Southern Ocean needs a cloud-\emph{amount} lever}, not more ice
nucleation. Two thirds of the error is missing area; the INP family moves area at the
noise level. Whatever is chosen must be scored on area, and the D2b gate ceiling should be
revisited against the observed $656$\,hPa cloud pressure.
\item \textbf{The tropical TOA deficit is largely not model error} and should come off the
open list at its old size.
\item \textbf{The cold-bias investigation starts at the extratropical tropopause}, and it
must treat cold-and-dry as one problem. A lever that warms without moistening will make
the energy target worse.
\item \textbf{The next coupled run is 11D at $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=15$ plus K1.} Ten years
cannot resolve a $1$\,K DJF signal, so if the P6-versus-P5 question is to be settled
coupled at all, it needs either a longer run or the AMIP answer taken on trust --- and
the AMIP answer says $15$.
\end{itemize}

\section{Round 23 (2026--08--09): the reference check, and a round withdrawn}\label{sec:r23}
Round 23 was designed to close the Southern Ocean cloud-\emph{amount} deficit. Three of its
four runs were withdrawn before completion --- one as a duplicate, two on a design error ---
and the round's lasting product is a reference check that decides which observation the
campaign is allowed to tune against.

\subsection{Prior work this campaign had not incorporated}\label{sub:r23prior}
Two AWI-ESM project issues (\#169, \#170, 2025--03 to 2026--04) cover the same Southern Ocean
warm bias from the coupled side and contain results this report was working without.

\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The origin is cy48's moist-physics upgrade.} Cycle 48r1 raised high cloud cover
everywhere relative to 43r3, strongest in the SH high latitudes, and lowered SO low cloud
(\#170). The exact diff is the ECMWF Newsletter 164 moist-physics patch, which was evaluated
mainly at ${\sim}5$\,km and mainly in low latitudes.
\item \textbf{The lead/lag test, which orders the causal chain.} Scored on \emph{year one}
rather than the 50-year mean, the hcc offset is already present while the T2m bias and the SO
lcc dip are \emph{not}. So hcc leads and lcc lags: \texttt{fix hcc} $\to$ \texttt{reduces t2m}
$\to$ \texttt{fixes lcc}. In the coupled model the SO low-cloud deficit is partly a
\emph{consequence} of the warm bias.
\item \textbf{\texttt{RVICE} is already the hcc lever.} The v3.4 tuning raised it from the
$0.13$ source default to $0.16$--$0.19$ (TUNE1--TUNE5, with \texttt{NDECOLAD} $2\to0$ and
\texttt{RALBSEAD} $0.06\to0.05$) precisely to suppress SO high cloud. Every run in this
campaign carries \texttt{RVICE}$=0.16$ for that reason.
\item \textbf{The compensating-bias trap is documented.} ``All further tests regarding
reducing HCC resulted in Arctic and Tropics becoming too cold'', and \texttt{RALBSEAD} was
found ``not a strong enough counter tuning method for the combined \texttt{RVICE} $+$
\texttt{ENTSTPC3}''.
\end{itemize}

\dead{Lowering \texttt{RVICE} below $0.16$ would add tropical cirrus and warm the tropics,
providing the counter-lever the Southern Ocean levers need.} Mechanically correct --- B7 at
$0.22$ gives $\Delta$high $-2.31$\,pp and $\Delta$LW\,CRE $-1.35$ --- but it would undo the
v3.4 SO tuning, which raised \texttt{RVICE} for exactly this reason. \texttt{RVICE} is global
and the two regions need opposite signs, so it cannot serve both.

\subsection{Four-way attribution: what kind of error each band has}\label{sub:r23attr}
Net TOA cannot distinguish a surface-albedo error from a cloud-reflection one. Splitting each
band against CERES into clear/cloud $\times$ SW/LW, period-clean, positive $=$ the model gains
energy:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
band & net & SW clr & \textbf{SW cld} & LW clr & \textbf{LW cld} & contribution \\
\midrule
90S--65S & $+8.11$ & $+1.53$ & $+3.98$ & $+4.35$ & $-1.75$ & $+0.390$ \\
\textbf{SO 65S--45S} & $+7.46$ & $-3.79$ & $\mathbf{+7.45}$ & $+4.21$ & $-0.40$ & $\mathbf{+0.735}$ \\
45S--30S & $-2.49$ & $-2.54$ & $-2.82$ & $+4.66$ & $-1.79$ & $-0.257$ \\
\textbf{tropics} & $-0.67$ & $-1.39$ & $-0.40$ & $+3.28$ & $\mathbf{-2.16}$ & $-0.336$ \\
30N--45N & $+2.08$ & $-4.90$ & $+4.67$ & $+4.36$ & $-2.05$ & $+0.215$ \\
45N--65N & $+5.35$ & $-2.67$ & $+2.45$ & $+5.11$ & $+0.45$ & $+0.527$ \\
65N--90N & $+1.53$ & $-3.79$ & $-1.45$ & $+6.30$ & $+0.46$ & $+0.073$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The two problems are different cloud types.} The Southern Ocean loses its
energy through \textbf{SW cloud} ($+7.45$): the low cloud does not reflect enough. The tropics
lose theirs through \textbf{LW cloud} ($-2.16$): the high cloud does not trap enough. With SST
prescribed and correct, a tropical LW-cloud deficit is an anvil/cirrus problem --- too low, too
thin, or radiatively too weak --- not a surface or a convective-heating one.

That is why no single microphysics knob has ever satisfied both, and it is also the shape of a
solution: a lever that makes ice cloud \emph{fewer but optically stronger} would move the
Southern Ocean and the tropics in opposite, both-correct directions. \texttt{RVICE} changes
amount alone, which is why it trades one for the other.

\textbf{Scanning all 15 NAMCLDP-family levers for tropical response}, only three warm the
tropics at all: B3 \texttt{clddiff}$=1.5$e-5 ($+2.02$, via SW not LW), B2
\texttt{clddiff\_convi} ($+0.65$) and K1 ($+0.37$, clear-sky, its subtropical
crop/semidesert albedo). \textbf{Nothing in 50 runs warms the tropics through the longwave},
which is the term that is actually deficient.

\subsection{Which reference is right about Southern Ocean cloud?}\label{sub:r23ref}
The round-22 amount deficit rests on one source. Bringing ERA5 in appeared to contradict it
outright, so before spending runs the references were tested against each other.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
SO 65--45S & model & ERA5 & CERES & MODIS \\
\midrule
cloud area [\%] & $83.1$ & $81.6$ & $\mathbf{89.5}$ & $\mathbf{89.3}$ \\
SW CRE [W\,m$^{-2}$] & $-60.39$ & $-61.52$ & $\mathbf{-67.84}$ & --- \\
vs CERES & $\mathbf{+7.45}$ & $\mathbf{+6.31}$ & --- & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The test is what the cloud DOES, not how much is counted.} Cloud area is not
one quantity: CERES \texttt{cldarea} is a MODIS \emph{mask} fraction that flags optically thin
cloud in full, while ERA5 and model \texttt{tcc} are radiative covers built by overlapping
layer fractions. Comparing them directly is a category error. Shortwave CRE is a difference of
two broadband fluxes and \emph{is} identically defined across all three.

If ERA5's lower area were a definition artefact, ERA5 would reflect like CERES. \textbf{It does
not: ERA5 has low area \emph{and} low reflection, in proportion} --- $6.31$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ short
where the model is $7.45$ short. ERA5 is reproducing the model's bias because ERA5 \emph{is}
IFS. \textbf{So CERES is the arbiter and the Southern Ocean amount deficit is real.}

\dead{The Southern Ocean cloud-area deficit may be an artefact of comparing a satellite mask
against a model \texttt{tcc}, since ERA5 puts SO cloud \emph{below} the model's.} Raised as a
concern and refuted the same day by the CRE test above. \textbf{ERA5 cannot be used as a cloud
reference for an IFS-derived model at all} --- its global cloud area is $62.8$ against CERES's
$67.4$, and in the tropics it reads $55.3$ where the model's $61.6$ matches CERES's $61.7$
almost exactly. On cloud, ERA5 is the outlier, not the model.

\textbf{A limit on CERES, in the other direction.} At 90S--65S CERES reads $67.5$ and MODIS
$59.8$ --- a $7.7$\,pp disagreement between two products of the same instrument. Passive cloud
masking over bright ice is unreliable. \dead{The Arctic has $+11.49$\,pp too much cloud
(\S\ref{sub:periodclean} band table).} Not supportable: that number is a cloud mask over sea
ice and snow, exactly where the two retrievals disagree most. \textbf{CERES cloud area is
trustworthy over dark ocean and should not be used over ice.}

\subsection{The runs, and why three were withdrawn}\label{sub:r23runs}
Four levers were designed on the P5 base (which already carries G4, K1 and D2b):

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\toprule
run & lever & outcome \\
\midrule
S1 & \texttt{RCLDIFF} $6$e-6 $\to3$e-6 & \textbf{cancelled} --- would cool the tropics \\
S2 & \texttt{RCL\_OVERLAPLIQICE} $0.65\to0.35$ & \textbf{withdrawn} --- this is A1b, already run \\
S3 & \texttt{RCLCRIT\_SEA} $2.5$e-4 $\to6$e-4 & \textbf{cancelled} --- same reason as S1 \\
\textbf{S4} & \texttt{RCL\_INPPMIN} $70000\to50000$ & \textbf{running} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\dead{\texttt{RCL\_OVERLAPLIQICE} $0.65\to0.35$ is an untested mixed-phase lever.} It is
\textbf{A1b}, run for 44 years in round 3; A1a is the same knob at EC-Earth4's $0.1$. The
A-series label ``ovl'' denotes the liquid/ice \emph{deposition} overlap, not the radiative
cloud overlap, and it was read as the latter. The duplicate is not a total loss: A1a/A1b give
a \emph{second, independent} mixed-phase route, and it behaves exactly like the INP route
($+0.62$\,pp of area for $-6.44$ of CRE, i.e.\ $93\,\%$ opacity), which is far stronger
evidence that opacity-not-amount is a property of the whole mixed-phase branch than one
parameter could be.

\textbf{S1 and S3 were cancelled for the tropics, not for the Southern Ocean.} Both add low
cloud, and B3 --- the same parameter as S1 moved the other way --- \emph{warms} the tropics by
$+2.02$\,W\,m$^{-2}$. S1 would therefore cool a band that is period-cleanly only $-0.67$ from
CERES, overshooting it roughly threefold. The deficit they were built to close is real; a
\emph{global} low-cloud knob is the wrong instrument for it. Their runscripts are kept under
\texttt{runscripts/oifsamip/withdrawn/}.

\texttt{RCLCRIT\_SEA} and \texttt{RCLCRIT\_LAND} were exposed to \texttt{NAMCLDP} for S3
(\texttt{sucldp.F90}: moved from the \texttt{ASSOCIATE} block onto the \texttt{POINTER}
mechanism every other \texttt{NAMCLDP} variable uses, with the association placed before the
defaults are assigned so a silent namelist still reproduces). The exposure is kept --- it
works, and the lever may be wanted later with better targeting.

\subsection{What round 23 changes about the plan}\label{sub:r23plan}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The Southern Ocean deficit is confirmed and stays the largest single term}, but
it needs a \emph{regime-selective} reflection lever. Global low-cloud knobs overshoot the
tropics; the D2b pressure gate is the template for what selective looks like.
\emph{Answered in \S\ref{sec:r24}}: marine CCN is that lever, it is selective by
construction, and it reaches the opacity third only.
\item \textbf{The tropics need longwave cloud \emph{effectiveness}, not more cloud.} The
deficit is $-2.16$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ of LW CRE, and no lever in 50 runs has moved the tropics
through the longwave at all.
\item \textbf{Reference hygiene is now a stated rule.} ERA5 is not an independent cloud
reference for this model; CERES cloud area is valid over dark ocean and not over ice.
\item \textbf{Check the prior issues before designing a round.} Both errors this round --- the
A1b duplicate and the \texttt{RVICE} proposal --- were already answered in \#169/\#170.
\end{itemize}

\section{Round 24 (2026--08--09): the marine CCN path, traced end to end}\label{sec:r24}
No runs. The question was whether cloud condensation nuclei offer the \emph{regionally
selective} Southern Ocean lever round 23 concluded was needed. The answer is a qualified
yes, by a route nobody had looked at, and the trace corrected one of my own claims on the
way.

\subsection{The path, and where it is decided}\label{sub:r24path}
Marine CCN reaches cloud albedo through four hops, each gated by a switch whose
\emph{runtime} value had to be read from \texttt{fort.4} rather than a source default:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\toprule
where & what & runtime state \\
\midrule
\texttt{surfrad\_ctl\_mod.F90}:772 & Genthon (1992) sea salt from wind & \texttt{LCCNO}$=$\textbf{.TRUE.} \\
\texttt{liquid\_effective\_radius.F90}:153 & $\mathrm{CCN}\to N_d\to r_{\text{eff}}$ & \texttt{NRADLP}$=$\textbf{2} \\
\texttt{cloudsc.F90}:1023 & MACv2-SP CDNC factor & \texttt{LMACV2SP\_CCNF}$=$\textbf{.true.} \\
\texttt{cloudsc.F90}:2050 & CCN $\to$ autoconversion (lifetime) & \texttt{LAERLIQAUTOLSP}$=$\textbf{.FALSE.} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent With \texttt{LCCNO} true the code takes the \texttt{PCCN\_SEA} branch, so
\textbf{\texttt{RCCNSEA} is inert} --- its own source comment (``now irrelevant'') is
correct, but only because of a switch three files away. The live formula is
\[
Q=e^{0.16u+1.44},\quad \mathrm{CCN}=10^{\,1.2+0.5\log_{10}Q},\quad
N_d=-1.15{\times}10^{-3}\mathrm{CCN}^2+0.963\,\mathrm{CCN}+5.30 .
\]

\dead{Marine CCN is pinned at a uniform $125$\,cm$^{-3}$ because \texttt{NAERCLD}$=0$
switches the aerosol--cloud path off.} Read from a \emph{default assignment} in
\texttt{sucldp.F90} and reported as the runtime state, while \texttt{fort.4} carries
\texttt{LMACV2SP\_CCNF}$=$\texttt{.true.}, \texttt{PCCN} had already been printed as a
2-D field, and \S\ref{sec:r18res} of this report already documented that CCN path. Three
independent disproofs, two of them on screen at the time.

\subsection{The correction that matters: $N_d$ is right}\label{sub:r24nd}
Evaluating Genthon on \emph{monthly-mean} winds gives a Southern Ocean wind of
$6.54$\,m\,s$^{-1}$ and $N_d=55$, which against an observed $60$--$120$\,cm$^{-3}$ looks
like a clear deficit. \textbf{That was wrong.} Monthly \emph{vector} averaging cancels the
transient eddies that are the entire Southern Ocean wind field, and the parameterisation
acts on instantaneous speed. On 6-hourly output:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
SO 65--45S & wind [m\,s$^{-1}$] & CCN & $N_d$ \\
\midrule
monthly-mean wind (wrong) & $6.54$ & $55.6$ & $55.3$ \\
\textbf{6-hourly wind} & $\mathbf{10.28}$ & $\mathbf{77.6}$ & $\mathbf{73.5}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\dead{Southern Ocean droplet number is too low, so the cloud is too dark, and raising
marine CCN is the opacity lever.} $N_d\approx73$\,cm$^{-3}$ sits squarely inside the
observed $60$--$120$. \textbf{The mean is correct.} This is the same error class as
$\mathrm{SCF}(\bar d)\neq\overline{\mathrm{SCF}(d)}$ from round 19 --- a nonlinear
operator applied to a mean --- committed hours after writing it into a skill as a rule.

\textbf{What survives is the phase.} The model's seasonal cycle is inverted:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
SO 65--45S & DJF & MAM & JJA & SON \\
\midrule
$N_d$ [cm$^{-3}$] & $\mathbf{68.5}$ & $74.9$ & $\mathbf{76.1}$ & $74.5$ \\
SW CRE error [W\,m$^{-2}$] & $\mathbf{+10.86}$ & $+3.71$ & $+2.09$ & $\mathbf{+13.11}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Model $N_d$ is \emph{lowest} in DJF because it is wind-driven and Southern Ocean
winds peak in winter. Observations put the maximum in DJF, driven by biogenic sulphate. So
the model has its fewest droplets exactly when the sun is up and the measured error is
largest. Crudely --- $\tau\propto N_d^{1/3}$, two-stream albedo $A\approx\tau/(\tau+7.7)$
on CERES's $\tau=7.41$ --- lifting DJF $N_d$ from $68.5$ to an observed ${\sim}95$ is worth
about $-6$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ of DJF SO CRE, roughly half that month's error.

\subsection{The amount two-thirds is unreachable from here}\label{sub:r24amount}
$N_d$ acts through $r_{\text{eff}}$, i.e.\ \textbf{opacity}, which round 23 measured as
$34.5\,\%$ of the SO error. The cloud-\emph{lifetime} route that would reach the amount
two-thirds runs through \texttt{LAERLIQAUTOLSP}/\texttt{LAERLIQCOLL}, and both are
\texttt{.FALSE.}

\textbf{Enabling them would divide by zero.} Traced: \texttt{callpar.F90}:1165 sets
\texttt{AUXL\%ZCCN}$=0$ when \texttt{NAERCLD}$\le0$; \texttt{cloud\_layer.F90}:191 passes
that array to \texttt{CLOUDSC} as \texttt{PCCN}; \texttt{cloudsc.F90}:2050 then evaluates
$(\texttt{RCCN}/\texttt{PCCN})^{0.333}$. Using the lifetime effect requires
\texttt{NAERCLD}$>0$ and prognostic aerosol, which also changes the direct effect --- so it
stops being one lever. \textbf{Recorded so nobody enables the switch.}

\subsection{The missing term, and that the data already exists}\label{sub:r24emdms}
Genthon is sea-salt-from-wind with \emph{no biogenic term}, and MACv2-SP --- the only other
live CCN path --- is anthropogenic plumes only. Natural marine biogenic sulphate is absent
from this model by construction, which is exactly why the seasonal phase is wrong.

\textbf{The input already ships with OpenIFS and nothing reads it:}

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\toprule
\texttt{climate.v020/95\_4/month\_dms} & \texttt{emdms}, paramId \textbf{210043} \\
units & kg\,m$^{-2}$\,s$^{-1}$ (DMS surface \emph{emission}) \\
grid & N96 reduced Gaussian, 40\,320 points --- \textbf{TCO95's own grid} \\
time & 12 monthly fields \\
land & \textbf{$-99$ sentinel} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent It exists at every resolution from TL21 to TCO1279. It is not ingested:
\texttt{ICMCL} carries only \texttt{al}, \texttt{aluvp/d}, \texttt{alnip/d},
\texttt{lai\_lv/hv}, and OpenIFS references DMS only in the TM5/M7 chemistry paths, which
need \texttt{NACTAERO}$\neq0$.

\textbf{Route, via ocp-tool rather than \texttt{mkclimr.sh}.} ocp-tool already owns
\texttt{ICMCL} (\texttt{config.py}:180--186, \texttt{paleo\_input.py}:278--320,
\texttt{run\_ocp\_tool.py}:189 step 14b) and, decisively, already applies a changed
land--sea mask to it. \texttt{emdms} is an \emph{ocean} field with the opposite land
convention to albedo and LAI, so under a moved coastline new ocean points need infill and
new land points need $-99$. \texttt{mkclimr.sh} cannot do that; ocp-tool can, which is what
makes the field paleo-safe.

\begin{enumerate}\itemsep2pt
\item ocp-tool: append \texttt{emdms} to \texttt{ICMCL} in deterministic order ---
\texttt{ece\_updclie\_climr.F90}:233 aborts on ``Inconsistent order of fields''.
\item ocp-tool: extend step 14b for the inverted land convention.
\item Reader: one new \texttt{paramId} case (210043) beside the existing 174 / 15--18 /
66--67 branches. \textbf{\texttt{max\_fields}$=10$ against 7 in use}, so there is headroom
for an eighth field without touching the limit.
\item Physics: an additive biogenic term on \texttt{PCCNO} in the \texttt{LCCNO} branch.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{The interface is a DMS surface flux in kg\,m$^{-2}$\,s$^{-1}$}, which is exactly
what a REcoM DMS module would produce. So a prescribed climatology now and a prognostic
source later are a drop-in swap at the ocp-tool boundary, with no change to items 3--4.
That is a better seam than a chlorophyll relation, which would need its own empirical fit
on both sides.

\emph{Unpriced, all cheap:} the provenance of ECMWF's \texttt{emdms} (Kettle \& Andreae
2000 or Lana et al.\ 2011 --- matters for citation); the flux$\to$CCN relation, which is the
one genuinely scientific choice and needs a published basis, not a fitted curve; and
whether $-99$ is the sentinel everywhere or only where it was sampled.

\emph{Why this is not scheduled first:} the SO band is $9.8\,\%$ of the globe, so closing
the opacity third perfectly is $2.57$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ in-band but only
${\approx}0.25$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ global, against a $+1.23$ error. 11E is a namelist edit on a
lever already validated over 44 AMIP years. Do the cheap decisive run first.

\dead{Prognostic marine biogenic CCN needs the \texttt{-cc} setup with REcoM ocean
biogeochemistry.} REcoM carries no DMS tracer; the only DMS in the tree is Icepack's
sea-ice biogeochemistry (\texttt{dmspp, dmspd, dms}), which is DMS \emph{within} sea ice and
is in a component this configuration does not run (\texttt{whichevp}$=1$, FESOM's native
EVP). And there is no coupling field or atmospheric sulphur chemistry to receive it. The
prescribed \texttt{emdms} route needs none of that.

\subsection{T1 read test: passed, and the \texttt{emdms} data characterised}\label{sub:r24t1}
The 1-year read test completed with no abort and the reader's own diagnostics confirm every
pass criterion: \texttt{Found 12 dates}, \texttt{Found 8 fields}, \texttt{emdms} discovered as
the eighth GRIB message matching \texttt{NCLIGC(8)}, and \texttt{CLIMR} slot 8 filled. Slot 8
takes \textbf{twelve distinct values through the model year}, following a clean seasonal
march on rank 1's northern high-latitude strip: $1.13$ in January, peaking at $3.04$ in June,
minimum $0.30$ in October, returning to $1.13$. \textbf{The DMS ingest works.}

\textbf{The data, characterised at full precision} (source file and built \texttt{ICMCL} are
identical, so the ocp-tool step is also verified):

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r}
\toprule
land sentinel ($-99$) & $29.9\,\%$ of points \\
ocean values & min $0.043$, median $2.05$, max $53.25$ \\
exact zeros & $240$ of $339\,360$ ($0.1\,\%$) \\
\midrule
SO 65--45S, September & $0.52$ \\
SO 65--45S, December & $\mathbf{5.06}$ \\
\textbf{austral seasonal ratio} & $\mathbf{9.8\times}$ \\
tropics, all months & $2.0$--$3.1$ (flat) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The phase is exactly the missing term.} Model marine CCN is wind-driven and
so minimises in DJF ($N_d=68.5$) and peaks in JJA ($76.1$), anti-phased with observation.
\texttt{emdms} peaks in DJF at $9.8\times$ its September value --- largest precisely when and
where the SO cloud error is worst (DJF $-6.94$, SON $-7.03$\,pp).

\dead{\texttt{emdms} is a DMS surface \emph{emission flux} in kg\,m$^{-2}$\,s$^{-1}$, which is
exactly what a REcoM DMS module would produce, so a prescribed climatology now and a
prognostic source later are a drop-in swap.} The GRIB carries that unit label, but values of
${\sim}2$\,kg\,m$^{-2}$\,s$^{-1}$ of DMS are physically absurd. A global mean of $2.36$ with
maxima of $53$ matches \textbf{DMS seawater \emph{concentration} in nmol\,L$^{-1}$} --- Lana
et al.\ (2011) put the global surface mean at ${\approx}2.3$. \textbf{The unit label is
wrong.} That inserts a step the round-24 design did not price: a sea--air flux
parameterisation (wind-dependent piston velocity, Nightingale or Wanninkhof) is needed
\emph{before} any flux$\to$CCN relation. The interface argument survives --- REcoM would also
produce a concentration --- but the flux calculation belongs on the atmosphere side.

\emph{Two diagnostic traps recorded, both mine.} The reader printed paramIds with an
\texttt{i5} format, so the 6-digit $210043$ came out as \texttt{*****}; widened to \texttt{i8}.
And the field was twice declared broken from too small a sample --- once from an \texttt{awk}
column error that reported the ocean maximum as $0$, once from reading four \emph{consecutive}
timesteps, which all fall in the same month, and concluding the field never varied. Both were
wrong; the sampling was.

\subsection{T2: the sea--air flux, validated in the model}\label{sub:r24t2}
\S\ref{sub:r24t1} established that \texttt{emdms} is a \emph{concentration}, so a flux has to
be formed before the field can drive anything --- and the flux is what must be validated
first. Implemented as a diagnostic in \texttt{surfrad\_layer.F90}, where the DMS field, the
model's own instantaneous wind and the surface temperature are all already in scope:

\begin{align*}
\mathrm{Sc} &= 2674.0 - 147.12\,T + 3.726\,T^2 - 0.038\,T^3 &&\text{Saltzman et al.\ (1993)}\\
k_{600} &= 0.222\,u^2 + 0.333\,u \ \ [\mathrm{cm\,hr^{-1}}] &&\text{Nightingale et al.\ (2000)}\\
k &= k_{600}\sqrt{600/\mathrm{Sc}}\,/\,360000 \ \ [\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}] \\
F &= k\,C_w\times10^{-6} \ \ [\mathrm{mol\,m^{-2}\,s^{-1}}]
\end{align*}

\noindent with $1$\,nmol\,L$^{-1}=10^{-6}$\,mol\,m$^{-3}$. The wind is the model's
