instantaneous lowest-level speed, \emph{not} a monthly mean --- $k$ is quadratic in $u$, and
\S\ref{sub:r24nd} showed monthly vector averaging halves the Southern Ocean wind.

\textbf{The pass criterion was set before running.} Hand-evaluating the chain at
$C_w=2$\,nmol\,L$^{-1}$, $u=7$\,m\,s$^{-1}$, $T=15$\,\textdegree C gives $k_{600}=13.2$,
$\mathrm{Sc}=1177$, $k=2.6\times10^{-5}$\,m\,s$^{-1}$ and
$F=5.2\times10^{-11}$\,mol\,m$^{-2}$\,s$^{-1}$; scaled by an ocean area of
$3.6\times10^{14}$\,m$^2$, $3.156\times10^{7}$\,s and $32.06$\,g\,mol$^{-1}$ that is
${\approx}19$\,Tg\,S\,yr$^{-1}$, against a published $18$--$34$ (Lana et al.\ 2011 give
$28.1$). So the model should print a mean flux of order
$10^{-11}$--$10^{-10}$\,mol\,m$^{-2}$\,s$^{-1}$.

The run completed a full year, $8760$ steps, no abort, $70\,088$ diagnostics
(8 blocks $\times$ 8760).

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r}
\toprule
flux, minimum & $3.78\times10^{-13}$ \\
flux, median & $2.33\times10^{-11}$ \\
\textbf{flux, mean} & $\mathbf{3.52\times10^{-11}}$ \\
flux, maximum & $3.31\times10^{-10}$ \\
\midrule
\textbf{predicted window} & $\mathbf{10^{-11}}$--$\mathbf{10^{-10}}$ \\
indicative global budget & $12.8$\,Tg\,S\,yr$^{-1}$ \\
published & $18$--$34$ (Lana et al.\ 2011: $28.1$) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The unit chain is correct.} The mean flux sits inside the predicted window,
and it varies while $C_w$ is uniform on a block --- so the variation is coming from wind and
temperature through $k$, which is the intended behaviour.

\emph{Three limits, stated so the number is not over-read.} The diagnostic covers rank~1's
blocks, a northern high-latitude strip, not the globe. Land points enter as zero because the
flux clamps $C_w$ at $0$ to reject the $-99$ sentinel, which drags the sampled mean down. And
the $12.8$ is that non-global mean scaled as though it applied over all ocean. Landing within
a factor of ${\approx}2$ of $28.1$ on so crude an extrapolation is a \emph{pass} for a unit
test --- the purpose was to catch an error of orders of magnitude, not to measure the budget.
A real budget needs a global reduction the diagnostic does not do.

\textbf{The run changes no physics.} The flux is computed and printed, deliberately not wired
into \texttt{PCCNO}, because the flux$\to$CCN step is a separate scientific choice that needs
a published relation rather than a fitted constant.

\subsection{T3: DMS-Rev3 ingested through ocp-tool}\label{sub:r24t3}
The shipped \texttt{month\_dms} is Lana et al.\ (2011). DMS-Rev3 (Hulswar et al.\ 2022)
rests on $865\,109$ observations against L11's $47\,313$, and the paper states the largest
revisions are in the polar oceans --- the only region this campaign is looking at.
Measured on the model's own grid after regridding, SO 65--45S:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
SO 65--45S & L11 & DMS-Rev3 & change \\
\midrule
annual & $1.93$ & $2.24$ & $+16\,\%$ \\
DJF & $4.23$ & $4.36$ & $+3\,\%$ \\
\textbf{SON} & $\mathbf{1.34}$ & $\mathbf{2.02}$ & $\mathbf{+51\,\%}$ \\
\textbf{November} & $\mathbf{2.18}$ & $\mathbf{4.24}$ & $\mathbf{+95\,\%}$ \\
global maximum & $53.25$ & $26.56$ & $-50\,\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{Rev3 does not raise the summer peak, it broadens it} --- the bloom starts
a month earlier --- and it halves the maximum, which is the paper's ``reduced patchiness''.
The model's SO shortwave CRE error is worst in SON ($-7.03$\,pp) and DJF ($-6.94$), so the
upgrade lands in the season the old climatology was weakest.

\textbf{Units derived, not read.} \texttt{DMS\_REV3.nc} carries no attributes at all.
Integrating the shipped \texttt{DMSflux} globally gives $28.19$\,Tg\,S\,yr$^{-1}$
(\texttt{DMSfluxSI} $28.36$) against a published $27.1$--$28.1$, which fixes the flux as
mol\,m$^{-2}$\,d$^{-1}$ and the concentration as nmol\,L$^{-1}$; every other assumption is
out by orders of magnitude. Only the \emph{concentration} is ingested --- the shipped flux
uses Rev3's own wind climatology, whereas the model must form the flux online from its own
wind, which is quadratic in speed. Their flux is retained as a validation target.

\textbf{Built through ocp-tool} (\texttt{dms\_rev3\_to\_grib.py}), which is what makes it
paleo-safe. Nearest \emph{valid} neighbour in 3-D --- 1\textdegree\ source against
${\approx}0.9$\textdegree\ target is matched resolution, so nothing is invented, no land
NaN bleeds into coastal ocean, and the dateline and poles cannot yield a spurious
neighbour. Values are overwritten into the shipped messages, so grid, \texttt{dataDate},
\texttt{paramId} and all headers stay byte-identical to what the reader accepts. The land
sentinel is taken from the template, preserving the model's own mask; a Rev3 hole at a
template ocean point would read as land, so L11 is retained there and counted --- for TCO95
that count is \textbf{zero}, with $12\,040$ land points held at $-99$.
\texttt{icmcl\_dms.py} then interleaves it: $84\to96$ messages, 12 dates $\times$ 8 fields.

\textbf{T3, one year, verified.} \texttt{Found 12 dates}, \texttt{Found 8 fields}, no
ordering abort, full year, no abort. Against T2 on the identical wind field:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
 & $C_w$ [nmol\,L$^{-1}$] & flux mean & indicative global \\
\midrule
L11 & $1.825$ & $3.515\times10^{-11}$ & $12.8$\,Tg\,S\,yr$^{-1}$ \\
\textbf{DMS-Rev3} & $\mathbf{2.044}$ & $\mathbf{4.218\times10^{-11}}$ & $15.4$ \\
ratio & $1.120$ & $\mathbf{1.200}$ & \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The flux rises by $20\,\%$ where the concentration rises by only
$12\,\%$.} That is not an inconsistency --- the flux is $\overline{k\,C_w}$, not
$\bar{k}\,\overline{C_w}$, and $k$ varies spatially, so the ratio of means need only agree
if the spatial pattern is unchanged. It means \textbf{Rev3 places more DMS where the wind
is stronger}, a positive $C_w$--$k$ covariance relative to L11, and the flux is what feeds
CCN.

\emph{Caveat on the $12.8\to15.4$.} That is rank~1's blocks --- a northern high-latitude
strip with land entering as zero --- not a global budget. Rev3 and L11 have near-identical
\emph{global} fluxes ($28.19$ against a published $28.1$), so the $+20\,\%$ is regional
sampling, consistent with the polar oceans being where Rev3 revised most, and is not a
global statement.

\subsection{Screening at one year: measured, with the threshold that makes it
safe}\label{sub:r24screen}
Project issue \#170 found the cy48 high-cloud offset already present in year~1 while the T2m
bias and the SO low-cloud dip were not, and concluded that one-year runs would suffice to
test tuning parameters. That claim is about \texttt{hcc}; the campaign's Southern Ocean
metric is SW CRE, a different quantity with different noise, so the transfer was measured
rather than assumed.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r l}
\toprule
lever & 44-yr $\Delta$ & \textbf{year-1 $\Delta$} & ratio & screens at 1\,yr? \\
\midrule
A1a \texttt{ovlliqice 0.10} & $-6.44$ & $\mathbf{-6.56}$ & $1.02$ & \textbf{yes} \\
D2a \texttt{inpsea 0.2} & $-4.80$ & $\mathbf{-5.23}$ & $1.09$ & \textbf{yes} \\
D2b \texttt{inp+p700} & $-2.61$ & $\mathbf{-2.01}$ & $0.77$ & \textbf{yes} \\
A1b \texttt{ovlliqice 0.35} & $-2.17$ & $-1.74$ & $0.80$ & no \\
B3 \texttt{clddiff 1.5e-5} & $+0.67$ & $-0.17$ & $\mathbf{-0.25}$ & no --- \emph{wrong sign} \\
B7 \texttt{rvice 0.22} & $+0.66$ & $+1.22$ & $1.85$ & no \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Control interannual scatter in SO SW CRE is $0.712$\,W\,m$^{-2}$, so a \emph{pair}
of single years resolves $\pm1.97$ against $\pm0.30$ for a 44-year pair. \textbf{The claim
holds, with a threshold: a lever screens at one year if its effect exceeds
${\approx}2$\,W\,m$^{-2}$, and is unreliable below it} --- B3 comes out with the
\emph{opposite sign} at one year.

\textbf{Why this is worth having.} Closing the SO amount deficit requires
${\approx}4.9$\,W\,m$^{-2}$, so any lever large enough to matter would show at one year. A
1-year AMIP leg is ${\approx}11$ minutes against $9.5$ hours for the 44-year standard, so
candidate triage is ${\approx}50\times$ cheaper and only survivors need full length. Round 23
spent four 48-year submissions on levers that could have been screened in 45 minutes
together.

\textbf{What it does NOT license.} Temperature. \#170's own lead/lag result is that T2m and
the SO low-cloud dip lag the cloud change, and the campaign's seasonal thresholds
(DJF $\pm0.588$\,K on 44 years) are far beyond a 1-year window. \textbf{Screen the radiative
response at one year; never adopt on one.}

\section{Round 25 (2026--08--09): the tropopause is not displaced, and cold-and-dry
share one entry point}\label{sec:r25}
No runs. Round 22 found the cold bias extratropical, peaking at 200\,hPa over both poles and
reversing sign at 100\,hPa, and read that shape as a displaced tropopause. It is not, and
what is there instead is better news.

\subsection{The tropopause height is right}\label{sub:r25height}
WMO lapse-rate tropopause, model and ERA5 on identical levels, annual, period-clean:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r}
\toprule
band & model [hPa] & ERA5 [hPa] & diff \\
\midrule
90S--60S & $250$ & $250$ & $0$ \\
60S--30S & $200$ & $200$ & $0$ \\
tropics & $100$ & $100$ & $0$ \\
30N--60N & $200$ & $200$ & $0$ \\
60N--90N & $250$ & $250$ & $0$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\dead{The sign reversal at 100\,hPa is a displaced tropopause: the model is still on a
tropospheric lapse rate at 200\,hPa where the real atmosphere has turned isothermal.}
\textbf{Falsified.} The tropopause sits on the same level in every band. The vertical
resolution here is one level, so a displacement smaller than ${\sim}50$\,hPa would not be
seen --- but a displacement large enough to produce a $4$\,K bias would be.

What the lapse rates show instead is a \emph{sharper} transition, not a moved one: the model
cools faster than ERA5 through 300--250\,hPa ($+1.76$ and $+1.78$\,K\,km$^{-1}$ at the two
poles, against $+0.21$ in the tropics) and then slower than ERA5 above 200\,hPa
($-1.49$, $-1.85$). Same tropopause, too much curvature at it.

\subsection{The cold point, and one caveat on reading it}\label{sub:r25coldpoint}
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r}
\toprule
band & $T$ model & $T$ ERA5 & $\Delta T$ & $p$ model & $p$ ERA5 \\
\midrule
90S--60S & $208.77$ & $212.03$ & $-3.26$ & $250$ & $70$ \\
60S--30S & $211.80$ & $213.32$ & $-1.52$ & $100$ & $100$ \\
\textbf{tropics} & $\mathbf{193.44}$ & $\mathbf{195.51}$ & $\mathbf{-2.07}$ & $100$ & $100$ \\
30N--60N & $211.54$ & $212.84$ & $-1.30$ & $100$ & $100$ \\
60N--90N & $213.21$ & $217.55$ & $-4.33$ & $250$ & $30$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The two polar rows are not like-for-like and must not be quoted as
$4$\,K cold points.} Model and ERA5 locate their profile minimum at different levels there
($250$ against $70$ and $30$\,hPa), because at high latitudes the upper stratosphere is
near-isothermal and the minimum is ambiguous. What those rows actually say is a structural
difference: the model's polar stratosphere is too \emph{cold} at 250\,hPa and too
\emph{warm} above 100 (\S\ref{sub:utls} gives $+0.25$ and $+0.26$ there). The three rows
where both datasets pick the same level are clean, and the tropical one carries the next
result.

\subsection{Cold and dry are one problem with one entry point}\label{sub:r25onecause}
Stratospheric water vapour is set by the temperature of the tropical cold point, which
freeze-dries air entering the stratosphere; the Brewer--Dobson circulation then carries that
air poleward. So a cold-biased tropical cold point dries the stratosphere \emph{everywhere},
including over the poles. Testing it quantitatively with Clausius--Clapeyron over ice
(Murphy \& Koop 2005) on the saturation mixing ratio at each dataset's own cold point:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r}
\toprule
tropical cold point, model & $193.44$\,K at $100$\,hPa \\
tropical cold point, ERA5 & $195.51$\,K at $100$\,hPa \\
saturation mixing ratio & $3.575$ vs $4.998$ (ppmv-mass) \\
\midrule
\textbf{predicted entry-air drying} & $\mathbf{-28.5\,\%}$ \\
\textbf{measured drying at 100\,hPa} & $\mathbf{-46.9\,\%}$ \\
\midrule
share explained & $\mathbf{61\,\%}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{A $2$\,K cold bias at the tropical cold point accounts for three fifths of
a $47\,\%$ stratospheric dry bias.} That reframes \S\ref{sub:utls}, which recorded cold and
dry as a compensating \emph{pair} --- two independent errors whose opposing OLR effects
happen to cancel. They are not independent. They share an entry point, and the polar UTLS
dryness is the tropical cold point seen downstream.

\textbf{Why that is much better news.} Two independent cancelling errors would have to be
fixed together or not at all, and fixing either alone moves net TOA several W\,m$^{-2}$ the
wrong way (\S\ref{sub:utls}). One error with one entry point means \textbf{a lever that warms
the tropical cold point should move both}, in the right direction, at once. The remaining
$39\,\%$ of the drying is a second source --- transport, or the model's own UTLS
microphysics --- and is not yet identified.

\emph{Caveat carried forward:} ERA5's upper-level humidity is weakly constrained by
observation, so the $-46.9\,\%$ denominator is the softest number here. The $-2.07$\,K cold
point and the $-28.5\,\%$ it implies rest only on temperature, which ERA5 constrains well.

\section{Round 26 (2026--08--09$\to$10): marine biogenic CCN wired and priced, and the
tropical longwave becomes the gate}\label{sec:r26}
Round 24 traced the marine CCN path end to end and found the DMS field read but
\emph{unused}. This round wires it into the radiation, brackets the one free coefficient
across its published range, and reaches a verdict that changes the shape of the remaining
campaign: the Southern Ocean lever works, and the tropical longwave deficit is what stops
it being adopted. The two are no longer separate items on the open list.

\subsection{The wiring, and why it is default-off}\label{sub:r26wire}
In \texttt{radpar.F90}, immediately after \texttt{SURFRAD} fills \texttt{PCCNO} from
Genthon (1992) sea salt, ocean points gain a DMS term:
Saltzman et al.\ (1993) for the Schmidt number, Nightingale et al.\ (2000) for the
transfer velocity $k_{600}=0.222u^2+0.333u$, flux $F=kC_w$, and
\[
  \texttt{PCCNO} \;\mathrel{+}=\; S \cdot F_S , \qquad
  S \equiv \texttt{ECE\_DMS\_CCN\_SENS},\quad F_S \ \text{in mg\,S\,m}^{-2}\text{d}^{-1}.
\]
\texttt{PCCNO} then flows to \texttt{radiation\_scheme}, through the Martin et al.\ (1994)
quadratic to droplet number and effective radius --- the Twomey effect. The input is
DMS-Rev3 (Hulswar et al.\ 2022), ingested through ocp-tool and verified in T3
(\S\ref{sub:r24t3}).

$S$ is a \texttt{NAMECECFG} namelist knob defaulting to $0.0$, and the ICMCL field is
registered only when \texttt{ECE\_CLIMR\_DMS} is set, so \texttt{NUMFLDS} is unchanged and
\textbf{every existing configuration reproduces bit-for-bit with the switch off}. That is
what allows one library to serve both arms of every pair below.

\textbf{Two near-misses worth recording.} The first attempt wired CCN into
\texttt{surfrad\_layer}, which would have been a \textbf{no-op}: the radiation's CCN comes
from \texttt{radpar}, where \texttt{PCCNO} is \texttt{INTENT(OUT)} and any earlier value is
overwritten. The second was a build failure, \texttt{error \#6404}, diagnosed twice as a
stale \texttt{.mod} and once as a CMake dependency-scanner gap --- \emph{all three wrong}. A
full clean \texttt{esm\_master recomp-} reproduced it. \texttt{nm} showed the symbol present
in the object file: the OpenIFS modules declare \texttt{PRIVATE} by default and export an
explicit list, so a new module variable is invisible at every \texttt{USE} site until it is
added to \texttt{PUBLIC}. \textbf{Check accessibility before blaming the build system, and
diagnose with \texttt{nm} rather than by theory.}

\subsection{U1, the kill test at the top of the published range}\label{sub:r26u1}
Woodhouse et al.\ (2010) measure $\mathrm{d}\mathrm{CCN}/\mathrm{d}F_{\mathrm{DMS}}$ anywhere
from $-43$ to $+166$\,cm$^{-3}$ per mg\,S\,m$^{-2}$d$^{-1}$ depending on season and
hemisphere. That range is too wide to pick a number from, so the coefficient was made a
namelist knob and the range \emph{sampled}, starting at the most optimistic end. Control is
T3: identical DMS-Rev3 input, identical winds, $S=0$.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r}
\toprule
$S=166$, 1 yr & DJF & MAM & JJA & SON \\
\midrule
SO 65--45S $\Delta$SW CRE & $\mathbf{-7.25}$ & $+0.03$ & $-0.63$ & $-1.14$ \\
tropics $\Delta$SW CRE & $-1.56$ & $-2.17$ & $-2.44$ & $-2.39$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Annual: SO $-2.25$, tropics $-2.14$, global $-1.72$\,W\,m$^{-2}$. December alone
is $-8.54$ and January $-7.34$.

\textbf{The lever is real and it is seasonally selective in exactly the right way.} The SO
DJF deficit is $+10.86$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ and this closes $67\,\%$ of it, in the season the
deficit is worst, at a DJF tropical cost of only $-1.56$ --- $4.6\!:\!1$ in our favour.
\textbf{The selectivity is the bloom's, not a tuned parameter}; no hemispheric constant was
invented to produce it. That also confirms round 24's phase argument quantitatively: the
model's SO droplet number was right in the mean but inverted in phase, and this is the
correction.

The annual means look like a wash only because the SO gain is concentrated in one season
while the tropical cost is flat year-round.

\subsection{U2 and U3: the bracket, and what one year can actually
resolve}\label{sub:r26bracket}
Two more arms at $S=43$ (the published floor) and $S=90$ (the midpoint), to read an
operating point off a curve instead of guessing a fourth value. Detection thresholds were
computed first, per band, from the control's own interannual scatter as
$1.96\,\sigma\sqrt{2}$ for a pair of single years --- the construction that gives the
already-known $\pm1.97$ for SO SW CRE:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r}
\toprule
metric & $\sigma$ (interannual) & 1-yr pair, $95\,\%$ \\
\midrule
SO SW CRE, annual & $0.712$ & $\pm1.97$ \\
SO SW CRE, DJF & $1.379$ & $\pm3.82$ \\
tropics SW CRE, annual & $0.357$ & $\pm0.99$ \\
tropics SW CRE, DJF & $0.441$ & $\pm1.22$ \\
global SW CRE, annual & $0.402$ & $\pm1.11$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{r r r r l}
\toprule
$S$ & SO annual & SO DJF & tropics annual & resolved? \\
\midrule
$43$ & $-0.32$ & $-1.78$ & $-0.76$ & nothing \\
$90$ & $-0.38$ & $-1.22$ & $-1.11$ & tropics only, marginally \\
$\mathbf{166}$ & $\mathbf{-2.25}$ & $\mathbf{-7.25}$ & $\mathbf{-2.14}$ & \textbf{all three} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent \textbf{The bracket produced exactly one usable point.} SO DJF is not even
monotonic in $S$ ($-1.78$, $-1.22$, $-7.25$), which is the signature of the two sub-floor
arms being internal variability rather than signal.

\dead{The tropical response is monotonic and clean at $0.0124$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ per unit $S$,
fitted through three arms.} \textbf{Withdrawn the same day.} Two of those three points sit
below the $\pm0.99$ tropical threshold, so the fit was reading a line through noise. The
analysis script now refuses to interpolate the SO points and guards the tropical
extrapolation explicitly --- \texttt{np.interp} would have \emph{clamped} to the sampled
range and returned a confident wrong answer. The same paragraph also treated the
$\pm0.5$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ tropical guardrail as though it were a detection threshold. It is not:
it is a \emph{tolerance}, set because the tropics are only $-0.67$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ from CERES
period-clean.

\subsection{The verdict, and why scaling cannot rescue it}\label{sub:r26verdict}
From the single resolved arm the tropical cost is $2.14/166 = 0.0129$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ per unit
$S$, so the $\pm0.5$ tolerance binds at $S\approx39$ --- \textbf{below Woodhouse's published
floor of 43}. Twomey scales with $\Delta\ln N_d$, so the response is \emph{sub}-linear and
the per-unit cost at low $S$ is if anything larger; the bound holds a fortiori. Third branch
of the pre-registered verdict logic: \textbf{no coefficient in the published range works as
a tuning knob.}

\textbf{Why the tropics respond more per unit $\Delta$CCN, and why that is not a bug.}
Twomey goes as $\Delta\ln N_d$, and tropical background CCN ($\sim52$\,cm$^{-3}$) is
\emph{lower} than the Southern Ocean's ($\sim78$), so the same absolute increment is a larger
fractional change there. Physically the model has \emph{no} marine biogenic CCN anywhere ---
Genthon is sea salt from wind, MACv2-SP is anthropogenic plumes --- so adding it in the
tropics is \emph{more} correct, not less. What fails is a tuning tolerance, not the physics.
And because both ends scale together, no choice of $S$ separates them.

\subsection{The tropical longwave deficit is now the gate}\label{sub:r26gate}
This reframes the open list. The two remaining regional failures act through
\emph{different} channels (\S\ref{sub:toadecomp2}): the Southern Ocean through SW cloud
($+7.45$), the tropics through LW cloud ($-2.16$). The tropical cost of the DMS lever is a
\emph{shortwave} cost; the tropical bias is a \emph{longwave} one. Those are independent
terms, so:

\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The tropical LW deficit is no longer a parallel item --- it gates the Southern
Ocean fix.} Close it and the SW cost of DMS CCN becomes affordable, and the best SO lever
this campaign has found goes in unchanged.
\item \textbf{The pair is complementary in the global budget.} DMS CCN \emph{removes}
$1.72$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ globally, which helps the $+0.52$ imbalance; closing the tropical LW
deficit puts roughly $+1$ back once the tropics are area-weighted. Together they fix both
regional biases and partly cancel globally --- a better position than either alone, since
the DMS lever's problem was never its SO effect but its global reach.
\item \textbf{The requirement on the LW lever is sharper than ``acts through the
longwave''.} \texttt{RVICE} is the counter-example: an ice fall speed applies wherever there
is cloud ice, so it \emph{trades} SO high cloud against tropical anvils, and it was raised
$0.13\to0.16$ precisely to suppress SO \texttt{hcc} (project issues \#169/\#170). A usable
lever must be keyed on \textbf{deep convection}, which barely exists over the Southern
Ocean, so that selectivity comes from where convection happens rather than from a latitude
band --- the same structural trick that made the DMS lever seasonally selective.
\end{itemize}

\noindent The cheap check comes first: ${\sim}52$ arms have been scored on SO SW and global
RMSE and \emph{none} on the tropical LW term. \texttt{tropical\_lw\_scan.py} is written to
answer that from data already on disk before any new run is spent; it has not yet been run.

\textbf{Not Blomquist.} The standing todo to switch the transfer velocity from the
Nightingale quadratic to Blomquist et al.\ (2017) should be dropped for this purpose: the
DMS-specific scheme is \emph{less} steep in wind, which shifts flux away from the windy
Southern Ocean toward the calm tropics. It would make selectivity worse, not better.

\subsection{Two coupled runs, on a campaign-branch coupled build}\label{sub:r26coupled}
The coupled tree was on \texttt{movcav-landice+co2-concdriven}, which carries neither the
snow-depletion knobs nor the CCN wiring. The campaign branch is a \emph{strict descendant}
of that branch's tip (\texttt{f3ccacb} is an ancestor), so it contains every coupled commit,
and every campaign feature is namelist-gated off by default --- switching the tree in place
removes nothing and changes no existing configuration's behaviour. One clean OpenIFS rebuild
then serves both runs. Six files completing the DMS ICMCL chain, plus the
\texttt{RCLCRIT\_SEA}/\texttt{RCLCRIT\_LAND} exposure, had been left uncommitted in the AMIP
tree; U1--U3 ran against them, so the library had them and only the commit was missing.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\toprule
run & change vs.\ its control & question \\
\midrule
\textbf{11E} & 11D $+$ \texttt{SWEMIN} $30\to15$ $+$ K1 & is the coupled configuration level
with AMIP? \\
\textbf{11F} & 11E $+$ \texttt{ECE\_DMS\_CCN\_SENS}$=166$ & does coupling change the
Southern Ocean answer? \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Both are 50 years, both submitted 2026--08--10. \textbf{11F is deliberately not a
tuning candidate} --- AMIP has already said no published $S$ clears the tolerance. It runs at
the only setting AMIP could resolve, to answer what AMIP structurally cannot: with SST and
sea ice free to respond, does brightening Southern Ocean cloud in bloom season move the
coupled SO state and the TOA imbalance by more than the tropical cost? Fixed SSTs suppress
exactly the feedback that makes an SO cloud change matter. Fifty years also prices the
tropical response with feedbacks and proper statistics instead of from one AMIP year.

\section{Synthesis: the causal web, and what is still dark}\label{sec:causal}
This section is the campaign's current best account of \emph{why} the boreal cold bias
exists, assembled from every round. It is written to be falsifiable: each link carries a
status marker, and the unknowns are listed rather than glossed.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\ding{51}\,\textbf{OBS} & established against observation (CERES, Rutgers/IMS satellite) \\
\ding{108}\,\textbf{MOD} & established only model-vs-model (ERA5 = HTESSEL sibling); suggestive, not authoritative \\
\ding{55}\,\textbf{DEAD} & tested and falsified \\
\textbf{?} & unknown --- no evidence either way \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{The picture}\label{sub:causalmap}
\begin{footnotesize}
\begin{verbatim}
                    ROUTE A  (transpiration - cloud)        ROUTE B  (snow - albedo)
                    ===============================         ========================

   RVRSMIN too low                                       ?  unknown Mar-Apr energy sink
   (tundra 80 s/m,                                          |
    lowest in table)  OBS-adjacent: table-internal           v
          |                                              melt-out 13 days late      OBS
          v                                                  |
   over-transpiration                        MOD               v
          |                                              Jun snow cover 2x observed OBS
          +--> too much latent cooling                        |
          |                                                   v
          +--> moist boundary layer                      Jun surface albedo +0.046  OBS
                     |                                        |
                     v                                        v
             Siberian JJA cloud +8.5 pp     OBS          -5.6 W/m2 surface SW       OBS
                     |                                        |
                     v                                        |  ? does this reach T2m?
             -7.0 W/m2 surface SW           OBS                |    G4 gained +0.95 K with
                     |                                        |    snow UNCHANGED, so the
                     v                                        v    link may be weak
             ================  Siberian JJA T2m ~2.0 K too cold  ================
                                          |
                                          |  (AMIP: severed deliberately)
                                          v
                              boreal forest fails to establish
                                          |
                                          v
                              tundra replaces forest -> brighter, smoother
                                          |
                                          +-------> colder  (runaway, coupled only)

   G4 CLOSES ROUTE A:  cloud term -7.04 -> +1.20 W/m2, T2m +0.952 K, 48% of the bias
   ROUTE B UNTOUCHED:  albedo term -5.45 -> -5.59 W/m2, and G4 makes the snow WORSE
\end{verbatim}
\end{footnotesize}

\subsection{Route A --- transpiration to cloud: closed}\label{sub:routea}
\textbf{Status: the strongest \emph{measured} result of the campaign --- but the mechanism is
only partly established.} The chain as proposed is: excess stomatal conductance cools the
surface latently \emph{and moistens the boundary layer}; the moisture becomes low cloud; the
cloud removes shortwave; the surface cools.

\textcolor{red}{\textbf{The moisture step is contradicted by this report's own profiles.}}
\S\ref{sub:vprof} finds the excess boreal cloud is \emph{thermally} driven, not
moisture-driven: Siberian RH is $+5.6$ to $+8.2\,\%$ too high while $q$ is \emph{too low}
($-0.2$ to $-0.3$\,g\,kg$^{-1}$). What was measured is the \emph{correlation} F4 $\to$ less
cloud $\to$ warmer; the moisture pathway is \textbf{[?]}, not \ding{51}\,OBS.

\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \ding{51}\,\textbf{OBS} Siberian JJA cloud fraction was $78.1\,\%$ against CERES
  $69.6\,\%$, and the cloud share of the SW deficit was $-7.04$\,W\,m$^{-2}$
  (\S\ref{sub:r14res}).
\item \ding{51}\,\textbf{OBS} G4 removes it: cloud term $-7.04 \to +1.20$\,W\,m$^{-2}$,
  worth $+0.952$\,K.
\item The lever is defensible on \emph{table-internal} grounds for tundra
  (\texttt{RVRSMIN(9)}$=80$, the lowest of any vegetated type, \S\ref{sub:tundra}) but
  \textbf{not} for needleleaf, where $250\to1000$ remains an unanchored $4\times$
  excursion that does not saturate (\S\ref{sub:r14res}).
\item \textbf{?} G4 slightly \emph{overshoots} in August ($+3.19$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ cloud
  term). A new bias in the making; watch it.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Route B --- snow to albedo: open, and the interesting one}\label{sub:routeb}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \ding{51}\,\textbf{OBS} June surface albedo $+0.046$ too high (CERES,
  \S\ref{sub:ceresalb}); June snow cover $0.380$ vs satellite $0.203$; melt-out $13$
  days late (\S\ref{sub:satsnow}).
\item \ding{108}\,\textbf{MOD} Snow \emph{accumulation} is right; the loss term is
  mistimed --- too weak in Mar--Apr, too strong in May (\S\ref{sub:melt}).
\item \ding{55}\,\textbf{DEAD} It is not the snow-\emph{cover formulation}: round 13
  changed \texttt{RQSNCR} and moved the albedo response into Sep--Nov instead
  (\S\ref{sub:hres}).
\item \ding{55}\,\textbf{DEAD} It is not an energy \emph{supply} problem: the model
  absorbs \emph{more} net SW than CERES in Mar--Apr ($+2.5$, $+1.2$), because its spring
  albedo is too \emph{low} (\S\ref{sub:ceresalb}).
\item \ding{55}\,\textbf{DEAD} It is not snow \emph{brightness}: model snow is
  \emph{darker} than both ERA5 and CERES. Brightening snow would worsen June.
\item \ding{108}\,\textbf{MOD} It is probably not cold content: \texttt{tsn} is within a
  few tenths of a K of ERA5 --- but this is model-vs-model (\S\ref{sub:noobs}).
\item \textbf{\ding{51} RESOLVED for the cover half} --- see \S\ref{sec:scf}: HTESSEL
  has no sub-grid snow depletion, so cover stays pinned near 1 while the median cell
  still holds 27\,cm in May. That explains the late melt-out and the June albedo, and
  explains why round 13 could not have worked.
\item \textbf{?} \textbf{The March--April mass deficit remains open.} ERA5-only, The absorbed shortwave is present and the snow does not melt. Candidates:
  turbulent flux to the atmosphere (\emph{unconstrained by any observation});
  conduction/refreeze within the pack; or the canopy-shaded snow tile (7) behaving
  differently from exposed snow --- the round-13 residual showed canopy masking already
  dominates in the melt months (\S\ref{sub:e5alb}).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The link that may not exist}\label{sub:weaklink}
\textbf{The most consequential uncertainty in this report.} Route B is a well-measured
\emph{radiative} bias, but its effect on \emph{temperature} is unproven:

\begin{quote}
G4 raised Siberian JJA T2m by $+0.952$\,K while May--June snow mass did \emph{not}
change (\S\ref{sub:r14res}), and while snow cover and melt date got \emph{worse}
(\S\ref{sub:satsnow}).
\end{quote}

\noindent Temperature moved a full kelvin with the snow term stationary or degrading.
Either the June albedo error is weakly coupled to T2m --- a real radiative bias that the
atmosphere largely exports --- or the two routes offset each other in a way we have not
separated. \textbf{Until this is resolved, the size of the prize in fixing Route B is
unknown}, and it is possible that closing it buys far less than its $-5.6$\,W\,m$^{-2}$
suggests. A clean test would be a run that changes the snow term alone with Route A held
fixed; round 13 attempted exactly that and failed for an unrelated reason (it moved the
wrong season).

\subsection{The coupled amplifier, deliberately severed}\label{sub:amplifier}
The AMIP configuration exists to cut the vegetation--albedo feedback so the root cause can
be hunted with the loop open. In coupled mode with LPJ-GUESS the chain closes: cold summers
$\to$ boreal forest fails to establish $\to$ tundra replaces forest $\to$ higher albedo and
lower roughness $\to$ colder. That runaway is why a $2$\,K AMIP bias becomes forest loss in
the coupled model, and it is why $48\,\%$ closure may or may not be sufficient --- \textbf{a
question AMIP structurally cannot answer.}

\textcolor{red}{\textbf{A new risk sits precisely here.}} G4 delays melt-out from 24 to
28 May (\S\ref{sub:satsnow}). Later melt is later growing-season onset for LPJ-GUESS. G4
may therefore improve AMIP T2m while \emph{harming} forest establishment through a
pathway AMIP cannot see. This should be weighed before G4 is committed to the PI spin-up,
and it is an argument for running coupled with G4 sooner rather than later.

\subsection{The energy target, now decomposed PER RUN}\label{sub:toadecomp2}
\emph{Where} the imbalance lives was settled long ago: \S\ref{sub:sobands} showed the global
budget is right \emph{by cancellation}, with the Southern Ocean carrying $+5.8$\,W\,m$^{-2}$
and contributing $+0.57$ on its own, from a cloud deficit --- the Hyder et al.\ signature.
Nothing below revises that.

What was missing is that the decomposition existed \textbf{only for the control}.
\texttt{amip\_toa\_decomp.py} never used the \texttt{RUNS} list, so across the tuning runs the
energy target was tracked by a single global number with no idea which band any lever was
moving. That is now part of the standard table and produced for every run automatically
(\S\ref{sub:evalsh}). The new information is the \textbf{response}:

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l r r r r r r}
\toprule
$\Delta$ net TOA vs control [W\,m$^{-2}$] & 60--90S & 30--60S & tropics & 30--60N & 60--90N & global \\
\midrule
\emph{control, absolute} & $-90.44$ & $-22.24$ & $+42.61$ & $-21.78$ & $-97.74$ & $+0.64$ \\
\emph{CERES} & $-98.82$ & $-21.63$ & $+45.11$ & $-24.29$ & $-97.98$ & --- \\
\emph{gap to close} & $\mathbf{+8.38}$ & $-0.61$ & $-2.50$ & $+2.51$ & $+0.24$ & \\
\midrule
A1a \texttt{ovl=0.10} & $-3.33$ & $-2.80$ & $-1.06$ & $-2.44$ & $-2.75$ & $-1.90$ \\
\textbf{D2a} \texttt{inpsea.2} & $\mathbf{-2.83}$ & $-1.91$ & $-0.38$ & $-0.79$ & $-0.81$ & $-0.93$ \\
D2b \texttt{inp+p700} & $-1.76$ & $-0.83$ & $+0.09$ & $-0.19$ & $-0.34$ & $-0.28$ \\
B3 \texttt{clddiff} & $-0.11$ & $+0.89$ & $\mathbf{+2.02}$ & $+0.86$ & $+0.25$ & $+1.34$ \\
B4 \texttt{entshalp3} & $-0.56$ & $-2.00$ & $-1.55$ & $-0.75$ & $-0.24$ & $-1.33$ \\
B5 \texttt{capdcycl0} & $-0.47$ & $+0.06$ & $\mathbf{-1.93}$ & $-0.14$ & $+0.18$ & $-1.00$ \\
F4 \texttt{rsmin1000} & $-0.40$ & $+0.04$ & $+0.07$ & $+0.30$ & $+0.38$ & $+0.10$ \\
G1 $=$ F4$+$D2b & $-2.03$ & $-0.83$ & $+0.10$ & $+0.26$ & $-0.02$ & $-0.20$ \\
\textbf{G4} $=$ G1$+$tundra & $-1.98$ & $-0.65$ & $+0.12$ & $+0.28$ & $+0.13$ & $-0.13$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Three things this view gives that the global number could not:

\textbf{(i) The 60--90S band is the most responsive in the model}, moving $-0.4$ to $-3.3$
under levers that were designed for entirely different purposes. It is not inert; it has
simply never been \emph{targeted}.

\textbf{(ii) The G-series buys about $-2.0$ of the $+8.4$ needed} there, all of it from D2b,
and D2a alone buys $-2.8$ --- but D2a costs $-0.93$ globally and was rejected on other
grounds. \emph{Roughly a quarter of the Southern Ocean TOA gap is already closed as a
by-product}, and the remainder is the same $\sim6$\,pp cloud-area deficit that no lever has
moved (\S\ref{sub:openlist}).

\textbf{(iii) The surface levers are confirmed regionally clean.} F4 moves the tropics by
$+0.07$ and G4 by $+0.12$, against B3's $+2.02$ and B5's $-1.93$. The tropical guardrail was
being checked as a single number; it is now visible as a band, and it shows the F/G family
does what it was designed to do and nothing else.

\textcolor{red}{\textbf{One trap the guardrail table invited.}} The PI target is
\textbf{net TOA $\approx 0$}, \emph{not} the CERES column. CERES is present-day (2005--2015)
and carries the real planetary warming imbalance, so it reads $\sim+1.1$\,W\,m$^{-2}$
globally: the control's $+0.64$ is \emph{too positive} even though it sits \emph{below}
CERES. Per-band CERES differences are the right way to use it --- for locating error, never
as a global tuning target.

\subsection{One command: \texttt{evaluate.sh}}\label{sub:evalsh}
The protocol had grown to five scripts run from memory, and skipping one has repeatedly
produced wrong conclusions here --- a lever promoted on 4-year noise (B8), a winter penalty
unnoticed for eleven rounds (B5), and a TOA decomposition done only for the control.
\texttt{scripts/analysis/evaluate.sh} now runs the whole protocol, with the TOA decomposition
included in the main table rather than as a separate invocation. The way to make a guardrail
effective is to make skipping it require effort.

\subsection{The open list}\label{sub:openlist}
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{?} Where the March--April melt energy goes. Unconstrained; no satellite
  product measures turbulent flux.
\item \textbf{?} Why F4/G1 \emph{increases} May--June snow mass ($+5.58$, $+5.68$\,mm,
  both significant). Sublimation was the suspect and is \emph{not} implicated in Mar--Apr;
  May snowfall rises significantly instead, for reasons unknown.
\item \textbf{?} Whether the June albedo bias reaches T2m at all (\S\ref{sub:weaklink}).
\item \textbf{?} Why G4 \emph{repairs} G1's Nordic Seas SW RMSE damage
  ($+0.300 \to +0.089$). Adding tundra resistance should not act on a Nordic ocean box.
  An unexplained improvement is as much a loose end as an unexplained degradation.
\item \textbf{?} The Southern Ocean cloud-\emph{area} deficit ($\sim6$\,pp), untouched by
  every lever in all 34 evaluated runs.
\item \textbf{?} Tropics net TOA $2.4$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ too low.
\item \textbf{\ding{51}\,but unaddressed} Global net TOA $+0.52$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ against a
  $\sim0$ target --- \emph{one of the two original problems}, barely moved by the whole
  campaign (control $+0.64$). For a PI spin-up this drift matters more than the last
  tenths of Siberian warming.
\end{enumerate}

\section{State of the campaign (2026--08--10)}\label{sec:state}
A snapshot, not a story. Numbers are 44-year means over 1872--1915 unless stated.

\subsection{Proven}\label{sub:proven}
\textbf{Added 2026--08--10 (round 26, \S\ref{sec:r26}).}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{Marine biogenic CCN is a real Southern Ocean lever, and the best one found.}
At $S=166$ it moves SO SW CRE $-7.25$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ in DJF against a $\pm3.82$ threshold ---
$67\,\%$ of the $+10.86$ DJF deficit, in the season the deficit is worst, from the bloom
rather than from a tuned constant.
\item \textbf{It cannot be adopted as a tuning knob at any published coefficient.} The
tropical cost is $0.0129$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ per unit $S$, so the $\pm0.5$ tolerance binds at
$S\approx39$, below Woodhouse et al.'s floor of 43; and Twomey's $\Delta\ln N_d$ scaling
makes the bound conservative. Scaling $S$ cannot separate the two bands because tropical
background CCN is \emph{lower} than the SO's.
\item \textbf{Detection thresholds for a 1-year pair, per band}: SO SW CRE annual $\pm1.97$,
SO DJF $\pm3.82$, tropics annual $\pm0.99$, tropics DJF $\pm1.22$, global $\pm1.11$. Two of
the three bracket arms fall below their own thresholds and carry no information.
\item \textbf{The tropical longwave deficit gates the Southern Ocean fix.} The tropical cost
of the DMS lever is shortwave; the tropical bias is longwave. They are independent terms, so
closing the LW deficit makes the SO lever affordable rather than competing with it --- and
the two are complementary in the global budget ($-1.72$ against ${\approx}+1$).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Added 2026--08--09 (round 25, \S\ref{sec:r25}).}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The tropopause is not displaced.} Model and ERA5 put the WMO lapse-rate
tropopause on the same level in all five bands. The 200/100\,hPa sign reversal is a
\emph{sharper} transition, not a moved one --- the model cools $+1.8$\,K\,km$^{-1}$ faster
than ERA5 through 300--250 at the poles and $-1.5$ to $-1.9$ slower above 200.
\item \textbf{Cold and dry share one entry point.} A $-2.07$\,K tropical cold point predicts
$-28.5\,\%$ of entry-air drying by Clausius--Clapeyron against a measured $-46.9\,\%$ at
100\,hPa, i.e.\ \textbf{61\,\%}. They are not the independent compensating pair
\S\ref{sub:utls} recorded, so a lever that warms the tropical cold point should move both
the right way (\S\ref{sub:r25onecause}).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Added 2026--08--09 (round 24, \S\ref{sec:r24}).}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{Southern Ocean droplet number is correct in the mean} --- $N_d\approx73$\,cm$^{-3}$
against an observed $60$--$120$, evaluated on 6-hourly wind. The marine CCN path is
mapped end to end and \texttt{RCCNSEA} is inert because \texttt{LCCNO}$=$\texttt{.TRUE.}
\item \textbf{But the seasonal phase is inverted}: model $N_d$ is lowest in DJF ($68.5$)
and highest in JJA ($76.1$), because it is wind-driven, where observations peak in DJF from
biogenic sulphate. The measured SO CRE error is worst in exactly those months.
\item \textbf{The cloud-lifetime CCN route would divide by zero} at \texttt{NAERCLD}$=0$,
so the amount two-thirds is unreachable from the CCN path (\S\ref{sub:r24amount}).
\item \textbf{A DMS emission climatology already ships at TCO95 and nothing reads it} ---
\texttt{emdms}, paramId 210043, on the model's own N96 grid, with \texttt{ICMCL} having
headroom for an eighth field (\S\ref{sub:r24emdms}).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Added 2026--08--09 (round 23, \S\ref{sec:r23}).}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The Southern Ocean amount deficit is confirmed against the right reference.}
CERES and MODIS put SO cloud area at $89.5$ and $89.3\,\%$ against the model's $83.1$; ERA5
reads $81.6$ but reflects $6.31$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ less than CERES against the model's $7.45$, i.e.\
it has low area \emph{and} low reflection in proportion. \textbf{ERA5 reproduces the model's
bias because ERA5 is IFS}, so it is not an independent cloud reference (\S\ref{sub:r23ref}).
\item \textbf{The Southern Ocean and the tropics fail through different cloud types.} SO loses
energy via SW cloud ($+7.45$); the tropics via LW cloud ($-2.16$). No global microphysics knob
can serve both, which is why \texttt{RVICE} trades one for the other (\S\ref{sub:r23attr}).
\item \textbf{Nothing in 50 runs warms the tropics through the longwave}, the term that is
actually deficient there. The only tropics-warming levers on record act in the shortwave.
\item \textbf{CERES cloud area is valid over dark ocean, not over ice} --- CERES and MODIS
disagree by $7.7$\,pp at 90S--65S.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Added 2026--08--09 (round 22, \S\ref{sec:r22}).}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The period-clean radiative error is $+1.23$\,W\,m$^{-2}$, about twice what
the PI framing reports.} \texttt{amip\_presentday} reads net TOA $+2.20$ against CERES's
$+0.97$ (\S\ref{sub:periodclean}).
\item \textbf{The tropical TOA deficit is mostly the reference period} --- $-2.51$
PI-vs-CERES against $-0.67$ period-clean. The Southern Ocean is the opposite: robust to
the epoch and \emph{larger} period-clean, $+7.46$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ and $+0.735$ of the global
mean, now unambiguously the largest single regional term.
\item \textbf{The SO deficit is cloud AMOUNT, $65.5\,\%$ of it}, zonally uniform to
$1.42$\,pp and worst in austral spring and summer --- and \textbf{no lever in the campaign
moves cloud area}: D2a $+0.63$\,pp and D2b $+0.43$\,pp of a $6.43$\,pp gap, against
$+0.48$ and $+0.45$\,pp from two boreal-\emph{land} levers that cannot physically act
there (\S\ref{sub:socloudarea}).
\item \textbf{The global cold bias is extratropical and lives at the tropopause}, peaking
at 200\,hPa over both poles ($-4.20$, $-4.45$) and reversing sign at 100\,hPa, with the
tropics the \emph{least}-biased band. The convective-heating hypothesis is excluded
(\S\ref{sub:utls}).
\item \textbf{The atmosphere is cold AND dry, and the two cancel in OLR.} $-1.62$\,K and
$-10.1\,\%$ over 700--300\,hPa; a Planck response to the cold alone predicts
$-6.10$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ of OLR against $-2.53$ measured. Fixing either alone moves net TOA
several W\,m$^{-2}$ the wrong way.
\item \textbf{The \texttt{tanh} falsification reproduces coupled on a controlled pair}:
11B minus 11A is $-18.99$\,K of Siberian DJF soil (\S\ref{sub:r11coupled}).
\item \textbf{The coupled branch already carries D2b}, not just G4 --- 11A/11B/11D all set
\texttt{RCL\_INPSEA} with the 700\,hPa gate. K1 and P5 are what remain missing.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Added 2026--08--07.}
\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The \texttt{tanh} depletion is falsified on observations, not on preference.}
Its range is open at 1, so complete snow cover is unrepresentable at any parameters, while
Russian snow courses report exactly $10/10$ in $99.74$\,\% of 14\,369 Siberian DJF surveys.
Cost: $-20.2$\,K against 174\,676 station soil observations where the scheme \emph{off} is
right to $+0.8$\,K (\S\ref{sub:r20rihmi}).
\item \textbf{The damage was distributional, not a matter of mean cover.} DJF cover is
identical to $0.002$ across N1/N2/O1/O2 while the soil differs by 23\,K; the clipping ramp
leaves $93.5$\,\% of the box perfectly insulated, the \texttt{tanh} smears the same deficit
into every cell (\S\ref{sub:r20mech}).
\item \textbf{P3 is adopted} --- both falsifiers passed, and it beats the scheme-off
baseline on bias, RMSE and JJA against 105 RIHMI stations (\S\ref{sec:r20res}).
\item \textbf{Aerosol is ${\sim}2$\,\% of the Southern Ocean clear-sky excess, not 13\,\%},
measured rather than inferred, and the two reflection columns are not separable
(\S\ref{sec:r18res}).
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{K1 is the carried-forward configuration (adopted 2026--08--05)} $=$ G4 $+$ the
  snow-free land albedo correction. Siberian JJA $+1.036$\,K ($t{=}8.29$), global T2m RMSE
  $1.579\to\mathbf{1.524}$ (the campaign's best bar K2), every season inside its own
  threshold, and no Arctic energy penalty --- 60--90N net TOA $-97.463$ against a control
  $-97.737$ and CERES $-97.98$ (\S\ref{sub:r17k}). Namelist-only on top of G4.
\item \textbf{Land surface albedo is CLOSED as an explanation for the universal bias.}
  Removing $74\,\%$ of a directly measured bias bought $0.089$\,K of ${\sim}0.7$\,K; a perfect
  fix buys ${\sim}0.12$\,K, one sixth of the target (\S\ref{sub:r17budget}).
\item \textbf{G4 is the base K1 is built on}: Siberian JJA T2m $+0.952$\,K
  ($t{=}7.68$). \emph{Closure depends on a denominator this report does not pin down}:
  against the $\approx2.0$\,K working figure it is $48\,\%$, but \S\ref{sub:perdirect} gives an
  honest range of $-1.3$ to $-2.0$\,K and \S\ref{sub:r15compensate} measures $-2.58$\,K against
  ERA5, so the defensible span is \textbf{37--73\,\%}, SO SW RMSE $6.88\to4.80$, subpolar N Atl $5.01\to4.74$,
  tropics untouched ($+0.124$), every season inside its own threshold. Namelist-only
  (\S\ref{sub:nmltune}).
\item \textbf{The boreal budget is nearly spent.} Siberia's JJA $-2.58$\,K is
  ${\sim}0.7$ universal (present over prescribed-SST ocean too), ${\sim}0.3$ orographic and
  $1.3$--$1.6$ boreal-specific; G4 has taken ${\sim}0.95$ of the last (\S\ref{sub:tilebias}).
  The remainder is not reachable by a boreal-indexed lever, and this --- not a parameter
  limit --- is why F5 $=$ F4.
\item \textbf{The boreal forest fails by TWO routes, not one} (\S\ref{sub:tworoutes}).
  BNE/BINE are \emph{gate-excluded} --- $0.7\,\%$ of NE-Siberian cells clear
  \texttt{tcmin\_est}$=-30$ and their LAI is exactly $0.000$. BNS (larch) has \emph{no} cold
  gate, clears its GDD gate on $84\,\%$ of cells, and is \emph{outcompeted}: grass carries
  $5.8\times$ its LAI. Since NE Siberia is ${\sim}80\,\%$ larch, the winter-gate mechanism
  does not explain the region that matters most.
\item \textbf{The near-surface cold is global, and the soil is not the cause.} All 20 surface
  types are cold in every season, and over low-lying land the soil and skin are the
  \emph{least}-biased parts of the column (\S\ref{sub:soilcol}, \S\ref{sub:tilebias}).
\item \textbf{The boreal cold bias had two routes.} Route~A (over-transpiration $\to$ low
  cloud $\to$ SW loss) is \textbf{closed}: the Siberian JJA cloud term went $-7.04 \to +1.20$\,W\,m$^{-2}$.
  Route~B (late melt $\to$ June albedo) is \textbf{diagnosed and fixable} but carries almost no
  temperature leverage (\S\ref{sub:r15notemp}).
\item \textbf{The snow field can be made correct, but not at this price}: melt-out 12~May
  against a satellite 11~May (\S\ref{sub:r15works}) --- and the scheme that does it costs
  $-16.2$\,K of coupled DJF soil for $+1.2$\,K of summer (\S\ref{sub:scfcoupled}).
  \textbf{Rejected}; round 19 tests a re-parameterisation (\S\ref{sub:r19}).
\item \textbf{The winter damage is seeded in AUTUMN, not winter.} Midwinter snow cover is
  identical between the schemes ($\Delta$\texttt{ZCVS} $=-0.0004$ in January); the deficit
  peaks in October ($-0.075$) exactly as the soil begins to diverge, then vanishes while the
  soil runs to $-24$\,K (\S\ref{sub:zcvsrecon}).
\item \textbf{The control soil is right, verified against direct observations.} RIHMI-WDC
  station soil temperature under natural cover puts the control within ${\sim}1$\,K at every
  depth the Russian network reports in winter, and its thermal offset within $0.7$--$2.3$\,K
  of the observed $+17.8$ to $+24.2$\,K (\S\ref{sub:rihmi}). The campaign no longer depends on
  ERA5 --- an HTESSEL sibling --- for any soil claim.
\item \textbf{I3 is the strongest summer lever built}: Siberian JJA $+1.322$\,K ($t{=}10.61$),
  and it closes the Siberian surface SW deficit to $166.58$ against CERES $166.26$ --- the
  deficit \S\ref{sec:albedo} opened the albedo thread with. It is \textbf{not adopted}: it
  buys that with $+1.72$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ at 60--90N, the largest NH$-$SH albedo shift of any run,
  and the campaign's only significant Nordic Seas RMSE degradation (\S\ref{sub:i3}).
\item \textbf{The Siberian winter penalty is not radiative.} DJF snow albedo, SWE and absorbed
  SW are identical across control/G4/I1/I2/I3 to three decimals, on an $8$\,W\,m$^{-2}$
  midwinter budget that cannot build $-2.4$\,K (\S\ref{sub:djfnotrad}). The route is thermal.
\item \textbf{Half the land albedo excess is the snow tile.} Of $+0.0154$ against CERES,
  $+0.0074$ is snow and $+0.0080$ the snow-free surface; every forest type is inside $+0.006$
  once snow is masked per cell per month (\S\ref{sub:snowtile}).
\item \textbf{\texttt{RVRSMIN} does not saturate}; $1000$ is a cap set by winter damage, not a
  knee (\S\ref{sub:r14res}).
\item \textbf{The energy target is Antarctic/Southern Ocean}, and the two campaign targets
  share that root cause (\S\ref{sub:sobands}, \S\ref{sub:toadecomp2}).
\item \textbf{Tuning is namelist-driven and mergeable with EC-Earth} --- one added line in an
  ECMWF file (\S\ref{sub:nmltune}).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Open, in priority order}\label{sub:open}
\textbf{Re-ranked 2026--08--10 after round 26 (\S\ref{sec:r26}).} The Southern Ocean lever
now exists and is priced; what blocks it is the tropics. The list collapses accordingly:
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The tropical longwave cloud deficit}, $-2.16$\,W\,m$^{-2}$. Promoted to first
because it is no longer a parallel item: it \emph{gates} the Southern Ocean fix
(\S\ref{sub:r26gate}). The lever must be keyed on \textbf{deep convection}, not on cloud ice
in general --- \texttt{RVICE} is the counter-example. \emph{Do the free check first}: no arm
of ${\sim}52$ has ever been scored on this term, and \texttt{tropical\_lw\_scan.py} is
written to answer it from data already on disk.
\item \textbf{11E and 11F coupled}, both submitted 2026--08--10 and queued. 11E decides
whether the coupled configuration is level with AMIP and gates the PI spin-up; 11F asks
whether free SST and sea ice change the Southern Ocean answer, which AMIP structurally
cannot price.
\item \textbf{The tropical cold point}, $-2.07$\,K, the shared entry point for the cold bias
and $61\,\%$ of the stratospheric dry bias (\S\ref{sec:r25}).
\item \textbf{S4} (\texttt{RCL\_INPPMIN} $70000\to50000$) completed 48 years on
2026--08--09 and is \textbf{unevaluated}.
\item \textbf{The SO cloud-\emph{amount} two-thirds} stays open with no identified lever; the
CCN path reaches the opacity third only.
\item \textbf{Dropped:} the Blomquist et al.\ (2017) transfer velocity. It is less steep in
wind than the Nightingale quadratic, so it moves flux away from the windy Southern Ocean
toward the calm tropics --- worse selectivity, not better (\S\ref{sub:r26gate}).
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Re-ranked after round 24 (\S\ref{sec:r24}), which answers round 23's question.}
Round 23 asked for a \emph{regionally selective} Southern Ocean reflection lever. Round 24
found one and priced it: marine CCN is selective by construction, but it reaches only the
\textbf{opacity third} --- $2.57$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ in-band, ${\approx}0.25$ global --- and the
amount two-thirds is unreachable from the CCN path because the lifetime route divides by
zero at \texttt{NAERCLD}$=0$. The list is therefore:
\begin{enumerate}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{11E coupled} (11D at $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=15$ plus K1). A namelist edit on a lever
already validated over 44 AMIP years, and the only route to three questions AMIP cannot
price. Cheapest decisive run available.
\item \textbf{The tropical longwave cloud deficit}, $-2.16$\,W\,m$^{-2}$, untouched by any
lever in 50 runs and the one term that is deficient in the direction the tropics need.
\item \textbf{The tropical cold point}, $-2.07$\,K, which \S\ref{sec:r25} shows is the
shared entry point for the cold bias and $61\,\%$ of the stratospheric dry bias. The
tropopause \emph{height} is correct, so this is a cold-point temperature problem, not a
displacement one, and one lever should move both errors.
\item \textbf{Marine biogenic CCN via \texttt{emdms}} --- real missing physics with the input
already on disk, but ${\approx}0.25$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ global and it needs a published
flux$\to$CCN relation, so it follows the cheaper items.
\item \textbf{The SO cloud-\emph{amount} two-thirds} stays open with no identified lever.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Re-ranked again after round 23 (\S\ref{sec:r23}).} The Southern Ocean stays first
but the requirement has sharpened: it needs a \emph{regime-selective} reflection lever, because
every global low-cloud knob overshoots a tropical band that is only $-0.67$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ from
CERES. Second is the tropical \emph{longwave} cloud deficit, untouched by any lever. Third is
the extratropical tropopause. Fourth is 11E coupled.

\textbf{Re-ranked 2026--08--09 after round 22 (\S\ref{sec:r22}).} The top of this list is
now: (i) \textbf{the Southern Ocean cloud-\emph{amount} deficit}, $65.5\,\%$ of a
$+7.46$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ band error, untouched by every lever and \emph{not} reachable by the
INP branch (\S\ref{sub:socloudarea}); (ii) \textbf{the extratropical tropopause cold-and-dry
pair}, which is the dominant land-T2m term and simultaneously the reason the global energy
budget cancels (\S\ref{sub:utls}); (iii) \textbf{11D re-run at $\mathrm{SWEMIN}=15$ plus
K1}, the first coupled configuration level with the AMIP one.

Two entries below are \textbf{superseded} and struck rather than deleted:
\dead{The snow-free land albedo residual $+0.0080$ is untouched by all 41 runs.} K1 is
exactly that lever and removed $74\,\%$ of it for $+0.089$\,K; the entry predates K1's
adoption and survived unedited (\S\ref{sub:r17budget}, which also retracted K3 before it
ran). \dead{Tropics net TOA $2.4$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ too low.} Period-clean it is $0.67$
(\S\ref{sub:periodclean}).

\textbf{Added 2026--08--07.} (i) \textbf{The autumn cover excess P3 introduced} ---
$+0.259$ in September against Rutgers, where as-released was $+0.107$. P5/P6 test
\texttt{SWEMIN} $=15$ and $30$; the offline optimum is 30 (\S\ref{sub:r20rutgers}).
(ii) \textbf{The Southern Ocean unexplained residual has grown to ${\sim}1.9$\,W\,m$^{-2}$}
now that the aerosol term is measured (\S\ref{sec:r18res}). (iii) \textbf{Scale awareness
is a two-anchor interpolation, not a law} --- a new resolution needs validation
(\S\ref{sub:r21}). (iv) The M1 clear-sky sign anomaly is significant and untraced.

\begin{enumerate}\itemsep2pt
\item \textbf{The blocker: a $-4.7$\,K Siberian winter bias} accompanying the snow fix
  (\S\ref{sub:r15compensate}). Not a radiation-supply problem (\S\ref{sub:r15lw}) and now
  positively excluded as radiative at all (\S\ref{sub:djfnotrad}), so the route is thermal.
  Round 16 tests skin conductivity; if that fails, boundary-layer mixing. \emph{J1/J2 are
  built on I1, not I3} --- diagnostic either way, but the production pair needs re-running on
  the better base (\S\ref{sec:lamsk}).
\item \textbf{Decide the coupled base: I3 or I1.} I3 gives $+0.072$\,K more JJA and a better
  global T2m RMSE, but carries the Arctic energy bill of \S\ref{sub:i3}, which \textbf{AMIP
  structurally cannot price} --- prescribed SST and ice absorb $1.7$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ without
  responding, and the coupled spin-up will not. Open question: whether a recalibrated mode~2
  keeps the $+1.3$\,K without the 60--90N surplus, since the SDOR scaling does most of its
  work outside the box it was tuned in (\S\ref{sub:snowhalflever}).
\item \textbf{The snow-free land albedo residual $+0.0080$} --- crops $+0.0204$, semidesert
  $+0.0169$, bare soil $+0.0145$, tundra $+0.0105$ --- is untouched by all 41 runs and is the
  one lever aimed at the ${\sim}0.7$\,K \emph{universal} bias rather than the boreal one. It
  \textbf{must not simply be stacked on I3} (\S\ref{sub:stacking}): the mid-latitude entries
  are safe, the tundra entry competes for the same Arctic energy budget.
\item \textbf{Global net TOA $+0.52$\,W\,m$^{-2}$} against a PI target of $\sim0$ --- one of the
  two original faults, barely moved by the whole campaign. Note this sits oddly beside a cold
  troposphere: in AMIP the prescribed SST absorbs the imbalance without warming, so the two are
  not contradictory, but the pairing is unexplained.
\item \textbf{The global tropospheric cold bias} of \S\ref{sub:vprof} ($-0.9$\,K at 700--850\,hPa,
  $-2.8$ to $-3.0$\,K at 200\,hPa) is now the dominant remaining term for land 2\,m temperature
  and has never been worked on. It is out of reach of every lever this campaign has built.
\item \textbf{SO cloud-area deficit ${\sim}6$\,pp}, untouched by all 34 evaluated levers, and now
  known to drive the energy target too.
\item \textbf{$-12$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ downward-LW deficit in Oct--Nov}, unexplained.
\item Tropics net TOA $2.4$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ too low; Nordic Seas SW RMSE moved by G1 and partly
  repaired by G4, both unexplained.
\item Whether $48\,\%$ of the boreal bias suffices for forest survival --- \textbf{AMIP
  structurally cannot answer this}; it needs the coupled tests.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Falsified claims, kept}\label{sec:falsified}
Every mechanism this campaign has killed, with the measurement that killed it. Retained
because the history has to stay honest, and because three of them were killed only after
being built on.

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{p{0.30\textwidth} p{0.42\textwidth} l}
\toprule
claim & what killed it & where \\
\midrule
\dead{The SO cloud deficit should be attacked by pushing the INP branch harder, since D2b
is the only lever that has ever moved the Southern Ocean} &
Scored on cloud \emph{area} rather than CRE: D2a $+0.63$\,pp and D2b $+0.43$\,pp of a
$6.43$\,pp gap, against $+0.48$ and $+0.45$\,pp from G4 and K1 --- boreal-land levers that
cannot physically act there. The INP area response is at the diagnostic's noise level; it
works on opacity, which is $34.5\,\%$ of the error. & \S\ref{sub:socloudarea} \\
\dead{Tropics net TOA $2.4$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ too low} &
Measured on a PI arm against present-day CERES. Period-clean, on \texttt{amip\_presentday},
the band is $-0.67$ --- most of it was reference period, not model error. &
\S\ref{sub:periodclean} \\
\dead{The snow-free land albedo residual $+0.0080$ is untouched by all 41 runs} &
K1 \emph{is} that lever (\texttt{RVVEGALB} on crops/irrigated/semidesert $+$
\texttt{ECE\_TUNE\_SOILALB} $0.95$) and removed $74\,\%$ of it. The open-list entry
predates K1's adoption and was never updated; acting on it would have re-bought the
remaining $26\,\%$, worth ${\sim}0.03$\,K. & \S\ref{sub:r17budget} \\
\dead{The boreal cold bias above 700\,hPa might be a tropical convective-heating error
propagating globally} &
The tropics are the \emph{least}-biased band at $-1.72$\,K against an extratropical mean of
$-2.97$. The bias is extratropical, peaks at 200\,hPa over both poles and reverses at
100\,hPa. & \S\ref{sub:utls} \\
\dead{Marine CCN is a uniform $125$\,cm$^{-3}$; the aerosol--cloud path is off} &
Read from a default in \texttt{sucldp.F90}. \texttt{fort.4} carries
\texttt{LMACV2SP\_CCNF}$=$\texttt{.true.}, \texttt{PCCN} is a field, and \S\ref{sec:r18res}
already documented that path. & \S\ref{sub:r24path} \\
\dead{Southern Ocean $N_d$ is too low, so raising marine CCN is the opacity lever} &
$N_d=55$ came from applying Genthon to \emph{monthly-mean} wind, which cancels the
transient eddies. On 6-hourly wind it is $73.5$, inside the observed $60$--$120$. A
nonlinear operator applied to a mean --- the round-19 error again. & \S\ref{sub:r24nd} \\
\dead{Prognostic marine biogenic CCN needs \texttt{-cc} with REcoM} &
REcoM has no DMS tracer; the only DMS is Icepack's sea-ice biogeochemistry, in a component
this setup does not run. The prescribed \texttt{emdms} climatology needs none of it.
& \S\ref{sub:r24emdms} \\
\dead{\texttt{RCL\_OVERLAPLIQICE} $0.65\to0.35$ is an untested mixed-phase lever} &
It is A1b, already run for 44 years; A1a is the same knob at $0.1$. The A-series ``ovl'' label
means the liquid/ice \emph{deposition} overlap, not radiative cloud overlap. Run cancelled.
& \S\ref{sub:r23runs} \\
\dead{Lowering \texttt{RVICE} would warm the tropics and pay for the SO levers} &
Mechanically true, but \texttt{RVICE} was \emph{raised} from $0.13$ to $0.16$ in the v3.4
tuning precisely to suppress SO high cloud (\#169/\#170). It is global; the two regions need
opposite signs. & \S\ref{sub:r23prior} \\
\dead{The SO cloud-area deficit may be a CERES-vs-\texttt{tcc} definition artefact} &
Refuted by the CRE test: ERA5 has low area \emph{and} low reflection in proportion, so it
shares the model's bias rather than measuring a different quantity. & \S\ref{sub:r23ref} \\
\dead{The Arctic carries $+11.49$\,pp too much cloud} &
A passive cloud mask over sea ice, where CERES and MODIS disagree by $7.7$\,pp at 90S--65S.
Not supportable. & \S\ref{sub:r23ref} \\
\dead{The 200/100\,hPa sign reversal is a displaced tropopause} &
The WMO lapse-rate tropopause is on the same level as ERA5 in all five bands. It is a
sharper transition, not a moved one. & \S\ref{sub:r25height} \\
\dead{Cold and dry are independent errors that happen to cancel in OLR} &
A $-2.07$\,K tropical cold point explains $61\,\%$ of the $100$\,hPa drying by
Clausius--Clapeyron. One entry point, not two. & \S\ref{sub:r25onecause} \\
\dead{${\approx}0.5$\,pp is the noise floor of the SO cloud-area diagnostic, set by land
levers that cannot act there} &
G4 and K1 \emph{contain} D2b (G4 $=$ F4$+$D2b$+$tundra), so their $+0.48$/$+0.45$\,pp was
D2b's own signal read back as a null. The measured threshold is $\pm0.098$\,pp and every
lever clears it; the conclusion rests on magnitude --- $10\,\%$ of the gap --- not on
significance. & \S\ref{sub:socloudarea} \\
\dead{Aerosol contributes ${\sim}0.7$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ (13\,\%) of the SO clear-sky excess} &
Inferred from a $+0.033$ AOD excess vs MISR. Measured by removing all anthropogenic
aerosol: $-0.116$\,W\,m$^{-2}$, $t=-18.9$ --- six times smaller. The unexplained residual
grows to ${\sim}1.9$. & \S\ref{sec:r18res} \\
\dead{\texttt{SCALE} is the parameter that controls spring depletion} &
Varying it $1\to4$ moves the offline Sep--May RMSE by $0.002$; P3 and P4 came out
near-identical. \texttt{SWEMIN} is the whole lever and P4 was a wasted run. &
\S\ref{sec:r20res} \\
\dead{The scale dependence of \texttt{SWEMIN} can be measured as a power law} &
The ladder confirms the sign but the values plateau beyond 133\,km, the per-cell fit
disagrees $3\times$ with the seasonal fit, and its own extrapolation to 2.5\,km gives $9.4$
against a measured anchor of $3$. Kept as an interpolation, labelled as such. &
\S\ref{sub:r21} \\
\dead{P1 was stopped by an unexplained \texttt{scancel}, not a model failure} &
The model aborted normally on \texttt{PROC=48}; I searched rank 1 only, for tokens that do
not match \texttt{ABOR1}/\texttt{MPL\_ABORT}, and built an infrastructure story on the gap.
& \S\ref{sub:r20crash} \\
\dead{B8 (\texttt{RVLAMSK}$\to$5) is the best boreal lever, $+0.502$\,K} &
44-year re-evaluation: $-0.038$\,K, pure noise. Two runs had already been built on it. &
\S\ref{sub:noisefloor} \\
\dead{The band contributions show no region carrying an anomalous TOA imbalance} &
Artefact of comparing bands to each other, not to CERES. Differenced against CERES the
Southern Ocean carries most of it. & \S\ref{sub:sobands} \\
\dead{About half the boreal bias is the reference period ($+1.12$\,K)} &
Indirect HadCRUT5 chain; direct measurement gives $+0.42$\,K, wrong by $2.7\times$. &
\S\ref{sub:perdirect} \\
\dead{D1: reformulating the \texttt{RCAPDCYCL} land closure will warm the boreal} &
Measured $-0.170$\,K, inside the $\pm0.242$ floor --- recovery excluded, sign unresolved. & \S\ref{sec:round11res} \\
\dead{Round 13: raising the snow-cover threshold $10\to30$\,cm recovers June's albedo term} &
JJA $+0.020$\,K ($t{=}0.17$). Arithmetically incapable of acting in spring --- it can only
bite where depths are near the threshold, i.e.\ autumn, which is what it did. &
\S\ref{sub:hres}, \S\ref{sub:scffail} \\
\dead{G4's gain comes via the melt-timing route} &
May--June SWE did not move ($+2.75$, $+1.60$, both inside noise) while T2m rose $+0.952$\,K.
The gain is the plain sensible-heat route. & \S\ref{sub:r14res} \\
\dead{\texttt{RVRSMIN} saturates near 1000, so the value sits on a knee} &
$500/1000/2000 \to +0.336/+0.521/+0.876$: increments \emph{accelerate}. No knee. &
\S\ref{sub:r14res} \\
\dead{The model vents its March--April melt energy as sensible heat} &
Compared our scheme against ERA5's, which is unconstrained HTESSEL output --- a sibling of
itself. No observational authority; survives only as a hypothesis. & \S\ref{sub:noobs} \\
\dead{The June albedo bias is worth ${\sim}5.6$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ of boreal warming} &
Fixing it to satellite accuracy gained $+0.094$\,K --- four times less efficient per watt than
the vegetation route. & \S\ref{sub:r15notemp} \\
\dead{The boreal void is a ``no closed canopy'' artefact of \texttt{FORESTFPC}$=0$} &
Diagnostic error in the void metric itself, not a model result; the most-repeated retraction
in this report. & \S\ref{sec:lpjg} \\
\dead{Siberian JJA net SW is $33$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ too low} &
Inferred from CRUNCEP, which is itself ${\approx}21$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ too bright. Against CERES
the deficit is $11$, and later $12.5$ split $7.0$ cloud $+$ $5.5$ albedo. & \S\ref{sec:jja} \\
\dead{\texttt{RVRSMIN} $500$ is the IFS default and was halved in this build} &
Misread of the table; $250$ is as-released for types 3/4. & \S\ref{app:params} \\
\dead{``Competition, not climate'' --- the boreal failure is an LPJG-internal competition error} &
The controlled forcing-transfer test reproduces most of the loss with byte-identical
parameters. \textbf{Narrowed 2026--08--05}: what died is competition as a \emph{parameter
error}. Competition as the \emph{pathway} by which climate removes larch is
\textbf{supported} --- BNS is permitted on $84\,\%$ of NE-Siberian cells and loses to grass
$5.8{:}1$. Forcing is the driver, competition the mechanism. & \S\ref{sub:xfer},
\S\ref{sub:compreconcile} \\
\dead{The missing boreal forest is explained by the winter evergreen cold gate} &
True for BNE/BINE ($0.7\,\%$ of larch-zone cells clear $-30$\,\textdegree C, LAI exactly
$0.000$) and false for the ${\sim}80\,\%$-larch NE Siberia, where BNS has no cold gate and is
outcompeted instead. Two routes, not one. & \S\ref{sub:tworoutes} \\
\dead{The excess snow cover was propping DJF up ${\sim}2.7$\,K, masking a $-4.7$\,K bias} &
Asserted, not measured: winter cover is unchanged between the schemes, and the same signature
was treated as disqualifying for H1. Route unidentified. & \S\ref{sub:r15compensate} \\
\dead{Sublimation explains why F4 increases spring snow} &
March--April evaporation changes are inside the noise floor; May \emph{snowfall} rises
instead, for reasons still unknown. & \S\ref{sub:openlist} \\
\dead{The measured $+0.0154$ land albedo excess contradicts \S\ref{sub:albreg}: Europe
$+0.0099$, Great Plains $+0.0153$, Sahara $+0.0207$, Siberia $+0.0165$, so temperate land is
\emph{not} within $\pm0.004$ and the ``no global fix'' conclusion needs revisiting} &
Those are all-months numbers and snow is not a vegetation type --- it is a tile overlying every
type. Masked per cell per month, every forest type falls inside $+0.006$ and tropical broadleaf
to $-0.0003$. \S\ref{sub:albreg} was right about the vegetation table; the real finding is a
$+0.0080$ residual on \emph{sparse and cultivated} surfaces. & \S\ref{sub:snowtile} \\
\dead{Round 16: raising the exposed-snow skin conductivity will warm Siberian DJF by
$+1$ to $+3$\,K} &
DJF got \emph{worse}, monotonically: $-2.834$ at $\lambda_{sk}{=}15$, $-3.226$ at $25$, against
I1's $-2.759$. Both runs score a global T2m RMSE worse than the control. Skin conductivity is
not the winter route. & \S\ref{sub:r16res} \\
\dead{A moss/organic layer on larch and tundra recovers ${>}2$\,K of boreal summer
(Gaillard 2025)} &
$-0.168$\,K vs G4 at $\lambda_{sk}{=}2.9$ and $-0.314$ at $1.7$ --- more moss is \emph{worse},
and the stronger run is a significant degradation. Falsifies the $\lambda_{sk}$ \emph{proxy};
a real organic layer needs heat capacity and moisture, not just skin coupling. &
\S\ref{sub:r17l} \\
\dead{The land albedo correction is worth ${\sim}1.1$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ and $0.1$--$0.2$\,K of land
warming} &
Measured $0.77$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ and $0.089$\,K. The sensitivity assumption ($0.12$\,K per
W\,m$^{-2}$) was right to $0.116$; the forcing estimate was optimistic. & \S\ref{sub:r17budget} \\
\dead{K3 should push the soil-albedo scale further, since K2 alone gives the best global RMSE} &
Retracted \emph{before} it was run: the residual $26\,\%$ is worth ${\sim}0.03$\,K, and $0.95$
already delivers the measured $-0.0163$ --- going further is tuning against the observation. &
\S\ref{sub:r17budget} \\
\dead{The snow scheme's winter soil damage does not appear in the coupled runs, so it is not
disqualifying for coupling} &
Artefact of sampling the larch zone alone, where 10A is already saturated near
$-21\,^\circ$C so both runs look alike. Across the full station network 10B is $-16.2$\,K
DJF; the AMIP run had already predicted it ($-13.6$\,K). & \S\ref{sub:scfcoupled} \\
\dead{The snow fraction is applied twice on the conduction path, so the pack drains through an
area-scaled conductance a second time} &
\texttt{ZHFLUX}, \texttt{ZDSN} and \texttt{ZSNCONDH} are all per m$^2$ of grid box; there is
no second division to pair with. Proposed and falsified within the hour. & \S\ref{sub:zcvsrecon} \\
\dead{Density cannot separate the autumn and spring regimes at their respective depths} &
Argued from the current parameters, not the formula. $m>1.23$ gives the right ordering and
$m=4$ a factor $5.5$; $m=1.6$ merely separates them by $0.077$. & \S\ref{sub:r19} \\
\dead{I3 is the best lever the campaign has produced} &
Drafted on the Siberian-box scripts alone before the guardrails were run. True for JJA T2m
($+1.322$\,K) and Siberian SW, but I3 carries $+1.72$\,W\,m$^{-2}$ at 60--90N, the largest
NH$-$SH albedo shift of all 37 runs, and the only significant Nordic Seas degradation.
Not adopted. & \S\ref{sub:i3} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\noindent Two further items are \emph{provisional} rather than dead: the March--April mass
deficit rests on ERA5 SWE (forest-biased), and mode~2's scale-awareness carries only
${\sim}16\,\%$ of the length scale over this box (\S\ref{sub:scfcaveat}).

\appendix

\section{Implementation \& lab-notebook detail}\label{app:impl}
Operational detail moved here to keep the work-plan science legible. Retained for reproducibility.

\textbf{A.1 esm-tools fixes for the AMIP--noLPJG generator (\S\ref{sub:amipgen}).} Model tree \texttt{oifsamip-cy48}
git-aligned to \texttt{awiesm3-develop} (oifs \texttt{local\_combined\_fixes}\,@\,1303a90, oasis \texttt{afac6a3},
xios\,\texttt{2.5.3}) + bundle/field patches. Three bugs fixed, committed \& pushed (commit \texttt{9921d95b}, branch
\texttt{development\_interactiv\_mesh\_awiesm3}, and a follow-up for the WAM recipe):
\begin{itemize}\itemsep0pt
\item \texttt{xios.yaml} dereferenced the \texttt{fesom} namespace via \texttt{choose\_fesom.*} and crashed \emph{any}
FESOM-less setup --- now gated on \texttt{"\$((\;'fesom' in config\;))"}.
\item \texttt{oifsamip.yaml} never overrode the OIFS \texttt{comp\_command}, so the binary compiled \emph{without} XIOS
or coupling despite \texttt{LXIOS:TRUE} --- now adds \texttt{--with-cplng2 --with-oifs-xios --build-type=release}
(like awicm3/awiesm3).
\item WAM aborted (\texttt{CDATEA}$\neq$\texttt{CDATEF}) at any start year $\neq$1990 because the OIFS
\texttt{preprocess} step (\texttt{change\_icm\_date.sh}, which re-dates ICMGG/ICMSH/wave init files) never ran:
\texttt{oifsamip.yaml} lacked \texttt{prepcompute\_recipe:\;\$\{oifs.prepcompute\_recipe\}} and fell back to the
default recipe with no preprocess step. Added (matches \texttt{awicm3.yaml}).
\end{itemize}
Runscript \texttt{oifsamip-cy48-levante-TCO95L91\_lpjgforcing.yaml} overrides XIOS output to
\texttt{file\_def\_lpjg\_spinup} + \texttt{field\_def\_lpjg\_safe}, emitting daily
\texttt{tas,tasmin,tasmax,pr,rsns,rlns,sfcWind} + \texttt{tdps,ps,psl,clt,prsn,evspsbl,prw,sd,rsn}.
\emph{esm\_master gotcha:} the single-component target is \texttt{recomp-<setup>/oifs-48r1} (the \emph{component}
name); the alias \texttt{/oifs} silently falls back to the setup and doubles the path
(\texttt{oifsamip-cy48/oifsamip-cy48}).

\textbf{A.2 Flux-accumulation fix (\S\ref{sub:amipgen}), at root.} The de-accumulation divisor in
\texttt{field\_def\_lpjg\_safe.xml} was the hard-coded \texttt{/21600} (6\,h). It is now templated:
\texttt{field\_def\_lpjg\_safe.xml.j2} uses \texttt{/\{\{oifs.flux\_accumulation\_period\}\}}, with
\texttt{oifs.flux\_accumulation\_period} defaulting to \texttt{\$\{oifs.time\_step\}} ($=$\,NFRHIS$\times$TSTEP; with
NFRHIS$=$1 this is the timestep). A validated $\times6$ scalar patch
(\texttt{AMIP\_noLPJG\_1d\_1870-1879\_TCO95\_PI\_fluxfix.nc}) corrected the existing forcing without a rerun.

\textbf{Controlled temperature test now ready.} Because the AMIP--CRUNCEP comparison shows meaningful
forcing sensitivity but the CERES replacement rules out net radiation as its dominant source, two additional AMIP
forcing cycles were created for the next attribution step. They add exactly $+1$ or $+2$\,K to
\texttt{tas}, \texttt{tasmin} and \texttt{tasmax} at all TCO95 cells north of or at 45\textdegree N; every field
south of 45\textdegree N and every non-temperature variable is unchanged. Their NH-land forcing-cycle means are
$-3.57$ and $-2.57$\,\textdegree C, compared with $-4.57$\,\textdegree C in the source AMIP file. These are
\emph{prepared inputs, not spin-up results}. Running them with unchanged LPJG parameters will quantify whether a
uniform boreal temperature increment crosses a vegetation threshold or merely produces a modest continuous response.

\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.97\textwidth]{plots/forcing_amip_temperature_nh_10yrmean_original_plus1K_plus2K.png}
\caption{\textbf{Input check for the planned AMIP temperature sensitivity.} Ten-year NH-land mean near-surface
temperature in the original, $+1$\,K and $+2$\,K forcing cycles. The increments were applied consistently to
\texttt{tas}, \texttt{tasmin} and \texttt{tasmax} at latitude $\ge45$\textdegree N; no vegetation response is
shown because these two spin-ups have not yet been run.}
\label{fig:amipwarmingforcing}
\end{figure}

\textbf{A.3 CERES forcing construction and validation (\S\ref{sec:ceresforcing}).}
The direct source is
\path{/work/ab0246/a270092/obs/CERES/CERES_EBAF_Ed4.1_Subset_200003-202106.nc};
the climatological source is
\path{/work/ab0246/a270092/obs/CERES/CERES_EBAF_Ed4.1_Subset_CLIM01-CLIM12.nc}.
The output files are
\path{/work/ab0995/a270270/input/cruncep_v7/CRUNCEP_noLPJG_1d_1901-1910_TCO95_CERES_rsns_rlns.nc}
and
\path{/work/ab0995/a270270/input/cruncep_v7/CRUNCEP_noLPJG_1d_1901-1910_TCO95_CERESclim_daily_variability.nc}.
Both contain 3652 daily timesteps and 40\,320 TCO95 cells. Coordinates and all non-radiation fields are unchanged
from CRUNCEP v3. The direct file has \texttt{rsns} range 0--358.23 and \texttt{rlns} range
$-197.30$--34.76\,W\,m$^{-2}$. The daily-variability file has ranges 0--455.61 and
$-206.92$--93.85\,W\,m$^{-2}$; every mapped CERES cell-month target is reproduced within
$8\times10^{-6}$\,W\,m$^{-2}$. Construction and preanalysis scripts, cached intermediates and the detailed
provenance document are retained under
\path{/work/ab0995/a270270/analysis/Offline_LPJ_GUESS_analysis/}.

\textbf{A.4 Phase-1B OpenIFS source edits (\S\ref{sub:phase1b}).} Paths under
\texttt{oifs-48r1/ifs-source/arpifs/phys\_ec/}:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{l l l l l}
\toprule
knob & file:line & current & proposed & effect \\
\midrule
\texttt{ZLMIN} & \texttt{vdfexcu.F90}:201 & $30$\,m & $40$--$50$\,m & min stable mixing length \\
\texttt{ZCB}   & \texttt{vdfexcu.F90}:190 & $5.0$ & $3.0$--$4.0$ & LTG $b$: lower $\Rightarrow$ longer tail $\Rightarrow$ more $K$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Secondary companions if needed: the $0.1$ / $300$\,m stable mixing-length limits (\texttt{vdfexcu.F90}:374,376) and the
surface-layer constants \texttt{RCHETA}/\texttt{RCHETB}/\texttt{RCHBHDL} (\texttt{surf/module/suvexcs\_mod.F90}, mirror
in \texttt{suvdfs.F90}).
